# GUI Improvement Log

A running log of improvement instructions for the graphical interfaces to
this toolchain. Two purposes:

1. Track what has been asked for and where it stands in the **macOS app**
   (`mac-app/`).
2. Serve as the **specification feed for the Windows app** (`windows-app/`,
   built and shipping since 2026-08-11) — every entry here describes
   behaviour the Windows app must also have, unless marked macOS-only, and
   keeping the two in step is what this feed is now for.

**This log is HISTORY, not a specification.** A row records what was true on
the day it was written and is never edited when the behaviour changes again —
so a sentence quoted in row 183 may have been replaced by row 184, and both
rows are correct about their own day. What is true NOW lives in
[`contracts/`](contracts/README.md): `assist-wording.json` for what the
assistant says, `assist-cases.json` for what must happen. Write tests against
those; read this for why things are the way they are. See "Where the truth
lives" in [`CLAUDE.md`](CLAUDE.md).

**The Windows column is required.** An entry is not finished until "Notes
for Windows port" says something a Windows implementer can use: what to do
differently, what is inherited unchanged, or the trap that would pass review.
"Shared Python, nothing to mirror" is a fine note when it is true; an empty
cell never is. And when a change makes existing Windows guidance wrong,
correcting it — here or in [`WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md`](WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md) — is
part of the change, because stale guidance is followed.

Baseline: both GUIs are thin wrappers. They collect input and present
output; all real work happens in the command-line toolchain (scripts on
macOS; the WSL2-backed equivalents on Windows). See
[`documentation/09-mac-app.md`](documentation/09-mac-app.md) for the design
principle and [`mac-app/README.md`](mac-app/README.md) for architecture.

| 24 | 2026-08-09 | The preview layout glitch persisted: starting a preview after one had already been shown pulled the progress header up under the window's toolbar. | ⚠️ Structural fix applied, needs visual confirmation — the detail area is now a ZStack whose base layer (progress/console) stays mounted and laid out in the normal safe-area-respecting flow, with the site preview overlaying it. Previously the two swapped places in one container, so the console inherited the departing full-bleed web view's geometry. Reproduced first with a stub launcher that really serves a page (the web view must have appeared for the glitch to occur). NOTE: XCUITest reports frames for this SwiftUI content that place it outside its own window, so geometry assertions proved untrustworthy; the regression test now checks behaviour (restarting a preview returns to the progress view) and the visual result needs a human eye. | Keep long-lived content mounted and overlay transient full-bleed content, rather than swapping siblings in one container. |

| 25 | 2026-08-09 | Deploy could publish without a preview ever being built. It should check whether a build exists that is newer than the course content: if so publish; if not, build first (silently, without showing a preview) and then publish. | ✅ Implemented — `BuildFreshness` compares the built site's date against the newest change anywhere in the course's content (hidden entries skipped, which excludes the generated output, Netlify markers, and Obsidian settings). Deploy runs `preview.sh --build-only` first when needed, then publishes, presented as ONE task with a combined six-step progress bar; a failed build stops before publishing. | Same rule and the same combined progress presentation; skip hidden/generated paths in the freshness scan. |

| 26 | 2026-08-09 | The whole UI went blank during the "publishing to Netlify" step. | ✅ Fixed — a main-thread stall, not a layout or crash problem (no crash report; the window survived but stopped drawing). The progress header refreshes every second and each refresh rebuilt the ENTIRE transcript into one string several times over, then scanned it for six markers — fine early on, ruinous once a publish had produced tens of thousands of lines. Output now has a single entry point that folds new text into the milestone count as it ARRIVES; the joined transcript is cached and capped at 4,000 lines; the fallback phase scan reads only the tail. Measured: five refreshes over a 5,000-line transcript now take microseconds, guarded by a performance test. | Same rule: never re-scan accumulated output on a UI timer — track progress incrementally as output arrives, cache any joined text, and cap what is retained. |

| 27 | 2026-08-09 | The whole interface went blank while publishing (round two — the earlier performance work did not fix it). | ⚠️ Partial (one real contributor) — the console's `maxHeight: .infinity` resolved to its IDEAL height whenever the height proposal was unbounded, and a scroll view's ideal height is its entire content. With a long publish transcript the view claimed 48,231 points (measured in a unit test); the window's SwiftUI content grew to 1940 points inside a 720-point window and slid 584 points out of view — a fully responsive app drawing a blank window. Fixed by declaring a small `idealHeight` alongside the min/max, so the console still fills available space but never claims content height. Guarded by a test that measures the view's claimed height with a 3,000-line transcript, both collapsed and expanded. | Any scrollable output pane must declare a bounded ideal size, not just a max — otherwise an unbounded proposal makes it claim its whole content. |

| 28 | 2026-08-09 | Blank interface while publishing — root cause identified by making the app name the oversized views. | ✅ Fixed at the root — the app's own logging showed the NavigationSplitView's AppKit scaffolding (NSSplitView, both item wrappers, the sidebar's glass views) sized 762.5, then 746.5, then 1940.5 in a 720-point window: the whole SwiftUI root was being sized by content ideal height rather than clamped to the window, so ANY overgrown descendant dragged the entire interface — sidebar included — off-screen. The root now declares a bounded ideal size alongside its minimum and a `.infinity` maximum, and the progress header's `fixedSize(vertical:)` (an ideal-height amplifier) is gone. | Give the window's root content a bounded ideal size, not just a minimum. Layout faults then stay local instead of moving the whole interface. |

| 29 | 2026-08-09 | Typing into the console froze the interface. | ✅ Fixed — my transcript caching had made `displayText` a MUTATING getter, and the console reads it during view body evaluation: writing observed state there invalidates the view, which re-reads, which writes again. The cache now lives in a reference box so reads mutate nothing (the compiler enforces it — `displayText` is non-mutating). Same root pattern as the earlier stall-detector loop. | Never let a view body write to observed state, including indirectly through a mutating getter or a cached property. |
| 30 | 2026-08-09 | Don't make the teacher open the console and type into a box at the bottom; ask for input in an alert and pass it to the script. | ✅ Implemented — the runner itself notices when a script sits at a question (prompt-shaped final line, no output for three seconds), publishes the question, and the app raises an alert with the question and a text field; Send passes the answer straight to the script. Detection lives in the runner, not a view, so nothing decides it mid-body. | Same: detect the waiting prompt in the process layer and ask with a native dialog; never require opening the log to answer. |

| 31 | 2026-08-09 | "One thing is needed to continue" is a false promise — a task can ask more than one question. | ✅ Fixed — the question alert is titled "A question needs your answer", which stays true whether one question comes or five. | Same: never let a prompt's title claim how many more are coming. |

| 32 | 2026-08-09 | When a publish finishes, give the teacher a clickable link to the live site (or a finishing message of that kind). | ✅ Implemented — the completed task now reads "Your website is live." above a clickable link to the site's address, picked out of the publisher's output ("Live URL:" on first publish, "Site URL:" afterwards). Netlify's own admin pages are ignored so the link is always the teacher's site. Covered by tests for both the found and not-found cases. | Same: parse the published address from the launcher's output and end the task with a link, not just a checkmark. |

| 33 | 2026-08-09 | A prompt's square-bracket default ("Enter Netlify site name [ics3u-s3-2026-gordon]:", and the same when a name is taken: "Choose a different Netlify site name (or 'q' to cancel) [ics3u-s3-2026-gordon-01]:") is command-line shorthand. Take it out of the question and put it in the answer field instead. Title the alerts "Input required". | ✅ Implemented — a bracketed default is lifted out of the wording and pre-filled into the text field, so agreeing is one keystroke and changing it is still easy; "[Default: x]" is understood too, while "[Y/n]"-style choice lists stay in the wording (they name options, not a value). Five tests cover the shapes. | Same: a GUI shows a default as a filled-in field, never as text the user must retype. |

| 34 | 2026-08-09 | "(or 'q' to cancel)" shouldn't appear in a GUI alert. | ✅ Implemented — a parenthetical that names a key to type (cancel/quit/skip) is dropped from the question and the spacing closed up, so the retry prompt reads "Choose a different Netlify site name:". Informative parentheticals such as "(y/n)" are kept, since they carry meaning rather than typing instructions. | Same: a dialog's buttons are the escape hatch; never repeat a keystroke instruction in the wording. |

| 35 | 2026-08-09 | With "(or 'q' to cancel)" hidden, Cancel must still cancel — and should do it through the script's own logic rather than by killing anything. Show a suitable closing message (orange) when it does. | ✅ Implemented — the key the script offered is captured before the aside is hidden, and Cancel sends it, so `deploy.py`'s own cancellation path runs (verified at `scripts/deploy.py:303`, which treats q/quit/exit as cancelling). The header then shows an orange "Cancelling…" while the task winds down and an orange "Cancelled" with "<task> was cancelled." once it stops — not the red failure state, even though the task exits non-zero, and the log no longer springs open. A question the script gives no way out of (the Netlify access token, for one) is cancelled by stopping the task itself, so Cancel always means what it says. | Same: capture the launcher's cancel token, send it on Cancel, and treat the resulting non-zero exit as cancellation rather than failure. |

| 36 | 2026-08-09 | Hide the guts when a task fails: a real publish hit `Netlify API error 429: API Deploy rate limit surpassed`, and the app answered by springing the raw output open. | ✅ Implemented — `FailureExplainer` reads the output and says what happened in a sentence: the rate limit ("Netlify is limiting how often websites can be published right now. Try publishing again in about a minute." — the wait read from the script's own reset line), a missing or refused Netlify token, no internet, and publishing before anything was built. The log stays one click away but no longer opens by itself. When nothing is recognised the app says nothing and shows the output, since an honest fallback beats a confident guess; a test locks that behaviour in. | Same explanations from the same markers; keep the recognise-or-stay-quiet rule. |

| 37 | 2026-08-09 | After a publish finished, starting a preview of the same section left the publish's panel on screen — the preview appeared but the interface never said it was being prepared. | ✅ Fixed — the console area chose its task by asking "has the publish any output?", and a transcript is never emptied, so a finished publish held the space forever. It now shows whichever task is running, or — once both have finished — the one that started most recently, so the publish's summary and live link stay until something else begins. Four tests cover the combinations. | Same rule: pick the panel by which task is current, never by whether a task has left output behind. |

| 38 | 2026-08-09 | The live-site link appeared only after a section's FIRST publish; publishing again to an existing site showed no link. | ✅ Fixed — a new site reports its `ssl_url` ("Live URL: https://…"), while a repeat publish quotes the saved site record, whose `url` is plain **http** ("Site:" / "Site URL:"). The link was found by looking for `https://`, so repeat publishes never matched. It now reads the address from the labelled line whatever its scheme, ignores the "Admin:" dashboard line, and offers the https form of a netlify.app address since Netlify redirects there anyway. A site on its own domain is now recognised too. Four tests cover first publish, repeat publish, custom domain, and dashboard-only output. | Same: find the address by the label the publisher prints, never by guessing at the scheme or hostname. |

| 39 | 2026-08-09 | Stopping a preview reported that something had gone wrong, though the output showed a clean run. | ✅ Fixed — ending a task on purpose kills the process, which exits non-zero, and any non-zero exit was read as failure. A task stopped by the teacher now says "Stopped" with "You stopped this.", shows no failure explanation, and no longer springs the log open. Same principle as cancellation: an ending you asked for is not a fault. | Same: distinguish "ended on purpose" from "failed" before judging by exit code. |

| 40 | 2026-08-09 | After a preview was stopped the panel still read "Preparing the preview of…", which was no longer true; the "You stopped this." line is redundant beside the "Stopped" status. | ✅ Implemented — the panel is named for what it is at the moment: "Preparing the preview of ICS3U-S5" while it is being made, "Preview of ICS3U-S5" once it has finished or been stopped. | 42 | 2026-08-09 | The example course itself was thin — it showed the folder structure but taught nothing and demonstrated little of what pages can do. | ✅ Rebuilt — roughly 225 pages of Grade 9 science (SNC1W), modelled on the structure and pacing of a real course: curriculum expectations for all five strands, 35 concept pages, 14 investigations, exercises with answers, rubric-backed tasks, tutorials, discussions, portfolio prompts, and two sections of class pages a few days apart. | 43 | 2026-08-10 | Clicking **Add Example Course** immediately raised "Input required — Install it now? (y/n):" with no context. Someone who clicked the button has already said what they want. | ✅ Fixed three ways. (1) The example install now passes `--no-update-check`, so nothing asks about updating in the middle of it. (2) The alert shows the line that preceded the question, but **only when the question cannot stand on its own** — a bare yes/no or a very short prompt. See entry 45. (3) The underlying misfire: the check compared the local image's digest against the registry's and treated *different* as *newer*. A locally built image has a digest the registry never issued, so it offered an "update" that was really a downgrade — and answering yes replaced a working build with an older one, which is exactly what broke the button. It now confirms the installed digest actually exists in the registry before offering anything. | Same flag, same context rule, same digest check (mirrored in the .ps1 peers). |

| 61 | 2026-08-10 | Cooperating with system restoration kept failing differently each run: values restored empty, folders swapped, then three windows from two. | ✅ The app now restores its own windows and system restoration is disabled (`.restorationBehavior(.disabled)`). | 66 | 2026-08-10 | Two windows, two previews: starting the second killed the first — both web views ended up showing whichever preview started last, because everything shared port 8081 and the toolchain kills that port before serving. Comparing two sections side by side is a real teacher need. | ✅ Implemented — each preview runs on its own port. The container publishes 8081–8084 plus 9081–9084 (Quartz's dev server opens a second, live-reload websocket port — the hidden collision even distinct site ports would not fix), older containers are recreated once to gain the range, and `preview.sh --port N` reaches `build_site.py`, which now kills only its own ports. The app leases ports across windows (`PreviewLeases`), refuses a fifth preview and a duplicate of an already-live section politely — the same course/section in a *different* folder is allowed, since that is the compare-two-years case — and releases the lease on stop, failure, or window close. Proven live: sections 1 and 2 of EXC2O served simultaneously on 8081/8082 with distinct content, four listeners (two sites, two websockets), both still up after continued polling. | 67 | 2026-08-10 | Phase 2: two windows on two working folders fought over the single shared container, and containers ran forever whether or not anything used them. | ✅ Implemented — one container per working folder, named `teaching-quartz-<hash of pwd -P>`; the app derives the identical name (via POSIX `realpath` — Foundation's symlink resolution strips `/private` where `pwd -P` keeps it, a mismatch the unit test caught before it stomped a live container). Each container gets its own probed HOST port block mapped to fixed container ports, `preview.sh` announces the resolved address and the app polls what was announced, and port leases contend per folder. Resources are freed: the last window on a folder closing — or a window switching folders, or the app quitting — stops that folder's container (`docker stop -t 2`; it restarts in about a second). The legacy shared container is retired automatically. Proven live: the same section of the same course served from two folders at once (host 8081 and 8091, two containers), and the app-style stop verified. | This design ports directly: name-by-folder-hash, probed port blocks, stop-on-last-window-closed. |

| 80 | 2026-08-10 | Context-clicking a course or section should offer "Open in Obsidian" — the vault the folder belongs to (a vault being just a folder of text files) — and the same action belongs in the window's top-trailing toolbar, tooltip included. First try errored: "Vault not found." | ✅ Implemented, in two rounds. Open in Obsidian sits in its own menu section — a course's menu reads Add Section… ⁄ divider ⁄ Open in Obsidian ⁄ divider ⁄ the folder commands, and a section's menu leads with it. The action lives in three places — the course/section context menu ("Open in Obsidian", `square.and.pencil` — the classic macOS compose glyph (`long.text.page.and.pencil` was tried and judged too busy); the button leads the section toolbar's trailing group, ahead of Preview/Deploy/Open in Browser), the section view's toolbar, and a new toolbar on the course settings view — each disabled when nothing answers `obsidian://` links, with the link's path percent-encoded (test-pinned). The hard-won part: `obsidian://open?path=…` only works for folders inside a vault Obsidian has REGISTERED in `~/Library/Application Support/obsidian/obsidian.json` — a `.obsidian` folder alone proves nothing (verified live), and a running Obsidian keeps its vault list in memory, ignoring registry edits until relaunch. So an unknown course is handled the long way: quit Obsidian if running (it restores its windows), seed the toolchain's bundled `.obsidian` defaults if the course has none, write the registry entry exactly as Obsidian's own "Open folder as vault" would (quitting BEFORE writing so the entry cannot be clobbered on exit), then fire the link. For a section, the vault registered is the COURSE folder, and because Obsidian opens files rather than folders (a bare folder path gets "File not found"), the link points at the section's landing page (`index.md`), falling back to the vault root when there is none. And so the teacher can SEE where they landed, the seeded defaults now include a `workspace.json` (authored by Obsidian itself, with the File Explorer's "auto-reveal current file" turned on — a layout-state flag, not an app.json setting, found by reading Obsidian's own code) so the landing page's folder highlights in Obsidian's sidebar. Round two, after a live test: Obsidian REBUILDS the layout on a vault's first open, discarding a pre-seeded one — but patching the layout Obsidian itself wrote survives a relaunch (verified). So whenever the app opens a vault while Obsidian is closed, it flips `autoReveal` on in the saved layout (everything else untouched — test-pinned); by the second open every vault reveals. The toolchain's seed stays for the day Obsidian honours it. Tests pin the containment check (a sibling folder whose name merely starts the same is not "inside"), the entry format, and that a teacher's existing vaults survive the write. Separately: the example-course installer never copied `.obsidian` in (the wizard did) — fixed in `setup_course.py`, verified locally. | Windows: same URI, same registry file under %APPDATA%/obsidian/obsidian.json — port the register-then-relaunch dance whole. |

| 81 | 2026-08-10 | The sidebar's − button stopped working: a highlighted course or section, a click, and no confirmation appeared. | ✅ Fixed — the Add Section sheet, attached after the sidebar's stack of alerts, silently broke the two that still used SwiftUI's DEPRECATED `.alert(item:)` API; the button's logic ran perfectly while its alert never presented. Both legacy alerts (removal and restore) now use the modern `.alert(_:isPresented:presenting:)` form. The real lesson is the regression test: the accessibility inspector gained a same-process `press(identifier:)`, and a hosted test now selects a course in the REAL window, presses the actual − button through the accessibility tree, and requires the confirmation alert to appear — presentation bugs like this one can no longer hide behind correct logic. | Presentation APIs age differently per platform; the Windows peer needs the same press-and-look regression test, whatever its dialog API. |

| 82 | 2026-08-10 | Polish: the Icon Composer icon becomes the app icon, and the stock About box is replaced with the custom panel designed in a companion chat. Round two: include the description text from the companion notes (tagline, the plantoir/dibber namesake, credits), scale the whole panel down — it felt big — and make it respect accessibility text sizing. | ✅ Implemented — `Plantoir.icon` lives in the repo and compiles straight into the bundle (`Assets.car` + `.icns`, `ASSETCATALOG_COMPILER_APPICON_NAME`); XcodeGen 2.46 types Icon Composer bundles natively (`wrapper.icon`), no folder-reference tricks. The About panel follows the companion notes with two deliberate deviations: it shows `NSApp.applicationIconImage` — the REAL compiled icon, shaped and margined by macOS — instead of a separate flattened `AboutIcon` asset (never drifts, nothing extra to maintain, no double-mask halo), and the support details are the notes' own — `plantoir.app/support` and `support@plantoir.app` (the domain is not yet registered; fine pre-release, must exist before shipping) — with the email row an optional so it can be hidden by setting nil. The panel now tells its story: the one-line tagline, the namesake paragraph (a plantoir is a dibber — the tool that opens a hole at the right depth so a seedling can take root), the support link, then quiet credit lines (Quartz by Jacky Zhao — a live link — Russell Gordon, Claude; the MIT sentence was later dropped) above the copyright, with `focusEffectDisabled()` so the panel does not open with a focus ring haloing the support link. Everything scaled down (128 pt icon, 26 pt name, 12 pt body, 540 pt panel) and EVERY measurement is a `@ScaledMetric(relativeTo: .body)` — icon, type, and panel width grow together under Accessibility ▸ Display ▸ Text size, the same pattern as the sidebar footer. Also fixed per the notes' warning: Info.plist carried HARDCODED version literals — now `$(MARKETING_VERSION)` / `$(CURRENT_PROJECT_VERSION)` from build settings, so bumps actually reach the About box. | Windows peer: same panel layout; version from the assembly info, icon from the executable's own resources. |

| 83 | 2026-08-10 | Regression: creating a course failed with exit 127 — "run_container_with_mount: command not found". And the progress dialog said "Creating your course" twice when details were showing. | ✅ Fixed, three layers deep. (1) `setup.sh` lost the whole container-helper trio (`run_container_with_mount`, `find_free_port_block`, `retire_legacy_container`) in the per-folder-container refactor — preview and deploy got them, setup did not, and only course creation exercises setup's create path (the PowerShell mirror had them all along). Restored. (2) The gate that should have caught it, `verify.sh`, never actually runs `setup.sh` — it now cross-checks that every helper a launcher calls is defined in that same file (comments and echoed prose excluded; proven to flag the broken file and pass the fixed ones). That check immediately caught bug three: `preview.sh`'s image pre-parser stopped at the first non-flag word, so `--image` after the course and section was never recognized — verify.sh's own preview run had been failing. Both parser halves fixed; full verify.sh green. (3) The dialog: the details console no longer repeats the task title — the header names the task once, and the detail row keeps only the precise outcome (spinner, Finished, or the exit code). | The .ps1 launchers were already correct; port the verify cross-check idea to any future Windows verify script. |

| 84 | 2026-08-10 | A new window opened while no other window exists should have NO working folder (the picker); a new window opened while others exist should adopt the folder of the window that was key when the command ran. | ✅ Implemented — the model no longer silently adopts the last-used preference at all (that lone-new-window surprise is gone; the choice stays recorded only for preference migration). Each window notes its folder whenever it becomes key; a new window whose restoration claims come up empty inherits that noted folder — falling back to any open window's folder if the memory is stale, and to nothing when it truly is the only window. Mid-session windows also skip the claim retries once claims have closed. Round two, after a janky flash of the picker: the mid-session folder decision moved to the window's `onAppear`, before its first frame commits. (A first attempt decided it in the model's initializer — WORSE: the window-group closure runs on every render, so the decision ran at the wrong moments and the flash grew longer. Initializers in a window group's content are the wrong place for once-per-window work.) Launch-time windows still wait for frame-matched restoration; pinned by test. Window RESTORATION is untouched: restored windows still get their own folders from the frame-keyed list. Policy pinned by tests; the superseded remembered-folder tests rewritten to assert the new behaviour. | Same policy on Windows: new window inherits the active window's folder, or opens the picker when first. |

| 85 | 2026-08-10 | After picking an empty folder, the picker still says "Choose Your Working Folder" above the confirmation — but a folder was just chosen. Show only the path bar and everything beneath it once a folder is selected. Round two: the path bar was not horizontally centred with the rest. | ✅ Implemented — the icon, headline, and intro paragraph appear only while a folder is still being chosen; once a candidate is awaiting confirmation, the view is just the path bar, the set-it-up explanation, and the two buttons. The bar itself centres via `ViewThatFits`: its scroll view greedily fills any width it is given (pinning a short path left), so a path that fits renders at natural size — centred by the stack — and only a path too long for the 520 pt cap gets the full-width scrolling form. An invalid choice (a problem message) keeps the header, since the teacher is back to choosing. | Same conditional header on the Windows picker. |

| 86 | 2026-08-10 | Small but important: Private Notes.md and Scratch Page.md must be created with `draft: true` — that is what keeps them from being published. | ✅ Implemented in both creation paths. The setup wizard (`setup_course.py`) now writes the two teacher-eyes-only pages as drafts, with body text saying so ("never published — a private place for your own notes"); every other per-section file still starts published. The app's Add Section keeps its sibling-mirroring (an existing section's draft flags already carry over) and now applies the same rule in its no-sibling fallback, pinned by test. Key Links.md deliberately stays published. | The rule lives in the shared Python and in `SectionAdder.unpublishedFileNames` — keep the two lists in step on Windows too. |

| 87 | 2026-08-10 | A new ADVANCED per-section setting: a custom domain. When set, every link to the published site shows the teacher's domain instead of the Netlify address. Post-creation settings only — the new-course wizard stays untouched. | ✅ Implemented — each section's settings gain a collapsed "Advanced" group holding a Custom domain field: entries are cleaned on the way in (a pasted `https://ics3u.school.ca/` stores as `ics3u.school.ca`), a gentle orange note appears when the entry does not read as a domain, and the caption says plainly that the site must already answer there (set up in Netlify first). Stored per section under `custom_domains.sections` in `course_config.json`. When a deploy finishes, the live-site link swaps its host for the domain — path preserved, promoted to https — leaving the Netlify address everywhere the domain is empty. Four tests: config round-trip, normalization, the link swap, and the runner end to end. | Same field and swap on Windows; the config key is shared. |

| 88 | 2026-08-10 | Every course section should get a social sharing card (the image iMessage and friends show for a shared link), in the design language of the plantoir.app card: section colour scheme, the chosen header and body fonts, course name large, course code as subtitle with the course emoji beside it, the S-marker setting respected, no logo — and no domain, since Apple's share sheet overlays the domain itself. | ✅ Implemented in the toolchain, so both the app and command-line users get it: `scripts/social_card.py` (Pillow) draws a 1200×630 card — scheme's light background, course name in the header font and scheme's secondary colour (auto-sized, wrapping to at most two lines), then the emoji (Noto Color Emoji, rendered at its bitmap strike and rescaled) beside "CODE S1" in the body font and darkgray. `build_site.py` draws it fresh on EVERY build over the Quartz scaffold's `static/og-image.png` — which the site's head already links — so name/scheme/font/emoji/marker changes always reach the card, and a failed draw never fails a build. The font files moved from the app bundle to `support/fonts/` (single source: the container draws with the same TTFs the app previews; the "Helvetica, Arial" system choice falls back to Inter). En route, two toolchain repairs: `toolchain_hash` now prunes non-recipe paths (from the repo root it was checksumming courses/, node_modules, and the app for many minutes, with a tag that changed every build), and `preview.sh`'s shebang moved to line 1 (it sat mid-file below a detection block, so the kernel could not exec the script directly). Cards verified visually: local renders for two schemes/font pairings/marker states, and the in-container render with the colour emoji. | The renderer is shared Python — Windows gets cards with no extra work; only the emoji font path is Debian-specific and already probed. |

| 89 | 2026-08-10 | Two course-title issues: (1) the toolchain always prepends "Grade x" to the chosen course title — it should be a switch, like the section-number setting; (2) renaming a course is not reflected when the site next builds. A test course showed a third: "Grade 12 Computer Science, Grade 12, U, Section 1" — the grade doubled onto a catalog name that already carried it. | ✅ Fixed at the root: the landing page's title was BAKED into `index.md` frontmatter at scaffold time and never touched again. It is now COMPUTED on every build from the current settings — course name, the new course-level "Show the grade in the site title" toggle (default on, in Course Settings beside the reading-time toggle), and the existing section-marker setting (which now governs the ", Section N" suffix by computation rather than by stripping) — and written only into the MERGED copy, so the teacher's source `index.md` is never modified. The grade never doubles: a name already carrying its grade label anywhere gets no prefix. The same rule now guides the app's Add Section scaffold titles and the wizard's (as sensible starting values; the build recomputes regardless), pinned by tests. Verified live in the reporting course: renamed to "Computer Science", built as "Grade 12 Computer Science" — rename flowed, single grade, marker-off honoured. | The toggle is one config key (`show_grade_in_title`); the compute lives in shared Python — Windows needs only the settings checkbox. Round two: the wizard's Appearance section carries the same toggle, right under the section-marker one, so a new course can choose from the start. Round three, from a live report: a course named with the Ministry's citation form ("Computer Science, Grade 12, U") contains its grade, so a no-doubling guard suppressed the prefix and the toggle appeared dead. A first fix stripped the catalog suffix automatically — REJECTED: the behaviour must not outguess the user. Final design: the switch is fully LITERAL (on prepends, off does not, no exceptions; every stripping/dedup cleverness reverted, the wizard suggests the Ministry's formal name untouched), and when the switch is on while the name already carries the grade label, a quiet orange warning under the toggle — in the wizard and in settings — says the title would repeat it and leaves the fix to the teacher: edit the name, or turn the switch off. Round four: the toggle is PER SECTION, like the marker — it lives in each section's settings block (the wizard's single Appearance toggle writes every section's value, marker-style), stored as `show_grade_in_title.sections.sectionN` with an older course-wide boolean still honoured. |

| 90 | 2026-08-10 | The course settings window's "Cancel" should read "Revert" — Cancel belongs to dialog boxes. | ✅ Implemented — the button now says Revert (still disabled until there is something to revert), because a settings form is not a dialog: nothing closes, the values simply go back to the last save. | Same label on the Windows settings form. |

| 91 | 2026-08-11 | The Preview and Deploy toolbar buttons should show text labels beside their icons — a play triangle and a paper plane are not self-explanatory. | ✅ Implemented — both buttons wear their titles via `.labelStyle(.titleAndIcon)`; while a preview runs, the first reads "Stop Preview" with the stop icon — the clearer wording won over keeping the button's width steady, since the width changes with the title anyway. The neighbouring buttons (Open in Obsidian, Open in Browser) stay icon-only: their symbols are the familiar app icons, and labelling everything would crowd the toolbar. | Give the Windows toolbar's Preview and Deploy buttons the same visible text labels; keep the others icon-only with tooltips. |

| 92 | 2026-08-11 | When creating a course, an option (on by default) should pre-populate it with ready-made example content for that specific course code — real pages in the style of the example course, using whatever Obsidian features suit the subject — and a second option should include the official Ontario curriculum, every expectation as a linkable page. Plantoir will eventually carry content for every Ontario course code; ADA1O (Drama, Grade 9, Open) is the first. | ✅ Implemented in the toolchain plus two wizard toggles. Content lives in `support/example_content/<CODE>/` — a `manifest.json` naming the folders/files it provides plus `shared/` and `per_section/` payload trees — so adding a course code needs no code changes anywhere. The setup wizard offers the two questions only when a payload exists for the typed code; the payload's folders join the structure prompts' DEFAULTS (still vetoable), content installs before scaffolding so nothing generic overwrites it, re-runs never touch an existing file, and both answers are saved in `course_config.json` (`prepopulate_example_content`, `include_curriculum_pages`). With curriculum pages declined, delimited blocks (`%%curriculum-start/end%%` comment markers) vanish from every page, whole-line `![[A2.2]]` transclusions are dropped, and inline links to expectation pages unlink into their visible words — no dangling links either way. The app's wizard shows a "Starting Content" section: the pre-populate toggle (on by default), the curriculum toggle beneath it (disabled when the first is off), or a quiet note when no content exists for the code yet. ADA1O ships 81 pages: all 26 specific + 9 overall expectations verbatim from the Ministry's 2010 Arts document (teacher prompts in folded callouts, source cited on About These Expectations), and drama-specific Concepts, Conventions, Tasks, Discussions, Setup, and Style folders — including the drama edition of "What This Site Can Do", where features drama has no use for (equations, code) are still demonstrated but framed as such. | The installer is shared Python, the payload ships in `support/` — the Windows app only needs the same two checkboxes writing the same two config keys, plus the bundled-manifest existence check that decides whether to show them. Round two: pre-populating LOCKS the structure — the Windows wizard must hide its folders/files editor when the toggle is on (a caption explains the example content chooses the structure) and show it again when the toggle is off. | 

| 93 | 2026-08-11 | Choosing to pre-populate scattered empty default folders (Examples, Exercises, Portfolios, Tutorials, the two Curriculum folders) around the example pages, All Classes held only four days, the section landing page lacked the example course's transclusions, and reference pages like Help Sessions and a populated Key Links were missing. The example content should decide the course's structure outright — the folders/files/hidden/expandable choices are not asked when pre-populating — and ADA1O should match the shipped example course's depth, with a semestered September–January arc. | ✅ Implemented. The manifest is now the WHOLE structure authority: when pre-populating, the setup wizard skips every structure question and prints the layout it is using; the app wizard hides its Structure editor behind a caption. No stray empty folders, and the LCS-specific default files can no longer leak in. ADA1O grew to 132 pages: a 26-class semester in four units (Building the Ensemble → Saying Yes → Telling Stories → Production and Performance, each closing with a performance task; the final class page is the payload's one `draft: true` example), a Warm-Ups folder (drama's practice library — 8 games, each naming what it trains), Tutorials (Learning Lines, How to Rehearse, Giving and Receiving Notes…), Portfolios (the Drama Journal hub moved here with first-entry, strong-entry, checklist, growth, and final-reflection pages), four new Concepts (Blocking and Stage Areas, Subtext, Ensemble, The Fourth Wall), a Culminating Performance task, Help Sessions, and a per-section landing page that transcludes the most recent class, Help Sessions, and Key Links — with a teacher-facing comment explaining how to advance it. The installer replaces a `__SECTION_NUMBER__` sentinel in per-section pages and always accepts a payload's `index.md`. | Same rule on Windows: pre-populate on → structure editor hidden, config lists come from the manifest via the shared Python. The payload format is unchanged — only content grew. |

| 94 | 2026-08-11 | The default empty course used LCS-specific terms. The factory defaults should be school-neutral ("Extra Help" rather than "Grove Time"; no "SIC Drop-In Sessions", no "College Board Curriculum" folder), with a "Use LCS-specific terminology" switch restoring the school's own set-up. | ✅ Implemented — both wizards. Factory defaults are now neutral: shared files "Extra Help.md" + "Learning Goals.md", no College Board Curriculum folder. The app's Structure section gains the toggle (off by default, hidden when example content drives the structure); flipping it swaps the FACTORY portion of the lists wholesale while file names the teacher typed themselves survive. The CLI wizard asks the same question before the structure prompts; saved lists always win over either factory set. The choice is stored as `use_lcs_terminology` in `course_config.json`. The hidden-by-default list covers both terminologies, so whichever names a course ends up with are hidden. | The Windows wizard needs the same toggle in its structure area, writing the same config key; the two factory sets live in `scripts/setup_course.py` (`DEFAULT_*` vs `LCS_*`) and must stay mirrored in the Windows equivalent of `WizardDefaults`. |

| 95 | 2026-08-11 | Dates matter: with every pre-populated class page carrying the same creation date, the All Classes listing cannot sort — the most recent class never floats to the top, which is the whole point of that page. Class pages need real, distinct, semester-shaped dates like a genuine course's. | ✅ Implemented — a second sentinel. Regular payload pages keep `created: __CREATED__` (install time); class pages carry `created: __CREATED_CLASS_K__` (K = the class's position in the course, 1–26 for ADA1O), which the installer turns into real dates: every other weekday at 07:00, anchored at September 8 of the current school year (installing any time from August through the following July lands on the same school year's September). ADA1O's semester runs September 8 → mid-November, all weekdays, strictly ascending — so the listing sorts, and the dates read like a real course's. | The sentinel replacement lives in the shared Python installer — Windows needs nothing beyond shipping the same payloads. Payload authors: number `__CREATED_CLASS_K__` in chronological order; the installer does the rest. |

| 96 | 2026-08-11 | Every page a class links to (a convention, a discussion, a warm-up…) carried the install-time date, so category pages (Conventions, Discussions, …) could not list their pages in the order the course actually met them — an order that has meaning for students. If Choral Speaking first happens on Unit 3, Day 5, its page should share Unit 3, Day 5's date. | ✅ Implemented — automatically, with no annotation burden on payload authors. At install time the installer reads the payload's class pages in chronological order, collects every page each class links to, and gives each shared page the date of the FIRST class that references it (`first_use_dates` in `setup_course.py`). Pages no class references — the site-reference pages in Style, for instance — keep the install-time date, which sorts them harmlessly below the semester. Verified in a built course: the Conventions listing now runs in teaching order, newest first. | The derivation lives in the shared Python installer — Windows needs nothing. Payload authors need to know only one thing: class pages' links ARE the schedule; linking a page from a class page is what dates it. |

**Full sync completed on Windows on the `windows-sync` branch.** All 457 tests
run clean via `dotnet test Plantoir.Tests/Plantoir.Tests.csproj` (0 failures).

- — **111–121, 123–129, 131–138** (mermaid fonts, labels and pie charts;
  KaTeX + mhchem; the "When did we do this?" / table-of-contents split; the

| 97 | 2026-08-11 | During first preview or deploy, the per-section dependency install (npm) can run quietly for a long time on slower machines, and the milestone label looks frozen. Show a live seconds counter — "still working… (Ns)" — while a step is quiet. | ✅ Implemented on macOS, ported FROM the Windows app (the feature's origin — commit "Fix empty glyphs, layout, wizard gating and slow-step reassurance"). Identical semantics: a step that reports its own count (uploads, build steps) shows the count — the count is the movement; otherwise, once the script has been quiet for 4 seconds, the milestone label appends "still working… (Ns)" with N counting up live each second. On macOS this rides the existing one-second TimelineView tick and the runner's `lastOutputAt`; three unit tests pin the quiet threshold, the own-count precedence, and that a finished task never shows the timer. | Already present on Windows — this row records the macOS port for parity. |

| 98 | 2026-08-11 | Restored windows briefly flash "Choose Your Working Folder" for a few seconds at launch before each loads the folder it had — the claim machinery polls for the restored frame to settle while the picker is already on screen. | ✅ Fixed two ways. (1) The AppKit layer now does the timing: `NSApplication.didFinishRestoringWindowsNotification` fires when state restoration completes and every frame is final, so each window tries its frame match at that exact moment instead of discovering it by polling (the polling stays as the fallback, and the notification path is success-only so a mismatched window can never give up early and order-claim a sibling's folder). (2) While a claim may still arrive — claims open AND unclaimed entries remain — a folder-less window shows a quiet small spinner instead of the picker (`isResolvingRestoredFolder`); the flag clears the moment the claim resolves either way, so a genuine no-folder window still gets its picker, a mid-session window immediately, and a first launch instantly (no remembered entries). Three tests pin `aClaimMayStillArrive`: true with open claims and entries, false after claims close, false with nothing to claim. | The Windows app restores windows through its own mechanism; if it shows a picker flash on restore, apply the same two ideas: resolve folder assignment on the platform's restoration-complete signal, and hold a neutral loading state (never the picker) while an assignment is still possible. |

| 99 | 2026-08-11 | A restored window comes back to the right working folder, but every course's disclosure triangle (and the Archived group) is folded up — the sidebar forgets which courses were being worked on. Track disclosure state per window and restore it. | ✅ Implemented — the state rides in the same per-window memory that restores folders. Course triangles and the Archived group now bind to the window's model (`expandedCourseCodes`, `isShowingArchived`) instead of view-internal state; every change re-records the window list; each remembered entry stores the open course codes (comma-joined — codes never contain commas) and the Archived flag beside the path and frame; and claiming an entry restores the folder AND the sidebar exactly as it was left. Old entries without the new keys load all-collapsed, and three tests pin the storage round-trip, the legacy-entry fallback, and the per-window recording. Round two adds the SELECTION: the chosen course, section, or archived item persists per window too, stored in a plain string form (`course\|CODE`, `section\|CODE\|N`, `archived\|ID`) with round-trip tests for every case; unrecognized or empty stored selections restore none. Verified live on-machine: two windows quit and relaunched with their expansion and selections intact, confirmed both in the claim log and in the re-recorded store. Live in-app probes also pin that a freshly created sidebar honours pre-set expansion — the restoration order. | Windows already restores per-window folders through its own memory; extend its entries the same way (expanded course list + archived flag + selection string) and apply them when a window claims its folder. |

| 100 | 2026-08-11 | The header-font sample previews a stand-in headline ("Grade 11 Computer Science") even when the course has a name. Once a course name exists, the sample should render THAT — the teacher previewing their own site's headline. | ✅ Implemented — the font editor takes a `sampleHeadline`; the per-section settings pass the saved course name, and the new-course wizard passes the name field LIVE, so typing "Drama" re-renders the header sample as "Drama" immediately. Whitespace-only or empty names keep the stand-in, pinned by two tests. Body and code samples keep their explanatory sentences — they demonstrate running text and code, which a course name cannot. Round two: the sample shows the landing title as the build actually computes it — grade prefix and \", Section N\" suffix included, exactly per the section's grade and marker switches (`CourseConfiguration.landingTitle`, mirroring `computed_landing_title` in `build_site.py`); in the wizard it follows the name, code, both switches, and the first section number live. | Same rule on Windows: feed the COMPUTED landing title (grade + name + marker, per the switches) into the header-font sample, with the stand-in as the empty-name fallback; mirror `computed_landing_title` from `build_site.py`. |

| 101 | 2026-08-11 | Some teachers publish on their own hosting rather than Netlify. Offer a per-course "publish to a local folder" option: Plantoir copies the built section sites into a chosen folder on this computer (remembered per course), and the teacher uploads them however they prefer (e.g. SFTP). Be smart about copying — only changed files should move. | ✅ Implemented. A course-level "Publishing" setting: Netlify (the default, unchanged) or "A folder on this Mac" with a folder chooser, stored as `deploy_target` + `deploy_folder_path` in `course_config.json`. Deploys then run `deploy.sh --to-folder <path>`: since the working folder is bind-mounted, the built site is already on the host, and the launcher does a host-side incremental sync (`rsync -a --delete` — only changed files copy, removals propagate; verified: second run copies 0 files). Each section publishes into its own `sectionN` subfolder so sections can never overwrite each other — the caption says so. Netlify credentials are never touched in folder mode. On completion the app shows "published to a folder" with a Show in Finder button (the launcher prints a `PUBLISHED_FOLDER=` marker) instead of a live-site link. `deploy.ps1` carries the same flag using `robocopy /MIR`. | The Windows settings UI needs the same Publishing picker + folder chooser writing the same two config keys, and its deploy invocation passes `--to-folder`; the launcher work is already done (`deploy.ps1`, robocopy exit codes below 8 are success). Completion shows the folder in Explorer via the same `PUBLISHED_FOLDER=` marker. |

| 102 | 2026-08-11 | Round two of folder publishing, from live testing: (a) the Publishing choice only existed in Course Settings, not the new-course wizard; (b) a course could be SAVED set to publish to a folder with an empty path — and the deploy then quietly went to Netlify; (c) nothing checked the chosen folder was writable; (d) the progress milestones still said "Connecting to Netlify…" during folder deploys; (e) after a folder publish, teachers may double-click the copied pages and wonder why the site looks broken. | ✅ Implemented. (a) A shared `PublishingChoiceView` (picker + folder field + Choose… + captions) now serves BOTH Course Settings and the wizard's new Publishing section, and the wizard writes `deploy_target`/`deploy_folder_path` into the config it hands the setup wizard — plus `setup_course.py` now carries over any config key it does not own when re-saving, so app-written keys survive the setup run (verified end-to-end in the container: deploy keys and a synthetic future key both survived). (b+c) `CourseConfiguration.deployFolderProblem(forPath:)` validates live — empty, nonexistent, a file, or unwritable each gets its own plain-words message shown in orange under the field; Course Settings disables Save while a problem stands, and the wizard blocks Create the same way. Choosing a folder through the panel runs the same check instantly. (d) Two new milestone lists (`deployToFolder`, `buildAndDeployToFolder`) replace the Netlify wording with "Copying your files…" keyed to the folder-mode output; a test pins that folder publishing never mentions Netlify. (e) The folder-deploy completion adds a quiet note: the pages won't look right opened straight from the folder — the site only displays properly once it's on the web host. | Windows needs all five: the Publishing group in BOTH its settings and wizard (share the control); block save/create on an empty, missing, or unwritable folder with the same inline messages; validate on selection; folder-mode progress labels keyed to `deploy.ps1`'s robocopy output (never "Netlify"); and the same render-note sentence after folder publishes. The `setup_course.py` preservation fix is shared Python — nothing to port. |

| 103 | 2026-08-11 | "Deploy" is web-developer jargon — and the empty-state message under it ("…or Deploy to publish it to Netlify") was wrong twice over for a course set to publish to a folder. Drop "Deploy" from the GUI entirely. | ✅ Implemented. The toolbar button is now "Publish" (same paper-plane icon), and the No Preview Running message follows the course's actual publishing choice: "…or Publish to put it online." for Netlify, "…or Publish to copy it to your publishing folder." for folder publishing. Those were the only two user-facing uses — the tooltip and progress title already said "Publish"; everything else matching "deploy" is internal (script names, config keys, output markers) and unchanged. The `deployButton` accessibility identifier is kept, so UI tests are untouched. | Same sweep on Windows: the visible button says "Publish", the empty-state message follows `deploy_target`, and internal names stay as they are. The launchers keep their file names (`deploy.ps1`) — teachers never see them. |

| 104 | 2026-08-11 | Adding a section re-runs the course setup, which rewrites the course's folders and files — while a preview or publish of that same course may be mid-copy of those very files. Clicking quickly could make the scripts stomp on each other. | ✅ Implemented. A small cross-window registry (`CourseActivity`) knows which courses are busy: previews via the existing `PreviewLeases` (held for the preview's whole life), publishes via begin/end records around `startDeploy` (ended on every exit path, including a failed build). The sidebar's "Add Section…" item is disabled while its course is previewing or publishing anywhere, with a short menu line naming what's in the way: "Available once preview completed" / "Available once publish completed" (or both). Round two, from live testing: the menu showed STALE state — context-menu content is built when the row renders, not when the menu opens, and the registries were plain static arrays, so starting a preview never re-rendered the row (a normal click did, which is why the gate seemed to work only after selecting the course first). Both registries now keep their arrays in `@Observable` stores, and the row reads the busy state during its own render, so rows — menus included — redraw the moment activity changes. Other courses in the folder are unaffected, and the same code in a different working folder is a different course — five tests pin the registry, including two publishes of one course ending independently. Adding a NEW course stays allowed during previews: its file tree is disjoint, builds are isolated per section, and the container patches are idempotent (analysed in this session; the rare container-recreation edge is in TODO.md). | Windows: same rule — its Add Section entry point declines while any of the course's sections is previewing or publishing in any window, with the same explanatory line. Track publishes centrally beside the port-lease registry the port-block behaviour (entry above) already requires — and heed the staleness lesson: make sure the menu item's enabled state is read when the menu OPENS (or the registry change re-renders whatever hosts the menu), not captured at some earlier render. |

| 105 | 2026-08-11 | Stopping a preview (the button, clicking away from the section, closing the window) terminated only the HOST-side launcher; inside the container the whole serve chain (python3 → npm exec quartz → node → esbuild) kept running — proven by SIGTERMing a live preview and getting HTTP 200 from inside the container afterwards. An orphaned server idles but holds node's RAM; an orphaned mid-flight BUILD burns real CPU until it completes. | ✅ Implemented. `preview.sh CODE N --stop` kills the section's container-side processes and exits. Two design points: (1) processes are found by WORKING DIRECTORY (`/proc/*/cwd` under the section's `.merged_output` folder), not by port — so mid-build processes are caught too, and another section's processes can never be touched; SIGTERM first, SIGKILL survivors after a second. (2) Stop mode never starts anything — no engine bootstrap, no image build, no container creation; engine down or container absent is "nothing to stop", exit 0. The kill script is piped to the container's python3 over stdin, so it works against ANY image, no rebuild needed. The app runs it fire-and-forget from `stopPreview` (`PreviewStopper`, output discarded, process held until exit) whenever a preview was actually running. Verified end-to-end: server up → `--stop` → "Stopped 4 process(es)", only the container's `tail` keepalive left, server unreachable — and the host-side launcher exits by itself once its docker exec loses the remote process. `preview.ps1` carries the same `--stop` (same stdin-piped kill script). | Windows app: call `.\preview.ps1 CODE N --stop` fire-and-forget wherever a preview stops (stop button, navigate away, window close). The launcher work is done; note it must run the WORKING FOLDER's launcher, quietly, and never surface its output. |

| 106 | 2026-08-11 | Teachers will be encouraged to use an LLM for bulk edits in Obsidian (re-dating a raft of files, say) — and an LLM can make a mess that is hard to undo by hand. Give the app whole-course backups: made on purpose before risky editing, listed in the sidebar, restorable, and deletable for good. | ✅ Implemented. "Back Up Now" in a course's context menu zips the whole course through the SAME exclusion list section-removal already uses (`.merged_output`, `node_modules`, caches — the rebuildable bulk stays out), named `<CODE>_backup_<timestamp>.zip` in the shared `courses/_backups/<CODE>/` folder. The NAME is the discriminator: backups, archives (`<CODE>_<timestamp>.zip`), and the wizard's automatic zips (`<timestamp>.zip`) each have a parser that accepts only its own form, so the three kinds never appear in each other's lists (pinned by tests). A "Backups" sidebar group sits above Archived (hidden when empty; opens itself when a backup is made; its disclosure and selection persist per window like everything else). Each backup row: Restore…, Show in Finder, Delete Backup…. RESTORE archives the current course first (WITHOUT removing it) — even a restore has an undo — then unpacks and verifies the zip and replaces the course folder's CONTENTS in place, never the folder itself: the folder is Obsidian's vault and its file watcher is anchored to the folder's identity, so a swapped folder left an open vault showing stale files until reopened (a test pins the folder's file-system identity across a restore); the zip STAYS after restoring (only deleting removes it); declined with a plain-words message while the course is previewing or publishing (`CourseActivity`). DELETE is the app's one true deletion, and both its confirmation and the restore confirmation say exactly what will happen (including that anything added after the backup was made isn't in it). Backing up stays available mid-preview — it only reads. Selecting a backup shows a detail pane with Restore/Delete, mirroring Archived. Round two: archives are deletable too — Delete Archive… in the archived row's context menu and detail pane, with a confirmation warning that if the course is no longer in Courses & Clubs, the archive is its only remaining copy. The removal-time promise ("nothing is deleted") stays true: deletion is a second, separate, deliberate act. | Windows: same three name forms in the same `_backups` folder (the zip/unzip helpers differ; robocopy-era code already handles paths); a Backups group above Archived with the same context menu, confirmations, and busy-decline; restore = archive current without removing, then replace the course folder's CONTENTS in place (never the folder itself — Obsidian's watcher is anchored to it), keep zip. |

| 110 | 2026-08-12 | The "still working… (Ns)" reassurance timer did not start at zero. Creating a course showed the first step — "Getting this Mac ready…" — opening at 35 seconds, which reads as though the app had been stalled since before the teacher pressed the button, undermining confidence in the whole progress bar. | ✅ Fixed at the source. `lastOutputAt` (the quiet clock) was initialized when the ScriptRunner was CONSTRUCTED, and a runner is built when its sheet or window appears — so the wizard's runner had been ticking for the whole time the teacher spent filling in the form, and the section view's preview/deploy runners tick from the moment a section is opened. Starting a run now resets the clock, so it measures silence from THIS task's start. Every runner benefited, not just the wizard's. A test pins it: a runner whose clock is 35 seconds stale, then started on a stand-in script, must show no timer and a fresh clock. | Same bug shape on Windows if its runner equivalent initializes the quiet clock at construction: reset it when a task starts, not when the view appears. |
| 111 | 2026-08-13 | Mermaid diagrams render differently in the Plantoir preview than in Chrome. | ✅ Fixed, and it was not the rendering engine. Quartz hyphenates body text, and that hyphenation was leaking into mermaid diagram labels — WebKit acts on it and Chromium ignores it, so a flowchart node read "A. STEM Skills, Ca-reers, and Connec-tions" in the preview and correctly in Chrome. A diagram label is not prose, so `append_mermaid_styles()` in `scripts/build_site.py` now turns hyphenation off inside `.mermaid`. Established by rendering the same page through both engines: the rule makes WebKit's output identical to Chromium's, moving only the node text. Verified through a real container build, and through a fresh build of an example-content course. It carries its own marker rather than joining the transclusion block, so course folders built before today pick it up on their next build instead of being skipped. | Shared Python, so Windows gets this for free — no Windows-side work. (A Chromium preview engine was also prototyped while diagnosing this; it works, but the CSS fix made it unnecessary, so it stays unmerged on the `chromium-preview` branch with its findings in `CHROMIUM-PREVIEW.md`.) |
| 112 | 2026-08-13 | Still wrong in Safari/the preview: mermaid label text is cut off mid-word ("An error ap", "Did any output p"), and Arc renders the same page correctly. | ⚠️ Fix shipped for the mechanism that explains it, but NOT reproduced locally — stated plainly rather than claimed. Mermaid sizes each box by measuring its label in the course's code font, which Quartz serves from Google Fonts with `display=swap`: the browser paints with a narrower fallback and swaps the real font in later. If mermaid measures before the swap, every box is sized for the fallback and the real text overflows and clips. `patch_mermaid_font_wait()` in `scripts/build_site.py` now awaits `document.fonts.ready` before `mermaid.run()`. On this machine the font was always cached before mermaid ran (`document.fonts.check` true at nav), so the clipping could not be reproduced here — the fix addresses the race rather than a reproduction. | Also corrects entry 111: the mermaid patches were inside the `full_rebuild` branch, so existing course folders would NOT have healed on a normal build as claimed. Both mermaid patches now run in the ALWAYS section, verified against a course folder that already existed. Shared Python, so Windows gets both for free. |
| 113 | 2026-08-13 | The pie chart in "What This Site Can Do" renders with its title cut off, in both WebKit and Arc — the showcase was demonstrating that the site renders pie charts badly. | ✅ Fixed, and it is mermaid's bug rather than ours — reproduced with mermaid alone, no Quartz involved. Mermaid centres a pie title on the PIE, which the legend pushes leftward, and never widens the chart to fit the title, so the overflow falls outside the viewBox and `overflow: hidden` eats it. Measured budget is a constant **450px** (~28 characters); legend width does not change it. 27 of the payloads' 33 pie titles were over it — "Where a working programmer's hours actually go" (711px) lost its first nine characters. All 33 titles shortened and re-measured through the real renderer: 0 clipped, tightest now 433px of 450px. `lint_payload.py` fails a pie title over 28 characters, and the rule is in the example-content skill. | Content and lint only — nothing for Windows to port, but the same limit applies to any pie chart a teacher writes, so it is worth knowing when supporting them. |
| 114 | 2026-08-13 | The pie-title fix should also work for pie charts teachers write themselves, not just the bundled example content. | ✅ `patch_mermaid_pie_title_fit()` in `scripts/build_site.py` now re-fits every pie chart's viewBox to what mermaid actually drew, immediately after `mermaid.run()`. CSS cannot do this — the viewBox is an attribute — and `getBBox()` covers the title even when it lies outside the box, so a long title widens the chart instead of losing its first words, and a short one loses the wasted margin. Only pie charts are touched (`svg[aria-roledescription="pie"]`); re-fitting every diagram would disturb layouts that are already correct. Proved end to end on a real build: a deliberately planted 68-character title clipped 300px before and renders complete with 6px clearance on BOTH sides after. Tested to 127 characters. Because this removes the constraint, the linter's hard 28-character failure was removed — it would now block legitimate titles — and the skill keeps short titles as guidance rather than a rule. | Shared Python, so Windows gets it for free. |
| 115 | 2026-08-13 | The first pie slice is drawn in the page background colour, so it disappears — and its legend swatch with it. | ✅ Not that one chart: EVERY pie chart in every course lost its first slice. Mermaid's base theme takes `pie1` from `primaryColor`, and Quartz sets `primaryColor` to `--light`, the page background — correct for a flowchart node, wrong for a pie slice. Measured: slice 1 `#faf8f8`, page `#faf8f8`, identical. `patch_mermaid_pie_colours()` now builds `pie1`–`pie12` from the site's own accents (`--secondary`, `--tertiary`, `--darkgray`) blended toward `--light`. Blending toward `--light` is what makes it work in BOTH themes, since `--light` is always the page and `--dark` always the text. Blend fractions were chosen by search, not by eye: each of the first six slices keeps ≥4.5:1 with the label text and ≥1.45:1 with the page, as distinct from each other as a two-accent scheme allows. Also sets `pieOpacity: 1` — mermaid draws slices at 0.7, blending them 30% into the page, which pushed dark mode below the contrast the palette was chosen for. Verified on a real build in both themes. | Shared Python; Windows gets it free. Any course colour scheme is handled, because the palette is derived at render time from that course's own variables. |
| 116 | 2026-08-13 | Does the pie palette hold up with more than three slices, and across the colour schemes teachers can choose? | ✅ It did not, and both questions were right to ask. The fractions in entry 115 were tuned against one scheme; swept across all 43 schemes × light and dark = 86 combinations, **74 failed on the first six slices**, some with slices barely distinguishable from the label text. The palette is now SOLVED at render time instead: build a pool of tints of that scheme's own colours, keep only those clearing 4.5:1 against the label and 1.45:1 against the page, then walk the pool taking the colour farthest from everything chosen so far — anchored on the course's main accent so the chart still looks like that course. Across all 86 combinations: **0 contrast failures**, worst separation 14 (was 0 — identical slices). Verified on real builds with a teacher-written page of 3, 6, 9 and 12 slice charts: contrast never below 4.55/1.45, no slice ever the page colour, separation 56/24/19/15 on the default scheme and 47/15/12/10 on a black-and-grey monochrome one. The patch also removes the earlier fixed-fraction block from a scaffold that already has it, so no duplicate declarations. | Shared Python; Windows gets it free. A monochrome scheme can only yield tints of one hue, so its slices differ by lightness alone — inherent, not a defect. |
| 117 | 2026-08-13 | On a page linked from many others, "When did we do this?" stretches up and crowds out the table of contents. Make them share the column, roughly half each, each with its own scrollbar — but keep the backlinks tucked under a short table of contents. | ✅ `append_sidebar_sharing_styles()` in `scripts/build_site.py`. The table of contents is now unshrinkable and capped at 50% of the column; the backlinks take whatever is left; the two LISTS carry the scrollbars, because their parents are row flex containers so the overflow happens one level in. Measured on the reported page (ICS4U ▸ The Software Project): before, the contents got 98px and were clipped; after, they get their full 291px with all ten headings and no scrollbar, while the backlinks take 417px and scroll. With both long the split is 386/354 and both scroll. The cap is skipped via `:not(:only-child)` so a page nothing links to still uses the whole column, and specificity is kept below Quartz's `.toc:has(button.toc-header.collapsed)` so collapsing still works. | Shared Python, so Windows gets it free. Known trade-off, stated rather than hidden: a LONG table of contents with only a few backlinks leaves the contents capped at half with slack below, because CSS cannot make one panel's cap depend on whether its sibling needs the space. |
| 118 | 2026-08-13 | "Getting this Mac ready" sits for nearly half a minute before a preview, and expanding the details shows only "Starting…". What is it doing, can it be faster, and can the pane say more? | ✅ It was computing the image tag — a SHA-256 of every file in the build recipe — by spawning one `shasum` PROCESS PER FILE. With eighteen example-content payloads now inside the recipe that is **3,536 files**. Measured on the reported Mac: the hash took **36.4s** of a 36.75s startup; the same run with `--image` (which skips hashing) finished in **0.33s**. `toolchain_hash` now pipes `find -print0 | sort -z | xargs -0 shasum`, batching them into a handful of invocations: **35s → 0.16s**, and the hash is byte-identical (`afef79f8` both ways), so no teacher gets a surprise image rebuild. Fixed in all three launchers, whose function bodies are verified identical. The launchers also now print "Checking whether your website builder is up to date…" before the step, so the details pane is never blank — worded to avoid colliding with any milestone marker. | Windows had the same bug, worse: `$combined += (Get-FileHash …)` inside a loop is quadratic because PowerShell strings are immutable. All three `.ps1` launchers now collect into `$hashes` and `-join` once — same characters, same order, same tag. **Not execute-tested: no PowerShell on this Mac.** |
| 119 | 2026-08-13 | Mermaid diagrams still mangled until a reload, on a fresh build where the font fix was demonstrably present. | ✅ Root cause found from a console screenshot, not from my measurements: the Google Fonts request was returning **HTTP 400**. Quartz builds ONE stylesheet request from all three typography choices, and Plantoir offers system stacks — this course used `Helvetica, Arial` for header and body. Google rejects the entire request if any family is unknown to it, so **no fonts downloaded at all**, including the IBM Plex Mono that mermaid measures diagram labels in. It also explains why `static/fonts/` was always empty: the emitter fetches the same URL to self-host and got the same 400. `patch_google_font_href()` now filters system stacks and known system families out of the request (a stack needs no download — it is already on the machine) and drops the request entirely when nothing is left to ask for; `Head.tsx` skips the empty link. Confirmed against Google directly: old URL 400, filtered URL 200. In the browser the page now reports `IBM Plex Mono 400 loaded` and `600 loaded` for the first time. | Shared Python — Windows gets it free. Worth knowing generally: `document.fonts.check()` returns true for a family that never downloaded, because the name resolves through the fallback. It misled four of my reproduction attempts. |
| 120 | 2026-08-13 | A chemistry page wants `\ce{}` — mhchem — which teachers coming from other KaTeX setups expect, and which three payloads warned was unavailable. | ✅ Available now, and it cost one import. Quartz renders KaTeX at BUILD time through `rehype-katex`, and mhchem is a KaTeX extension loaded by importing it — the `katex` package Quartz already installs ships `katex/contrib/mhchem`, so nothing new is downloaded. `patch_katex_mhchem()` in `scripts/build_site.py` adds `import "katex/contrib/mhchem"` to `quartz/plugins/transformers/latex.ts`, in the ALWAYS section so course folders built earlier pick it up on their next build. Verified on a real build of the SCH3U example course: `$$\ce{CH4(g) + 2O2(g) -> CO2(g) + 2H2O(g)}$$` renders with upright symbols, true subscripts and a drawn reaction arrow, `\ce{CaCO3(s) <=> CaO(s) + CO2(g)}` gives a real equilibrium arrow, and the whole built site has zero `katex-error` spans. The three chemistry payloads (SNC2D, SCH3U, SCH4U) carried a teacher warning saying mhchem was NOT here and failed silently — the opposite of the truth once the import landed. They were then CONVERTED outright, because these pages teach by example and a teacher meeting `\ce{}` for the first time should meet it in use: **1,533 maths spans across 110 files** now write chemistry as `\ce{}` and nothing carries the `\text{}` longhand, including the two `[\ce{H+}]` spans in MHF4U's curriculum. The conversion was mechanical but gated — every rewritten span was rendered BOTH ways through the build's own KaTeX and compared character by character, so the only differences that survived review were the intended ones (a bond dash where `--` had been setting an en dash). Units were the risk and were left alone: `1.10\ \text{V}` is volts, `T(\text{K})` is kelvin, `^\circ\text{C}` is Celsius. A real build of SCH4U emits 278 pages with zero `katex-error` spans. The example-content skill's chemistry rule now requires `\ce{}` and forbids hand-built formulae. The teacher callout that compared the two forms was then dropped entirely — a teacher does not care which form was used, only how mhchem works — and replaced with a **mini-tutorial**: a three-column table (what you type / what appears / what it means) covering subscripts, coefficients, charges, brackets, states, both arrows, hydrates, bonds, electrons, precipitates, and a condition set above the arrow, tailored per course. Every row was rendered through a real build to confirm it does what the row claims. | Shared Python, so Windows gets it for free. |
| 121 | 2026-08-13 | "What This Site Can Do" — the page that shows a teacher what the tool actually does — was buried in the Style folder, and in two courses it was not in Key Links at all. | ✅ It is now the LAST entry of `Key Links` in all eighteen payloads (first entry stays Curriculum Expectations), so a teacher evaluating the software meets the tour from the sidebar rather than hunting for it. Verified on a real build: the page's link list ends with it. | Content and lint only — nothing for Windows to port. |

All 18 payloads sweep clean through the installer; 248 app tests pass, including two new ones. | Windows: its Add Section equivalent needs the same pass over the course-level pages — extend each page that already uses the per-section form with a pair for the new section, copy the lowest-numbered section's draft state, stamp a fresh date, and leave plain-`created:` pages untouched. Cells addressed by a page in `Tasks` carry a ring, and each strand's overall expectations appear as chips — green when assessed work reaches them, red when nothing marked does — which is the second Ontario rule answered on the same page. Hovering a cell gives Quartz's own page preview: the generated anchors carry `class="internal"`, and Quartz's link processor adopts them exactly as if they had been written as wikilinks. **What counts is deliberately narrow**: a page covers an expectation when it TRANSCLUDES it, which is the form a curriculum connection takes. The first implementation keyed on the `%%curriculum-start%%` markers and reported 57 of 57 expectations uncovered — the installer strips those markers when it installs a course, so they never reach a built site. **Only published pages count**, added after the first build shipped: a page still marked `draft: true` is not on the site, so it cannot have addressed anything — next week's lesson, written early, leaves the map exactly where it was until the day it is published. | 131 | 2026-08-14 | On the Curriculum Coverage page, the hover preview of an expectation slides UNDER the left-hand navigation — the preview is unreadable exactly where it overlaps the sidebar. Is that a z-index problem? | ✅ It was, and the cause was ours. Quartz builds a hover preview by appending it INSIDE the link you are hovering, so the preview's own `z-index: 999` only ranks it against its siblings within that link. Our coverage styles brighten a hovered cell or chip with a CSS `filter`, and a filter makes the element a stacking context — which sealed the preview inside the cell, at whatever depth the cell sits, while the sidebar carries `z-index: 1` and painted over it. Fix: every cell and chip is now `position: relative` at rest and lifts to `z-index: 1000` while hovered, so the element carrying the preview outranks the sidebar and takes the preview up with it. Proven in a real browser rather than by reading the CSS: a headless Chrome harness hovers a cell in a built course, waits for the preview, and asks `document.elementFromPoint` which element wins at a spot where the preview overlaps the sidebar — with the rules removed it answers the sidebar's folder list, with them present it answers the preview. (That harness needs a host that resolves extensionless URLs the way a real one does; a plain static server 404s the preview's fetch and no preview is ever created, which reads as "fine" while testing nothing.) | Shared Python — the coverage stylesheet is written by the toolchain, so Windows gets the fix with no separate work. Nothing in the GUI to mirror. |

| 132 | 2026-08-14 | Hovering a cell on the coverage map changes the LABEL's colour, and against some cell colours that label is then hard to read. Brighten the cell's background a little instead.
