The label colour is now pinned to whatever it was at rest (an `!important` is the only thing that answers theirs), and the hover shows itself by lightening the BACKGROUND: a translucent white wash that composites over whichever level colour the cell carries, so it needs no per-level rules. The wash strength was measured, not guessed — lightening the field a white label sits on COSTS that label contrast, so the first attempt at 22% took the two mid-ramp colours below 3:1; at 12% the change is plain on the dark cells, where most of a healthy map lives, and the worst-off label gives up about half a point. **The panel:** map, rule, and legend now sit together in one rounded plate drawn in the theme's own light grey — a light grey in light schemes, a dark grey in dark ones, so it looks native everywhere instead of introducing a second palette. Every cell and legend key now also carries an inset hairline in the theme's mid grey — mid in BOTH light and dark — which holds the pale end and the dark end away from any ground. Checked by screenshot in both themes and by a contrast sweep over all five levels, hovered and at rest. | Shared Python — the coverage stylesheet and page are written by the toolchain, so Windows gets all of it with no separate work. Nothing in the GUI to mirror. |

Windows inherits the palette with no separate work. |

| 135 | 2026-08-14 | The deepened top step reads as black, not as a green. Use a navy blue. | ✅ "Addressed four or more times" is now navy `#1e3a8a` instead of `#052e16`. The top step leaves the ramp's hue on purpose, and that turns out to be the fix for a problem the deepening had only papered over: with two greens at the top, the pair could be told apart ONLY by how dark they were, and a green dark enough to be distinct was a green indistinguishable from black. Navy separates by hue, which survives being dark — it holds its own on a dark scheme (1.76:1 against the panel, against 1.26 for the near-black green), keeps white text well clear at **10.36:1**, and is distinguishable from the greens for a red-green colour-blind reader, while still reading as "past the end of the scale" rather than as another grade of green. Checked by screenshot in both themes and by a contrast sweep of all five levels. | Shared Python. Windows inherits the palette with no separate work. |

| 136 | 2026-08-14 | "Not yet addressed" and "addressed once" are practically the same colour. Rethink the whole scale, keeping the same core colours, and make sure every step is far enough from every other. | ✅ Rethought by measurement rather than by eye, because eye-picking is what produced the problem: the two reds measured **ΔE 10.2** apart (CIEDE2000 — RGB distance does not match what an eye reports), and anything under about 10 reads as one colour. Every combination of candidate shades in the five families was scored on the distance between EVERY pair, rejected unless each label cleared 4.5:1 against its own fill, and scored AGAIN through simulations of deuteranopia and protanopia — colour is the only visual carrier of the count here, so a scale that separates for most readers and collapses for the rest has not solved the problem. **The result is not the obvious answer, and the search is what showed why:** a mid-tone red fails BOTH labels — too dark for white, too light for dark ink — so red and orange cannot both be mid-tone, and the only way to hold them apart is to separate them by lightness as well as hue. Hence a deep red carrying white (`#7f1d1d`, 10.02:1) and a bright orange carrying dark ink (`#f97316`, 5.24:1), then `#facc15`, `#15803d`, `#1e3a8a`. Closest pair any ordinary-sighted reader now sees: **ΔE 31.4**, against 10.2 before; for a colour-blind reader, ΔE 12.4 and 11.1, against 6.0 and 8.9 before. The fill and its label are now defined together in one `COVERAGE_LEVELS` list that generates both the resting rule and the hover pin, so a fill can never end up wearing a label chosen for a different fill, and the overall-expectation chips take their red and green from the same list so the page never shows two reds. The search itself is committed as `scripts/choose_coverage_scale.py` — a design-time tool, not part of the build and not in the image — so the next person to question a colour can re-run the reasoning instead of re-guessing. Verified by rebuild, a contrast sweep of all five levels hovered and at rest, and screenshots in both themes. | Shared Python. Windows inherits the palette with no separate work. |

| 137 | 2026-08-14 | Career Studies and Civics are HALF credits — 55 hours each — and a student takes them back to back: Career Studies from September, then Civics picks up where it ends. Neither the linter nor the installer could express that. | ✅ Both now read it from the manifest, so a half credit is a declared property of a payload rather than a special case in the code. **The linter** had 110 hours, a 104–116 window, and three review classes hard-coded; it now reads `credit_value` (default 1.0) and `final_evaluation_hours` (default 3) and scales — a 0.5-credit payload is checked against 55 hours with a proportional tolerance and two review classes, and its error message names the credit it was checked against. **The installer** anchored every class page to September 8, which is right for the first course of a pair and wrong for the second: both would have been dated onto the same mornings. It now reads `class_start_school_day` (default 1) and moves the anchor that many school days later, counting school days rather than calendar days so the winter break and Thanksgiving still fall out correctly. Proven by measurement rather than by reading the diff: a full credit still runs 8 September to 19 January unchanged; a 42-period half credit runs 8 September to 5 November; and the same payload declaring `class_start_school_day: 43` runs 6 November to 15 January — the school day after the first course's last class, with no overlap. Regression-checked against all five existing payloads (still clean) and the installer end-to-end. **Correction, same day:** the first cut of this threaded `start_school_day` through every function but failed to PASS it at one of the two `install_payload_file` call sites — the one that installs per-section pages, which is where class pages live. The date arithmetic was proven at the function level and passed, so the gap only appeared when a real payload was installed and Civics came out dated to September alongside Career Studies. Proof now runs at the level the feature is used: installing GLC2O gives 8 Sep to 5 Nov, installing CHV2O gives 6 Nov to 15 Jan, and a full credit still gives 8 Sep to 19 Jan. | Shared Python, so Windows inherits both. Nothing in the GUI to mirror — but if the Windows New Course dialog ever exposes course length, it must write `credit_value`, and a teacher pairing two half credits needs somewhere to say which one runs second. |

| 138 | 2026-08-14 | Two courses shipped with a Curriculum Expectations link in the sidebar that led to a page nobody had written. Find out how the linter missed it, and whether anything else is broken the same way. | ✅ The linter had a dead-link check the whole time; it had two holes, and both are now closed. **It compared only the last path segment**, so `[[Curriculum/index]]` matched against any folder's `index.md` anywhere in the payload — which is exactly why CHA3U and THJ2O passed while pointing at nothing. Folder-qualified links now have their whole path checked. **It scanned line by line**, so a wikilink split by the 80-column wrap was never matched at all: the check most likely to catch a dead link was structurally blind to the commonest way of making one. Wrapped links are now found by a separate pass over the whole page. The second hole was the expensive one — **nine dead links across six already-released payloads** (BOH4M, CGC1W, ENL1W ×2, MCV4U, MPM2D ×2, MTH1W, TEJ4M, TGJ2O), every one a piped link where the wrap happened to fall between the target and its alias, so the prose read perfectly and the link led nowhere. All nine rewrapped. Worth recording why they were dead: Quartz's wikilink regex is `[^\[\]\|\#\\]+`, which does NOT exclude a newline, so it matches the wrapped link happily and builds a target with a newline inside it — no page has that name. Checked against the real regex in the tree rather than assumed. **Four payloads (CGF3M, CHA3U, CIA4U, THJ2O) reproduced verbatim Ministry expectation wording with no `About These Expectations` page at all** — no citation, no source URL, no copyright line — where the other 33 had one; written, with each document title, year, © holder and page range verified against the PDF, and an honest "there is no link to give you" where the source genuinely no longer exists rather than a plausible-looking URL. That sweep found more: **three payloads cited `dcp.edu.gov.on.ca` course pages that do not exist** (TEJ2O and TGJ2O, replaced with an honest statement; the 2009 Technological Education courses are simply not on that site), and **two used a course slug the Ministry's CMS does not have** (BOH4M and MPM2D needed `-pdf`). These looked live because dcp is an Angular app that answers 200 for any path — the CMS Delivery API is the only thing that actually tells you, and every dcp link in every payload was checked against it. All 37 payloads lint clean. | Shared Python — the linter is authoring-time tooling and the payloads are shared content, so Windows inherits every fix with no separate work. Nothing in the GUI to mirror. |

| 139 | 2026-08-12 | Teachers asked (back in the CLI era) for somewhere other than Netlify to publish. Add a third destination that is genuinely free at a teacher's scale, with no per-file surprises for ordinary course material. | ⬜ **Not yet on macOS** — this row runs the OTHER way: implemented on **Windows first**, and the mac side owes the launcher and GUI. Full brief with every design decision is in [`MAC-HANDOFF.md`](MAC-HANDOFF.md). The shared half is already done and the mac inherits it: `scripts/deploy.py --target cloudflare` discovers or accepts the account, creates or reuses this section's Pages project (marker `courses/<CODE>/.cloudflare_sites/section<N>.json`, mirroring the Netlify one), hands the built folder to wrangler, and prints the `Live URL:` label both apps' parsers already read — so no parser change was needed on either side. | Windows (done, `0306c98`/`e6611cc`/`8883ad9`): the Publishing picker offers Netlify, Cloudflare Pages, or a folder, in both Course Settings and the wizard. Choosing Cloudflare reveals a **Cloudflare Account ID** field with live validation (32 hex characters) gating Save/Create — asked for in the UI, not by the launcher's console prompt, because a token scoped to Pages **cannot list its own account** (verified against a real token: `/user/tokens/verify` says active while `/accounts` returns an empty list) and the app has nothing attached that could answer a prompt. The account lives in app settings, not course settings — it identifies the teacher, like the token. A permanent orange note states the one real difference between destinations: Cloudflare refuses any single file over 25 MB, documents and slide decks are comfortably under, long video is not, and embedding from YouTube or Vimeo avoids it; `deploy.py` also checks sizes BEFORE uploading and names the offending files. Milestones never say "Netlify" (pinned by a test). Verified end to end: a real section published to `<project>.pages.dev`, second publish reused the project in 23 s. Cloudflare's 500-builds/month limit does not apply — a Direct Upload reports `build=idle`, no build runs. |
| 140 | 2026-08-12 | The frontmatter flag that decides whether students see a page was `draft:`, with `draftSection<N>:` for shared pages. Make the flag `publish:` / `publishForSection<N>:`, and keep it true that a page with no flag at all stays visible. | ⬜ **Not yet on macOS** — Windows-first again, and almost all of it is shared. The mac app owes only the reading and writing of the new key names; the toolchain half is done and inherited: `build_site.py` maps `publishForSection<N>` → `publish` for the section being built, `patches/publish.ts` gives Quartz a `PublishFlag` filter, and `setup_course.py` creates new courses in the new schema. | Windows (done): shared toolchain plus the app's `PageFrontmatter`, which reads the new keys first, falls back to the legacy pair **inverted**, and writes the new key **in the old key's position** so a migrated page shows a one-line diff rather than reordered frontmatter. Quartz needed a patch, but not the obvious one: v4.5.0 already ships `ExplicitPublish`, which reads `publish === true` — and it flips the default, so the 60 of the sample course's 225 pages carrying no flag (every curriculum page among them) would have silently vanished. `PublishFlag` is eight lines that keep the forgiving default and change only the word. No flag day: legacy courses build exactly as before, and a page migrates the first time something edits it. The example content was inverted wholesale (1145 keys across 957 files, a pure one-line-per-key rename) along with the prose that teaches the flag. Three inversion bugs surfaced during the swap — a plan that thought published pages still needed publishing, a dangling-link check that found nothing in either direction, and a transition line that told the teacher the exact reverse of the truth — every one caught by a test that already existed. | 
| 141 | 2026-08-14 | Windows renamed the visibility flag from `draft:` to `publish:` (entry 140), but only the Windows side and the build were converted. Finish it: the payloads, and everything that reads or writes the flag. | ✅ Converted, and the conversion is what found the bugs. **Content:** 5,968 payload pages, 108 EXC2O course-level pages, and 1,944 skeleton pages, plus the skeleton GENERATOR so a regeneration cannot put the old key back — regenerated and diffed to prove it. The polarity is inverted, which is the whole risk of this change: `draft: true` means the page is HIDDEN and `publish: true` means it is SHOWN, so a careless one-word swap publishes 192 pages a teacher meant to keep private. Every flip was scripted and counted, and the built site checked afterwards. 144 pages of PROSE describing the flag were rewritten too, keeping the ~150 ordinary English uses of "draft" — "Report drafted; due next class", a `Pitch → Report → Draft → Edit → Publish` diagram, and the curriculum's own "produce revised drafts of texts" — which a blind find-and-replace would have mangled. **Three real defects came out of it, all invisible until something was actually installed:** `per_section_frontmatter` split only `created`/`draft`, so 493 shared pages carrying `publish:` were left UNSPLIT — two sections would have shared one publishing state, which is the exact thing that function exists to prevent. The coverage map's own `_is_draft()` knew only the old key, so a page held back with `publish: false` counted as published and could turn the map green before the class had happened. And the macOS app's `SectionAdder` copied `draftSectionN` when adding a section: on a course installed from a migrated payload it would have found nothing and published a page the teacher had held back. All three now read either key, with the legacy one inverted on the way through, and a test (`testALegacyDraftSectionKeyIsReadAndInverted`) pins the inversion so it cannot regress. The payload linter now REJECTS `draft:` outright, so a payload cannot drift back to a key whose polarity is the opposite of what it looks like. Verified end to end rather than by diff: 37/37 payloads and 50/50 skeletons lint clean, verify.sh green, the mac app's full suite passes, and CIA4U installed and rebuilt to the SAME coverage map as before the change — 64 codes, identical level distribution, 15/15 chips, 85 class pages published, the held-back final class still absent, no broken links. | Shared Python plus the macOS app. Windows already had its half (entry 140); the payloads, skeletons, generator, splitter, and coverage map are shared, so Windows inherits all of it. The one piece with a Windows counterpart is `SectionAdder` — if the Windows app grows an add-section feature it needs the same read-both-keys-and-invert rule, or it will publish pages a teacher held back. |

| 142 | 2026-08-15 | Colima is pinned at 2 CPUs / 4 GB in every launcher — a figure chosen for an 8 GB machine and then applied to every machine. Size it to the Mac it is running on. | ✅ `_colima_cpus` takes half the cores (floor 2, cap 6) and `_colima_memory_gb` a third of the RAM (floor 4 GB, cap 12 GB), so an 8 GB laptop gets exactly what it gets today and a 48 GB desktop gets 6 CPUs and 12 GB. Deliberately NOT the whole machine: the teacher is using the Mac while the site builds. The harder half was the VM that already exists, because Colima is shared infrastructure — another toolchain may have built it, and this Mac's was already 6 CPUs / 8 GB rather than the 2/4 the launchers create. So `_colima_growth_flags` only ever asks for MORE, and only when the VM is STOPPED: a VM somebody else sized up keeps its size, a VM already big enough is left completely alone, and resizing never takes down containers another tool is using, because resizing recreates the VM. Proved against four VM shapes with a stubbed `colima`: 2/4 upgrades to 6/12, this Mac's real 6/8 upgrades memory only, a deliberately larger 10/32 is untouched, and one already at 6/12 is untouched. verify.sh green. | Shared shell, but the mechanism is macOS-only — Windows uses WSL2, which sizes itself from the host already. Nothing to port; the `documentation/03-launcher-scripts.md` passage describing the old fixed default has been rewritten. |

A PAGE is published (the `publish:` frontmatter flag, which the assistant can change); the SITE is deployed to Netlify, Cloudflare, or a folder (the teacher's own act, which the assistant never takes). One word for both makes "I published tomorrow's class" mean a flag to one person and a live site to another. 24 strings across 14 files: the toolbar button and tooltip, both No-Preview invitations, the progress title, the settings group (now "Deploying") and its "Deploy to" picker, the folder problem messages, the busy lines, the script phase markers, and the folder-copy completion note. | 144 | 2026-08-15 | Build the local assistant for macOS, and see whether the Windows side's three-minute wait to get ready can be cut. Use whatever approach suits a Mac; different models on different Macs are fine, but do not hog the machine. | ✅ Built, and the three minutes became **two seconds** — because the wait was never about the model. **Windows runs its model in Docker-in-WSL2, which gets 2 cores and no GPU; the Mac equivalent would be Colima, a Linux VM with no access to Metal.** Same model file, byte for byte, same 3,411-token tool surface: **175 s to read the prompt in a container, 2.1 s natively; generation 5.5 tok/s against 158.** So `llama-server` ships in the app (25 MB, MIT, fetched by `mac-app/Vendor/fetch-llama.sh` rather than committed) and runs as an ordinary child process with `--n-gpu-layers 999`. Two consequences: their disk-cache save/restore mechanism — "the biggest win available" at 163 s a session — is simply **not needed** and was not ported, and the one-off prompt read is warmed in the background as the window opens, so a teacher never waits for it. **Different Macs get different models, and the ladder was decided by measurement rather than by size.** Under 16 GB gets Qwen2.5 1.5B (1.1 GB, ~0.3 s a reply); 16 GB and up gets 7B (4.7 GB, ~1.2 s). **There is deliberately no 3B rung** even though it is the obvious middle step: measured, it INVERTS POLARITY — asked to hide a page it called publish, in two of three trials, and answered three hide requests with undo — while also scoring BELOW the 1.5B (70% against 81%). That is the exact failure the separate publish/unpublish verbs exist to prevent, so the case was deleted from the enum rather than marked risky, on the same reasoning as having no delete tool: what cannot be selected cannot be selected by accident. The 7B routes at **94%, matching the Windows figure**, with no inversions. Not hogging the machine is enforced rather than intended: threads are capped at half the performance cores (never more than six, because generation is bandwidth-bound long before it is thread-bound), the model is resident only while the window is open, and a test asserts no tier ever takes more than a third of memory. The window is per section so the preview stays visible beside it; only DEPLOYS wait for a button, because publishing is backed up and undoable and a gate in front of every write teaches teachers to click through gates. **Still to do:** serving the same tools over stdio so Claude Code drives them, which is what keeps the two clients' safety rules from drifting. | Windows should try this too — llama.cpp publishes native Windows builds (CUDA, Vulkan, CPU) in the same release, and the rest of the design does not care where the server runs, since it is the same OpenAI-shaped HTTP endpoint either way. Written up in WINDOWS-HANDOFF with the numbers. Also recorded there: their "date appended, not prepended" finding REPRODUCED natively (13–14 points, against their 15), while "rewrite the schema examples to the real course" did NOT — 0 copies in 87 responses where they saw 9 of 9. That is grounds to re-test on the shipped model, not to remove it, and macOS keeps the rewriting. |

| 145 | 2026-08-15 | The rest of the Windows parity list: a section remembering when its classes meet, placeholder classes, inserting a class and pushing the rest back, curriculum expectations for a page, scheduled deploys, and Cloudflare Pages. | ✅ All six. **Timetable memory** is the shared `.internal/timetable/section<N>.json` — read and written as a FORMAT rather than shared code, under `.internal/` so it travels through backup, archive and restore. A partial list is refused whole and no file is written: a half-remembered timetable gets trusted and then dates the wrong classes. **Class insertion** renames highest-day-first, and the test earns that by simulating the WRONG order — Day 2 → Day 3 is refused because a real Day 3 still holds the name, leaving a hole, a mislabelled lesson and a silently skipped class. Links are rewritten by us, since a rename on disk reads to Obsidian as a delete plus a create; all five wikilink forms are handled and the Markdown-link limitation is written down rather than left to be discovered. **Curriculum mentions** put transclusions INSIDE the `%%curriculum-start%%` markers, or a course installed without curriculum would keep a dangling reference on a live site; the tools read out the full wording and the MODEL decides which fit, because that is a judgement about meaning. Those three tools go to the MCP surface ONLY — the local model still sees exactly fifteen, because fifteen is what its routing was measured against, and a test pins the count. *(Superseded by row 154: the local surface is now thirteen, and the pinned count moved with it. The principle — MCP may grow, the local surface only changes with a measurement — is unchanged.)* **Scheduled deploys** are a launchd agent labelled per course AND section, proved by round-tripping the plist through `PropertyListSerialization` rather than by scheduling anything; `launchctl` sits behind a fake in tests. **Cloudflare** needed more than the UI: `deploy.sh` had no Cloudflare support at all — Windows had `deploy.ps1`, the mac launcher did not — so the UI would have shipped dead. And a scheduled deploy sends the last BUILT site on both sides; whether it should build first is an open product question, flagged rather than decided. |

| 146 | 2026-08-15 | A scheduled deploy sent the last BUILT site. It should build when the alarm fires and then deploy — what the Deploy button does if you were sitting there and pressed it. And it should skip the build when nothing has changed, rather than burning a container start on identical bytes. | ✅ Both. The button's sequence is `BuildFreshness.needsRebuild` → `preview.sh --build-only` → `deploy.sh`, and the launchd agent ran only the last of those. That was not a small gap: **`deploy.sh` never builds** — it refuses outright when there is no built site — so a scheduled deploy either failed at half six or sent whatever was last previewed. The agent now runs the same three steps, with the staleness test written out in shell because the app is closed when the agent fires and cannot be asked. All THREE parts of the Swift rule are carried across, and the middle one matters most: a **preview's** build is never deploy-fresh however recent it looks, because serve mode bakes a `ws://localhost` live-reload client into every page and deploying that makes a visitor's browser knock on their own machine (row 108). The other two are "no built page at all" and "content newer than the built page", the latter skipping hidden entries — without that guard `.merged_output` is itself newer than the page inside it, so the site would look stale the instant it was built and rebuild every single time. Proved by running the shell rule against real files across six cases: no build, unchanged, content edited, rebuilt after the edit, only a hidden folder touched, and a preview build. The deploy is gated on the build having succeeded, mirroring the button returning early rather than sending the previous one; cleanup runs either way, so a failed build still leaves nothing pending and the agent does not fire again tomorrow. The plan's wording said the opposite of all this and was rewritten to match. 372 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows schedules with `schtasks` and has the same shape of gap worth checking: if its task runs `deploy.ps1` alone, it deploys a stale build — and if it ever gains a build step, it needs the ws://localhost check too, or a scheduled deploy can put a live-reload client on the live site. Two differences already noted: launchd runs a MISSED job at the next wake, which schtasks may not, and Cloudflare IS schedulable on the mac because the plist carries `--account`. |

| 147 | 2026-08-15 | Test the models properly — there is a big jump between 1.5B and 7B, and the 3B was vetoed on only three trials per probe. | ✅ Re-run at **10 trials** (29 probes × 10 = 290 per model), same tool surface throughout, and it changed the answer. **The 3B veto holds and is not a small-sample artefact: 9 inversions in 10.** Llama-3.2-3B, a different family entirely, inverted **10 in 10 — on the same sentence** ("Hide tomorrow's class again… Ohm's Law"), which says something about that phrasing rather than about one model. Phi-4-mini and Gemma-2-2b are DISQUALIFIED and the reason was diagnosed rather than guessed: neither chat template has a branch for a top-level `tools` array, so llama.cpp silently drops it — 30-token prompts instead of 3,417 — and they cannot tool-call at all. **The find is Qwen3 4B: 100% on the Windows-comparable set, zero inversions, 0.74 s warm turn, 2.5 GB download.** It beats the 7B on accuracy, latency, memory AND download, so the 7B is no longer shipped — a 4B outscoring a 7B is not what anyone would have predicted from the sizes. **The trap is that this is one flag deep.** With thinking left ON, the same weights score **39%**: the 256-token budget is eaten inside `<think>` and 196 of 290 turns never reach a tool call at all. `--reasoning-budget 0` is now passed at startup, the arguments were pulled into a testable function to make that assertable, and a test fails if the flag ever goes missing — because losing it makes the assistant quietly worse rather than visibly broken. Resident memory turned out to be mostly KV cache and therefore adjustable: 5.04 GB at 16k, 3.87 GB at 8k, 3.22 GB at 4k, with routing bit-for-bit identical at all three. **8 GB Macs stay on the 1.5B, and that is a hold rather than a result** — Qwen3 4B at 8k would be a 21-point gain on exactly the machines with the worst model today, but 3.87 GB is 48% of an 8 GB Mac that is also running Colima at 4 GB, and the assistant can start a build itself so the two peak together. Every measurement here is from a 48 GB M4 Pro; that rung wants a real 8 GB machine before it moves. Qwen3-1.7B was measured and rejected for a subtler reason: no inversions, but it filled in TODAY's date on 20 of 40 "tomorrow" calls — the wrong day is still the wrong page in front of students. 376 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows should re-measure on the same footing before assuming its 1.5B is the right choice — the whole comparison is in `research/ai-assist/macos-native-10-trial-comparison.txt`, and the suite is the one already in `research/ai-assist/`. If Windows adopts Qwen3, the reasoning flag matters there just as much: the per-request equivalent is `chat_template_kwargs {"enable_thinking": false}`. |

| 148 | 2026-08-15 | The local model is a router and routers are sometimes wrong. Show the teacher what it understood before it acts — and stop backing up on every single command, which is wasteful on a vault full of images. | ✅ **Plan mode**, and the backup moved to once per conversation. A write now runs its `plan_` twin first, and the teacher sees the plan in the twin's own words with **Go** and **Cancel**; the model is not consulted twice, so what runs on Go is exactly what was described. **Writes only** — reads answer immediately, because gating "what do students see right now?" makes every question two clicks and teaches people to press Go without reading, which costs the gate its whole value. **Always on for the small model and not turnable off**: 79% routing is not a rate at which anyone should be handed a "stop asking" button, and a remembered "off" from a capable Mac is IGNORED on an 8 GB one so the setting cannot leak across machines. On the capable model it is offered off **after five accepted in a row, once**, while getting it right five times running is still fresh rather than months later in a settings pane; a **Cancel resets the run**, because somebody who has just stopped the assistant doing the wrong thing must not then be asked whether it should stop asking. "Keep checking" is the default button, so a reflexive Return keeps the safer arrangement. Deploys still always ask. **Two premises behind this turned out to be false and were worth checking:** undo is NOT git-backed — courses are not git repositories at all — it is in-memory before/after snapshots that die with the window, and it deliberately SKIPS files edited in Obsidian since, so it is a weaker net than it sounds; and the app was not backing up once per conversation but **before every write**. Both corrected: one lazy backup per conversation (a read-only chat makes none), and that zip is what Restore uses. Backups now carry provenance in the name (`_assistant-section<N>`) so a teacher choosing among several can see what made each and why, five are kept per course, and pruning touches only backups — archives and the wizard's zips share the folder and their parsers reject each other's forms. **A test caught a real bug in this work**: `planTwinName` claimed a twin for every write including four that have none, so plan mode would have asked for `plan_rebuild_preview`, been told there is no such tool, and shown the teacher an error where their plan should be. The agent now asks the surface whether the twin exists. 392 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows should match all of it — written up in WINDOWS-HANDOFF under "Plan mode, undo, and how often to back up", including the reasoning for writes-only, the five-in-a-row offer, the Cancel reset, and why the backup is per conversation rather than per command. Also corrected there: that file still told Windows the visible verb is "Publish, never Deploy", which rows 140 and 143 reversed. |

| 149 | 2026-08-15 | Automatic pruning should only touch backups the assistant made. | ✅ Narrowed, and the distinction is the point. A teacher's backup is a DECISION — they pressed Back Up because they were about to do something they were unsure of, or the app saved one on their behalf before adding a section — and deleting that on a schedule they never agreed to is the app overruling them about their own work. The assistant's are different in kind: it saves one per conversation whether or not anybody asked, so clearing up after itself is its job. The two are now counted separately, which matters: a teacher with twenty of their own keeps all twenty, and they never crowd out the assistant's five. The list's own wording followed the rule rather than lagging behind it — each row now says what happens to THAT copy ("You made this one, so it is kept until you delete it" against "The assistant made this one; its five most recent are kept"), because a teacher deciding whether to keep their own copy needs their answer, not the assistant's. Two tests pin it: twelve teacher backups all survive a prune, and with seven of each interleaved the assistant loses its two oldest while every one of the teacher's survives. Archives and the wizard's zips are still untouched. 401 unit tests, 0 failures. | Written into WINDOWS-HANDOFF alongside the per-conversation backup rule. The trap to avoid is counting both kinds together — that quietly deletes a teacher's oldest backup as soon as the assistant has made five of its own. |

| 150 | 2026-08-15 | The local assistant should run one at a time on a machine — no working with the assistant on another section until the open one is closed, with the other menu entries dimmed. | ✅ Enforced app-wide by `AssistActivity`, and the reason is worth recording: the DOWNLOAD is shared (one file per Mac in Application Support, whatever course or section, surviving app updates) but the RUNNING copy is not. Each window starts its own engine and loads the weights again, so two windows is twice the resident memory — on a 16 GB Mac that is most of the machine, and it undoes the point of sizing the model to the hardware in the first place. It also removes a subtler cost: two conversations about two sections would evict each other's prompt cache on every alternating turn, since their prefixes differ from the first sentence, so both would feel slower than either alone. The menu item for any other section is dimmed with a line naming what to close — "Close the assistant for ICS3U Section 1 first" — because dimmed alone says "no" while naming the holder says what to do about it, the same shape "Add Section…" uses when a course is busy. Three details that are easy to get wrong and are pinned by tests: the claim is taken when the WINDOW opens rather than when the engine is ready (a teacher three minutes into a download has it open as far as they are concerned); it is released unconditionally on close, and a newer claim replaces a stale one, because a leaked claim locks the feature out until the app restarts — a worse failure than briefly allowing two; and the section that already HOLDS it stays enabled, since choosing it just brings that window forward. The registry is read during the row's render, not in the button's closure, or the menu shows the answer from whenever the row last drew. Governs the built-in assistant only — Claude Code over MCP has no engine of its own. 409 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows needs the same rule for the same reason, and it applies whatever engine they end up running: the constraint is resident memory per window, not anything macOS-specific. Written up in WINDOWS-HANDOFF under "One assistant at a time, machine-wide", including the claim-on-open and release-unconditionally details and the render-time staleness trap their own `CourseActivity` equivalent already documents. |

| 151 | 2026-08-15 | The context-menu label should read "Revise with local AI assistant…" rather than "Revise with AI…". | ✅ Changed. "AI" alone invites exactly the opposite assumption, and a teacher weighing whether to point it at student work should not have to open the window to find out. Title case and the ellipsis CHARACTER (…) rather than three periods, matching every other menu item and the macOS convention that a trailing ellipsis promises a further step before anything happens. 409 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows should use the same words for the same reason. | 152 | 2026-08-15 | The assistant's suggestions should fold under a disclosure group per kind of request, and be pinned to the TOP of the window with the chat scrolling beneath. It is now pinned at the top, above the transcript, four disclosure groups **shut by default**: open, the eleven suggestions are taller than the conversation deserves to give up, so it costs four scannable lines until asked for more, and past 240 points it scrolls, meaning a teacher who opens every group gives up their own scroll position rather than the conversation. **A `ScrollView` takes every point it is offered**, so capping it with `maxHeight` alone left a stripe of dead space under the closed groups — the shelf measures its content with a preference key and asks for the SMALLER of that and the cap, which is what makes four closed lines occupy four lines. The open groups are remembered in **`@AppStorage`, deliberately not `@SceneStorage`** — scene storage is per WINDOW, so it would restore this window when it came back while a teacher opening the assistant on a different section met everything shut again, which is the opposite of remembering. It is a preference about how they like the shelf, not a fact about one window; this app also already carries a note about scene storage sharing one value across a window group and losing to the last writer. Stored as one `\|`-separated string since AppStorage holds no sets, with a test that no group title contains the separator. The suggestions themselves are unchanged, and that matters: the routing suite probes them word for word, and several are matched in code rather than routed, so what the shelf promises is what the window delivers. 412 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows needs the same placement and the same persistence scope. Two traps: a per-window store looks correct until somebody opens the assistant on a second section, and a scrolling container that fills its offered space will leave the same dead stripe under the collapsed groups. If its own suggestion list ever diverges from these eleven, the routing suite has to be re-run against the new wording, because the phrasings are measured rather than chosen. |
| 153 | 2026-08-15 | (Found while re-measuring, not asked for.) The assistant was thinking on every single turn — the flag believed to stop it only capped it. | ✅ Fixed, and the fix is one word. `llama-server` has two separate settings: `--reasoning [on\|off\|auto]` decides whether the chat template opens a thinking block at all, and `--reasoning-budget N` decides how long it may run once opened. The app shipped for several days passing `--reasoning-budget 0` alone, on the reasonable-sounding belief that a budget of zero meant no thinking. It does not: Qwen3's template still opened `<think>` on every turn and filled it to the cap. Measured on one prompt, same weights, same surface, same temperature — **`--reasoning-budget 0`: 16.1 s and 512 completion tokens; `--reasoning off`: 8.4 s and 44 tokens; both together: 8.4 s and 44 tokens.** The useful reply is 44 tokens and the other 468 were thinking nobody sees, so this is **half the assistant's response time for a flag**. Both are now passed and `testThinkingIsTurnedOff` asserts both for every tier; the budget stays as insurance against a future model whose template ignores the switch, which is exactly how this shipped in the first place. **Why it survived review is the transferable part:** the wrong flag does not produce a wrong answer, only a slow one, and both obvious checks come back green — a tool call *does* arrive (512 tokens is enough to think AND still route on a short prompt), and there is no `<think>` in `message.content` because llama.cpp parses thinking out into `reasoning_content` by default, so the tag's absence proves the parser ran rather than that the model stayed quiet. The honest signal is the completion-token count and the clock. Note also that the routing suite had always run with `--reasoning off`, so the measured 93–97% never described the shipping app until now. Full record in `research/ai-assist/reasoning-flag-measurement.txt`; 455 unit tests, 0 failures. | **Check your launcher's flags before reading further — this may be part of the 3-minute wait.** If it passes the budget alone it has the identical fault. Two caveats: `--reasoning` is NEWER than `--reasoning-budget`, so confirm it exists in your build's `--help` before adding it (ours is llama.cpp b10435); and verify against your own engine rather than trusting our table, because the behaviour belongs to the model's chat template, not to the model. The measurement to run is completion tokens and wall-clock on a single prompt, NOT whether a tool call came back. |

The local surface fell from ~3,900 to **~2,230 prompt tokens**. `includeLinked` is gone; **no tool on either surface now takes a boolean at all**. Unpublishing is worked out to a FIXED POINT rather than in one pass — when a page joins the ones coming down, the pages only IT linked to become free to follow, so a chain A→B→C completes instead of stopping half-published — and the pages left behind are reported with their reason, named as the teacher would see them ("Ohm's Law — "Unit 3, Day 2" still links to it"). The three exclusions are checked BEFORE the reference rule and apply to the sweep only: a page the teacher names by hand still comes down. Re-measured against the shipping 13-tool surface, 42 probes × 10 trials: **the 4B routes 110/110 of the window's own suggestions, 94% of the like-for-like Windows probe set, 0 polarity inversions, 0 malformed calls, 0 invented dates, median warm reply 0.53 s**; the 1.5B manages 77% and 67% with 0 inversions. Cutting the surface cost nothing and bought latency. One real miss found in the same pass, and the fix for it is the more useful finding. A typo'd "publsh tomorows class … and the stuff it links to" chose `publish_pages` 10/10 and, with no page named, filled in an open-ended `onOrAfter` — one lesson quietly turned into the whole rest of the term. Both publishers carry linked pages, so the word "links" alone was pulling it there. **The obvious fix was tried first and measured worse:** adding "NOT for one day's class — use publish_class_on" to the description fixed that probe and broke three others, sending "Publish Unit 2, Day 3" — a named page with no date in it at all — to `publish_class_on` 10/10, and dropping the window's own suggestions from 110/110 to 90/110. A small model reads a sentence naming another tool as a recommendation rather than a boundary. So it was reverted to the byte, and the rule went into CODE: an open-ended publish (no pages, a start date, no end) is refused with a message saying to use `publish_class_on` for one day or to give both dates for a stretch. A refusal changes nothing the model reads, so it cannot cost accuracy, and it comes back as ordinary text the assistant reads out — so the model corrects itself on the next turn. Publishing only: an open-ended UNPUBLISH hides rather than exposes, is undone by the same backup, and clearing a section back to a date is a real thing to want. 457 unit tests, 0 failures. | Windows should make the same cuts and keep the same split: the MCP surface may grow, the local surface changes only with a measurement behind it, and a test should pin the count. Score against the surface you actually ship, not the suite you inherited. Two rules that will look like clutter to whoever reads the code next, so they are written down rather than left to be re-derived: publishing's linked-page rule is unconditional because a published page whose links lead nowhere is broken *always*, and unpublish is deliberately NOT its mirror because taking down this week's lesson must not strip a page last week's lesson still points at. |

| 157 | 2026-08-15 | Up and Down should cycle through the teacher's own previous prompts in a chat, the way a Terminal does. | ✅ Added, per section and kept across launches — which is what makes it worth having, since a teacher working on one section over several days asks for the same handful of things in the same handful of words. The keys hand back `.ignored` when the box holds more than one line — the arrows have to move the caret between those lines — and when there is nowhere further to walk, so the field answers instead of the keystroke vanishing. | 162 | 2026-08-15 | Unpublishing a class must also repoint the section's index at the previous class, and move the index's date to match that class. | ✅ Done, and stated as an INVARIANT rather than a patch to one direction: *the section index transcludes the most recent class page students can see, and carries that class's date.* Written that way, publishing a newer class is covered by the same rule without a second one — and a rule stated once holds in the cases nobody listed. The date follows because the landing page's date IS the newest lesson's date to a reader; a front page dated August above a January lesson reads as abandoned. Three details earn their code: only the transclusion naming a CLASS page is touched, matched against the section's actual class titles rather than by position, since repointing `![[Key Links]]` at a lesson would be far worse than the bug being fixed; the newest class is read back from the GRAPH after the pages are written rather than worked out from the plan, so there is no second calculation to get wrong; and it happens inside `AssistPublishPlan.apply`, which puts it in the same `AssistChange` as the pages, so **"Undo that" takes the landing page back with them** — an undo that restored the lessons and left the front page pointing at the wrong one would be a worse state than either. A section with no dated visible class is left alone rather than pointed somewhere arbitrary. Four tests, including the undo. **Deliberately NOT done at build time**, though that was raised and would have covered a teacher who edits `publish:` by hand in Obsidian: it breaks the contract the toolchain rests on, that what is in the vault is what gets published. The builder works on a merged copy so it would not rewrite the teacher's files — which sounds like it escapes the objection and is in fact worse, because the live site would then show a page the vault does not contain and the teacher would have nothing to blame when the two disagreed. So the automation fires only where it can be seen to fire, and hand-editing frontmatter leaves the index exactly as written. **The note stays but now says the opposite thing**, in all 89 places it appears: 37 example courses, the 50 subject SKELETONS a course with no payload is built from, and both sections of the standalone example course. They told the teacher to repoint this by hand after every lesson; they now say to ask the local AI assistant, which does it and moves the date. Four payloads and the skeletons carried different wordings of the same instruction, so each was handled rather than assumed. **The skeleton GENERATOR was the one that mattered** — `generate_skeletons.py` still emitted the old sentence, so the next regeneration would have quietly undone all fifty; it was fixed, re-run, and its output diffed byte-for-byte against the checked-in skeletons to prove the two agree. Every payload still lints clean. 480 unit tests, 0 failures. | Port the invariant, not the bug fix — "unpublish repoints the index" leaves the publish direction broken and nobody notices until a teacher publishes a class and the front page still shows last week's. Two traps: match the transclusion by class title, or a section index that also transcludes Key Links will eventually have Key Links repointed at a lesson; and put the index write inside whatever your undo records, or undo restores the lessons and strands the landing page. |
| 163 | 2026-08-15 | A write command's cycle should be: the preview stops (if running), the file edits are made, the preview starts again for that section. The stopping and starting was not happening. | ✅ Fixed, and the mechanism was replaced rather than patched. Row 159 had the assistant POST a request that the section window observed and acted on — which cannot express an order at all: the runner posts, carries on, and the window stops the preview at whatever moment SwiftUI next evaluates a body, which may be after the writes. It also failed silently, which is worse than failing — the files changed, the preview did not move, and nothing said why. Now the section window REGISTERS its own `startPreview()`, `stopPreview()` and `isRunning` with `SectionPreviewControllers` when it appears, and the runner calls them directly, in order, on the main actor: stop → write → start becomes ordinary sequential code. Those are the same functions the toolbar button calls, so the assistant and the button cannot drift. **The stop sits beside the WRITES, not beside the start**, because that is the whole reason it is a separate step: a preview left serving while the pages beneath it are rewritten serves a half-changed site, and the teacher's next refresh is a race they cannot see they are in. Starting builds, so nothing does a separate `--build-only` pass first — that would build the same site twice and make a teacher wait through both. With no section window open there is nothing to drive: the site is brought up to date on disk and the answer says so plainly rather than claiming a preview nobody can see. Keys are standardised and case-folded on the way in, since the previous version compared raw folder paths and a mismatch there would have been invisible. **The test pins the SEQUENCE, not the set**: a fake preview records "stop", "write", "start" in the order they happen, so a stop that lands after the writes reads as `write, stop, start` and fails. 482 unit tests, 0 failures. | Take the registry, not the request. If your preview is owned by a window, the assistant needs the window to hand over its start/stop rather than to be told about an intention — anything observer-driven gets you "all three happened" while the order is wrong, and the symptom is a preview that looks stale for reasons nobody can reproduce. Two more: put the stop next to the writes it is protecting, and assert the order in a test, because every incorrect ordering still passes a test that only checks all three occurred. |
| 164 | 2026-08-15 | The assistant window should remember its placement, per section. | ✅ Remembered, and only the PLACEMENT. The window group stays `.restorationBehavior(.disabled)`, because reopening it at launch would load a model — gigabytes of a teacher's memory — before anyone asked for one; that decision stands. What is remembered is where the window goes when the teacher opens it themselves, which is the part they notice: an assistant that reappears in the middle of the screen, on top of the very section it is meant to sit beside, gets dragged back every single time. Per SECTION, because that is how it is used — section 1's assistant may live on a second monitor while section 2's sits beside the preview, and one shared position would mean arranging it again on every switch. AppKit's own frame autosave does the saving, so the frame is written on every move and resize and survives quit without any of our code needing a turn at the moment a window closes, which is exactly when it is least likely to get one. The saved frame is applied BEFORE the autosave name is set: the other order invites AppKit to write the freshly-cascaded default frame under that name and quietly overwrite the position being restored. Course codes are upper-cased into the key, so a course reached as `ics3u` from a typed tool argument and as `ICS3U` from the sidebar cannot end up with two remembered positions for one window. | Windows has its own placement memory; the two things worth copying are the SCOPE (per section, not per app) and the separation of placement from restoration — remembering where a window goes is not the same decision as reopening it, and conflating them is how an AI assistant ends up loading a model at launch. |

Also keep the cursor in the box after sending. | ✅ All six. **The plan staying is the one that matters most.** It used to live only in the approval card, so the moment a teacher pressed Go or Cancel the description of what they had just agreed to vanished — and with it the context for everything after. | 166 | 2026-08-15 | The tail was a harsh triangle and overlapped badly; make it curved. The tail is two quadratic curves: it leaves the bubble tangentially, swells outward, and hooks back in. It is also drawn INSIDE the shape's rect now: the first version drew past `maxY` and `maxX`, outside the bounds the background was given, so the tip was clipped — which is most of what made it look severe. The body gives up a strip below and to its own side, and the bubble's padding grows to match, so the words keep the same distance from every edge. **Plan text**: headings are bold and list items have no leading indent. The indent was actively harmful in a narrow bubble — every line of any length wraps, and the wrapped half returns to the left margin, so the indent marked only the first line of each item. Bold needs `AttributedString(markdown:)` with `.inlineOnlyPreservingWhitespace`: `Text(aStringVariable)` does NOT parse markdown (only string literals do), and the default markdown interpretation throws the line breaks away, which would run a plan's headings and its list into one paragraph. Anything that fails to parse is shown exactly as it arrived. | Two portable traps. A tail built as a separate shape will show a seam the moment the bubble is translucent, and one drawn outside the background's rect gets clipped on any platform. And check how your toolkit treats a runtime string: several of them style literals only, so emphasis built at runtime reaches the user as literal asterisks. |

| 168 | 2026-08-15 | After unpublishing, the preview stopped but never restarted, and a manual restart showed content from before the change. | ✅ One cause for both. `PreviewStopper` ran `preview.sh --stop` as a detached process and did not wait for it — right behind a button, where the teacher has already moved on, and wrong in front of a rebuild. | 180 | 2026-08-15 | Decision, from a documentation review: **Windows should move its AI model out of the container too.** The macOS assistant now runs `llama-server` as a host process on Metal, and it works well enough that the Swift implementation is the reference for how this feature should be built. | — Already the macOS arrangement; nothing to change here. Measure what a teacher's machine with integrated graphics actually gets — that decides the tier ladder, not whether to do it. Five things change on the way out of the container: `--no-mmap` is no longer needed (it exists for the cgroup and there isn't one on the host); the prompt-cache save/restore machinery can be deleted (it buys 175 s in a container and 2 s outside one, and an empty save silently poisons the next session); the weights move to the host, one file per machine, verified by exact byte count; a second executable ships and must be signed with the rest, with the llama.cpp build number PINNED so measurements stay comparable; and if you follow the mac to Qwen3, `--reasoning off` **and** `--reasoning-budget 0` become load-bearing — passing neither is correct for Qwen2.5 and a 97%→39% bug the day the model changes. Full write-up in `WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md` under "The local assistant: run the model natively". |

The layout is worth copying rather than eyeballing: the link and checkbox line up with the LEFT EDGE of the text above them, and the three vertical gaps around them match the dialog's own paragraph gap (16pt on macOS, measured off the running dialog through the accessibility API — a checkbox and a text field each eat about a point of their own, so the container has to be told 18 to look like 16). |

| 216 | 2026-08-16 | Reported from the real app: the checkbox stayed hidden after the assistant HAD been used. | ✅ Root cause was where the prompt was recorded, not the checkbox rule. What the teacher typed was written only when the model REPLIED, so anything short of a completed answer — an engine error, a reply still running, the window closed mid-thought, or opening the assistant and reading the reply without the turn completing — left no `asked:` line, and the checkbox correctly concluded there was nothing of theirs to include. It is now recorded the moment the message is SENT, which is where it always belonged: a routing problem cannot be looked into without the sentence that caused it, and that sentence must survive the model failing to answer. Also added: a line when the assistant could not answer at all, which was the one assistant failure leaving no trace anywhere. | Two new contract events — `assistant asked` and `assistant could not answer` in `activityTrail.mustRecord`, which will redden your suite until you record them, as designed. The lesson generalises: **record what the teacher DID at the moment they did it, never at the moment it succeeded.** Anything recorded on the success path is missing from exactly the sessions a report gets asked for. |
| 217 | 2026-08-16 | A problem report was named by date alone — people send several in a day. | ✅ Named to the second: "Plantoir problem report 2026-08-16 at 15.50.22.zip". Dots rather than colons, which are not legal in a file name and which the Finder renders as a slash; it is the shape macOS gives its own screenshots, so it reads as familiar rather than as a format. The folder INSIDE the archive carries the same stamp — otherwise two reports unzipped side by side both land in "Plantoir problem report" and collide, which is the same muddle one layer down and on the machine of whoever is trying to help. | Same format, from `shared-rules.json` → `problemReportDialog.reportName`. Colons are illegal in file names on Windows too, so the dotted time carries over unchanged. Stamp the inner folder as well as the archive. |
| 218 | 2026-08-16 | Reported again: asked the assistant several questions, still no checkbox. | ✅ Same root cause, one branch higher. `say()` returns EARLY for a phrasing matched in code — those never reach the model at all — and the recording sat below that branch, so every card phrasing ("Undo that", "Deploy this section now", "What would students see…") left no trace whatsoever. Moved above every branch, to the moment the teacher's words are accepted. Added a line saying a phrase was matched in code rather than sent to the model, which is the only place "why did it not think about what I said?" is answered. Two regression tests now drive `say()` end to end and were CONFIRMED to fail against the old placement before being kept. | New contract event `assistant matched a fixed phrase`. The rule, now stated twice because it has bitten twice: **record what the teacher did where they did it — above every branch, never at a later point that a branch can skip, and never on the success path.** Both failures looked like a broken conditional and were a misplaced write. |
| 219 | 2026-08-16 | Add a Settings panel so a teacher can choose which local assistant runs on their Mac, switch between them, and remove a downloaded one to get the disk space back — with guidance about each one's size, and without naming any model. | ✅ Implemented — **Plantoir ▸ Settings… (⌘,)**, a `Settings` scene, one pane. Three choices: **Choose for me** (the factory setting, and what every Mac did before the panel existed), **The smaller assistant**, **The larger assistant**. Each names both costs — download size and memory while working — because they are different decisions and neither is guessable from the other; the figures are derived from `AssistModelTier`, not typed. "Choose for me" is stored as an INTENT, never resolved-and-written-down, so a later change to the ladder still reaches a Mac whose teacher opened Settings once. A hand-picked choice that takes more than a third of the machine gets a caution naming both numbers and stays selectable — their Mac, their call. "On this Mac" lists both rungs with Download / Stop / Remove; Remove is refused while any assistant window is open and says WHICH section to close. Removing the one in use is allowed and explained, because that is the state every Mac is in before its first download. Six trail events, 22 tests. | **Three bugs here were found by driving the real app, not by the tests — expect all three.** (1) A `Section`'s content, header and footer each get their OWN observation-tracking scope, so footers that read only the file system never invalidated: the rows redrew after a removal while the summary sentences went on describing a deleted model. Half a panel updating is more convincing than none. Whatever your framework, make disk-derived answers depend on something observable. (2) The panel and each assistant window made their own download store for the SAME path — press Download in Settings, open the assistant, and the second one deletes the part-finished file and starts again: two transfers, one destination, gigabytes, school connection. One store per rung, process-wide. (3) Closing the assistant window cancelled the download — correct when that window started it, wrong once the stores are shared, since Settings' whole point is fetching ahead of time. Scope the cancel to the window that started it. Also: a settings window is HIDDEN, not destroyed, so its state outlives every visit — look at the disk again when it comes forward. |

The rule is copied from Quartz's own `fileTrie.ts` rather than invented, so the assistant and the sidebar cannot drift: frontmatter `title:` unless it is literally "index", then the FOLDER's name, then the file name. **(b)** Referrers were remembered by NAME and looked back up — and every folder's landing page is called `index`, so the lookup returned whichever one the folder walk reached first. Invisible while the answer printed as "index"; a confidently WRONG folder the moment it printed as one. Referrers are now carried as pages and de-duplicated by PATH. Verified against the reported course: both lines now read "Portfolios". 9 tests, and both end-to-end ones were CONFIRMED to fail against the old code — (b) alone printed "Concepts", a folder that does not link to the page at all. | **Both halves apply to `Plantoir.Core/Assist` unchanged — this is not a mac-shaped bug.** New contract section `shared-rules.json` → `pageNaming` with the rule, the five cases, and the identity-vs-label rule; run it. The trap worth stating on its own: **fixing only the label makes the output worse.** A teacher sent to Concepts to find a link that lives in Portfolios concludes the assistant is lying and stops reading the list, where "index" merely told them nothing. If you key referrers, backlinks or any page set by name anywhere, `index.md` collapses them all onto one key — check for it before touching the label. |

Then you asked me to undo that, and I have done so." The count was wrong as well as ugly: hiding one class writes two files, because the section's landing page is repointed in the same change. **Three separate sentences now, where there was one slot:** undone; undone but some pages left alone because they were edited since; and nothing undone at all — that last case used to fall through to the success sentence, so a teacher was told their change had been taken back while not one file had moved. `nothingToUndo` narrowed to "any pages", because it claimed more than it knew. | Deserialise the seven new `assist-wording.json` entries — `undid`, `undidPartly`, `couldNotUndo`, `undoIsStillAvailable`, `nothingToUndo`, `undoDoesNotReachTheLiveSite`, `aCreatedPageCanBeTakenBack` — which carry a `{change}` placeholder for the clause. **The rule that generalises: a stored clause that only ever appears inside a sentence somebody wrote on purpose cannot come out ungrammatical.** Do not build these by concatenation at the call site, and never reuse a success slot for a refusal. |
| 222 | 2026-08-16 | Same report: the undo made the file edits but the preview was never restarted. It could not do this before for a structural reason: a change knew only its FILES, so an undo could not tell which section's preview to touch. A change now carries its course and section. **And it rebuilds only when the change it reverses rebuilt** (`rebuildsThePreview`), so undoing a created class page — which arrives unpublished and changes nothing the preview shows — does not cost a rebuild. | A `FakePreview` test asserts the ORDER, not the presence: `[stop-begins, stop-ends, write, start]`. The same skip rule protects it as protects an edited page: once the teacher has written in it, the page is theirs and the undo leaves it alone. The line telling teachers "“Undo that” does not take away a page it created" was corrected — a line describing what a feature used to do is worse than no line. | Optional-`before` is the whole trick; an empty string will not do, because an empty page and an absent one are different states. The app appends "(Today is …)" to every message and with it the same card is exact. **A probe that does not send what the app sends measures nothing, and the number it produces reads exactly like a fault in the product.** Also from the older suite: that line must be APPENDED, not prepended — prepending costs 15 points of routing. |

| 226 | 2026-08-16 | When a class page (Unit x, Day y) is published and it links to other pages that have never been published before, those linked pages should take the class page's date. | ✅ Implemented, and it uncovered a split. **`publish_class_on` already moved dates; `publish_pages` never did** — so "Publish tomorrow's class" dated the pages a class brought and "Publish Unit 2, Day 3", naming the very same page, left them on whatever day their FILE was made. Same teacher, same class, two answers depending on the sentence. Both routes now run one rule, `dateMovesFollowingClasses`. A linked page takes the class's date when it is **hidden right now** (this publish is the first time students will see it) and is **not itself a class page** — a class's date is its place in the schedule. Where several published classes share a page the EARLIEST claims it, matching `first_use_dates` in `setup_course.py`, so a pre-populated course and a hand-published one agree. A shared page students have never seen now moves. Contract: `class-planning.json` → `datingPagesAClassBrings`, which carries the conditions, the key names, the earliest-class rule and the reasoning. "Never published" is INFERRED from the page being hidden — nothing on disk records history — so a page published once and later hidden counts as never published. |

Dates come from position in the timetable exactly as a single new class does, which is why it can add days to Unit 4 while Unit 5 pages already exist and still date them right: the count is of PAGES, not of unit numbering. Asking for more days than there are dates refuses WHOLE and opens the schedule sheet, rather than creating some and leaving the teacher to work out how many. | The phrasing is parsed, and understands words as well as digits — a teacher asking for a few more days writes "five", not "5". `unit` and `days` are not in the tool's schema. |
| 238 | 2026-08-16 | "Duplicate Unit 3, Day 2 as my next class" — copy it to the next day of the same unit with a date from the schedule, shifting later classes back when room is needed. | ✅ The copy becomes Unit 3, Day 3 with the source's content and a date of its own. **Making room is `ClassInsertionPlanner`'s job rather than a second copy of it** — it renames from the HIGHEST DAY DOWN so nothing is written over, re-dates everything from the insertion point onto real meeting days, and rewrites every wikilink that pointed at a renamed page. When nothing needs moving, the same call simply adds the page at the end. The copy starts hidden however the source was: a duplicate of a published lesson is a draft of next week's. | **Undoable ONLY when nothing else moved.** A partial undo that deleted the new page and left every later class renamed would be worse than no undo, so when classes shuffled the answer says the way back is the backup taken before any of it. Duplicating needs the schedule, so it goes through the same `askForTheTimetable` as everything else. |
| 239 | 2026-08-16 | A page kept published by a HIDDEN class page referrer is wrong. Corollary: publishing a class that links to a hidden page must publish that page, and disclose it. | ✅ "X still links to it" now counts only when students can SEE X. Before, a page could sit visible and reachable from nothing, held up by a draft nobody had published — kept alive by a page that is not there. The corollary already held and is now pinned by a test: publishing follows links and the plan says "“Ohm's Law” will become visible". | Safe in one direction because of the other: a page taken down here comes back the moment anything visible needs it, since publishing is transitive. The pair is worth testing together — one test that a HIDDEN referrer does not keep a page, one that a VISIBLE referrer still does, because the second is the rule that stops one class being broken to tidy another. |
| 240 | 2026-08-16 | Plan mode should be a SETTING now that app settings exist — always describe and confirm by default, and invite the teacher to turn that off when they trust the assistant. | ✅ New first section in Settings ▸ Assistant: "Ask me before changing anything", on by default. One stored answer shared with the gate, so the switch and the assistant cannot disagree; a conversation already open follows it without being reopened. The old per-tier BLOCK is gone — a teacher can turn it off on either assistant, because which one is running changes the odds, not whose decision it is — and the smaller one gets a caution carrying its measured number instead of a disabled switch. Deploying still always asks, whatever this is set to. | **Two `AppSettings` instances over one defaults store is not the same as one object**, and a test caught it: each caches what it read at construction, so a change through one was invisible to the other. In the app both would have been `AppSettings.shared` and it would have worked by luck. `AssistPlanMode` now takes the settings OBJECT. New contract event `assistant confirmation changed`: a report saying "it just did it without asking" is explained entirely by that line. |
| 241 | 2026-08-16 | After 15 confirmed actions — app-wide, and just once — mention that the setting exists, for discoverability. | ✅ Was five in a row, per conversation, reset by a Cancel. Now fifteen, counted app-wide across every conversation and course, never reset, and said ONCE ever. The message points at the switch by name rather than offering an inline toggle. | The old count reset with every window, so a teacher working in short bursts could accept a hundred plans across twenty conversations and never be told the setting existed, having never hit five in one sitting. A Cancel no longer zeroes it either: the number measures how much of the assistant's work this teacher has READ, and a Cancel is evidence of reading rather than against it. The assistant still only RAISES it on the larger model — that part stayed measured — while the switch itself is available on both. |

Now pinned by a test that sets up the exact situation the narrowing created — the ONLY page linking to each of the four is HIDDEN — and it was CONFIRMED to fail with the exclusions removed. | The order in `reasonToKeep` is load-bearing rather than incidental, and there was nothing saying so until now. Narrowing a sweep rule makes every exclusion above it matter more than it did the day before, so the exclusions are the thing to re-test after any change to what gets swept. |
| 243 | 2026-08-16 | Both assistants should follow the same rules for the confirmation setting — discoverability. | ✅ The tier no longer decides anything about the gate. The measured number (one request in five) is put in front of the teacher as a caution in Settings instead — which respects the measurement AND the person, rather than deciding on their behalf. |
| 244 | 2026-08-16 | A scheduled deploy set for 9:49 PM did not fire, and macOS's Background Activity notice called the thing "bash" rather than naming Plantoir. | ✅ One cause for both. The launch agent ran `/bin/bash -c <script>`, so macOS had no application to attribute it to: the notice named the interpreter, and — the half that actually broke — the privacy system granted it nothing. The working folder is on the Desktop, which is protected, and the agent's log is `Operation not permitted` on every path INCLUDING `getcwd`, while the identical deploy from the app seven minutes earlier finished in 144 seconds. The agent now runs the app's own signed binary with `--run-scheduled-deploy <script>`, and the one-shot command moved from a line inside the plist to a file beside it. All the script's logic — build if stale, deploy only if the build worked, clear the alarm either way — is unchanged. | **Proved by an A/B probe rather than reasoned about**: two launch agents running the identical script in the same second, one through `/bin/bash` and one through the app. Bash got `READ DENIED` and `Operation not permitted`; through Plantoir it read the folder and found `course_config.json`. The mechanism is the same one that makes pressing Deploy work — a script the APP spawns is attributed to the app. Windows has no equivalent (Task Scheduler, no TCC), but the general lesson travels: **a background job that runs a bare interpreter has no identity, and on a platform with per-application permissions that means no access to the teacher's files.** Cancelling now removes the script as well as the alarm. |
| 245 | 2026-08-16 | After the fix, macOS's Background Activity notice named "Russell Gordon" rather than "Plantoir" — is that Apple's design? | ✅ Yes, verified against the certificate: Background Task Management lists background items by the signing certificate's ORGANISATION (`O=Russell Gordon`), not by application name. Same reason that pane shows "Google LLC" and "Dropbox, Inc." rather than Chrome and Dropbox. Nothing to fix; the finding is written into `ScheduledDeploy` so it is not re-investigated. **And the fix itself is confirmed working end to end** — a real scheduled deploy built the site and uploaded 23 files to Netlify, ✅ Deploy complete, where the same alarm through bash had failed on every path. | **`SMAppService.agent` would put the app's own name there and cannot be used**, which is worth knowing before somebody spends an afternoon on it: it associates an agent with the app bundle only for a plist BAKED IN at build time, and this plist's whole content is a `StartCalendarInterval` the teacher chose, one per course and section. | 249 | 2026-08-16 | Re-date a section's classes onto a schedule once it has been provided — the way a teacher rolls a course over to a new year. Classes map to dates purely by POSITION. | ✅ `SectionReDatePlanner`, plan-then-apply, behind the same gate as every other write and undoable in one step. Three kinds of page move: **classes** take their position's date (first class, first date); **what a class brings** — every page it links to, transitively — takes that class's day, earliest class winning a shared page; **pages the section uses all year**, meaning what Key Links points at, take the first day of class. On the shelf. | **Curriculum pages are deliberately NOT re-dated, and finding out why is the useful part.** `build_site.py` already dates them on every build — to the LATEST date in the section, so they sort to the top — and it does it to the copy it PUBLISHES, so anything written to the source would be overwritten on the way out. Moving them here would be work that does nothing while fighting a rule that already exists. Check what your own build does before adding a rule about dates. **Position, not numbering**: a course where a unit ran long, or with a page called "Field Trip" rather than Unit 2, Day 4, is dated wrongly by counting unit and day numbers. Counting POSITION cannot be wrong about a course it has never seen. The tool is on the surface but OFF the local model's list — the thirteen it is shown has not grown, and a test now says so in as many words. |
| 250 | 2026-08-16 | Re-dating refused when a section had more classes than the new year has days. Move or delete them when you have decided what to do." | **Refusing read as careful and was the opposite.** It left EVERY page on last year's dates — the state the teacher asked to be rid of — over a few classes at the end they had not thought about yet. A school year is rarely the same length twice, so coming up short is the ORDINARY case, not the error case, and what a teacher does about it (move things, merge two lessons, drop what no longer fits) is planning, which is theirs. The general rule: **when a job is 95% doable, do the 95% and show the remainder — do not withhold all of it over the part that needs a human.** Note `add_next_class` still refuses when the dates run out, and that stays: declining to CREATE pages you cannot date is not the same as declining to re-date pages that already exist. |
| 251 | 2026-08-16 | Adding a class should stop refusing when the recorded dates run out, the same way re-dating now does. | ✅ `add_next_class` — one class or five days' worth — makes the pages on the LAST class date instead of refusing, and the plan says so: "This one has no class date left, so it shares the last day with the class already on it. Defaulting to the longer string in order to have it thrown away is the wrong way round. | 263 | 2026-08-17 | The Deploy button was disabled while a section preview was active or building; clicking Deploy now automatically cancels/stops any running or building preview, awaits container-side process termination, and starts the deploy. | ✅ Implemented in `SectionDetailView.swift`. The Deploy toolbar button's `.disabled` condition was changed from `isBusy` (which evaluated to true whenever a preview was running) to `deployRunner.isRunning`. In `deployAndWait()`, if `previewRunner.isRunning` is true, it calls `await stopPreviewAndWait()`, terminating both the host runner and container-side Node/Quartz/build processes via `PreviewStopper` before proceeding with the production build (`preview.sh --build-only`) and deployment (`deploy.sh`). Host execution completely removes WSL2/Docker overhead and gives users native on-device assistant performance. |
