| 346 | 2026-08-18 | The Windows assistant is still chatty: its replies do not match what the mac says for the same request, even after rows 344 and 345. Find out why and fix it comprehensively. | ✅ Implemented on Windows (`AssistToolAnswer.cs` new, `PlantoirTools.cs`, `AssistAgent.cs`, `McpClient.cs`, `PublishPlan.cs`, `AssistWorkspace.cs`). **The cause was structural, not wording.** Every Windows tool returned ONE string and it was shown to the teacher and fed to the model both — so "read Unit 2, Day 3" put a lesson's entire Markdown in the chat, "what pages are in this section" put sixty file paths there, a publish said "Published 4 pages (A, B, C, D) and rebuilt the preview of ICS3U Section 1", and a plan ended with "Show this to the teacher and ask before going ahead" — a sentence addressed to the model, read by the teacher. The mac has split the two since the assistant shipped (`AssistToolOutcome`: a one-line `summary` for the transcript, a long `detail` for the model), and that split is the whole reason its replies are short. Ported: the summary now travels out of `plantoir-mcp` in the result's `_meta`, the text content is unchanged so Claude Code sees exactly what it saw before, and the app shows the summary. Teacher lines are now the mac's, word for word — "Read “Unit 2, Day 3”.", "Found 42 pages in ICS3U Section 1.", "Published 4 pages.", "It's already been published.". Undo now speaks the CONTRACT's sentences (`AssistWording.Undid` and friends) instead of its own "Undid publishing “X” — put 3 files back.", which needed the undo history's descriptions turned past-tense. `list_recent_changes` no longer names `undo_last_change` to a teacher. The turn-ending list named `roll_over_course` for a tool called `roll_over_section` and omitted five other writes, so those got the model an extra lap and the teacher a paragraph restating the line above it. 502 tests pass. | **The mac needs no change — this is Windows catching up**, and the entry in `MAC-HANDOFF.md` says which contract case is still wanted. The one thing worth carrying back: the CHANNEL. macOS calls its tool runner in-process, so its split costs nothing; Windows drives the same `plantoir-mcp` Claude Code drives, so the summary needed a wire. `_meta` was chosen over a second text block (Claude Code would read both) and over `structuredContent` (that field is validated against a declared `outputSchema`, and declaring one changes what every client sees). `_meta` is the protocol's own extension slot, needs no schema, and is dropped by every client that does not know the key. `ToolAnswerTests.TheTeachersLineTravelsInMetaWhereTheAppLooksForIt` serialises a real result and asserts the wire shape, because a silent fallback to the model's half is exactly how this regresses. |
| 347 | 2026-08-18 | The Windows Settings switch "ask before changing" was wired to nothing: macOS says the PLAN before every write and waits for Go, Windows just did it and reported afterwards. Make the setting mean what it says. | ✅ Implemented on Windows (`AssistAgent.cs`, `PlantoirTools.cs`, `AssistToolAnswer.cs`, `AssistWindow.xaml.cs`). `ConfirmationMode` was read from `AppSettings.AssistantAsksBeforeChanging` and then never consulted — so the switch did nothing, and the "the assistant shows what it is about to do before doing it" nudge after fifteen accepted plans described behaviour the app did not have. Now: before any write with a `plan_` twin, the twin runs, its plan is SAID in the transcript, and Go / Cancel appear under it. The model is not asked a second time — the arguments it chose are carried through, so what runs on Go is exactly what was described. A fixed phrasing from the promise card is gated too: skipping the MODEL is not skipping the teacher. A plan twin that REFUSED gets no buttons, because a refusal is an answer and "Shall I go ahead?" under one invites a teacher to approve a dead end. Cancel now answers the model as the TOOL ("The teacher decided not to. Nothing was done.") rather than as the assistant, so the model's dangling call is not a hole the next turn reads across. A scheduled deploy's card said only "Shall I go ahead?" — no time, no section — and now says what it is a deploy of, and offers "Deploy" rather than "Go". 505 tests pass. | **Inherited from the mac unchanged in intent; two Windows-specific pieces to know.** (a) The gate needs to tell a PLAN from a plan tool's REFUSAL, which on the mac is `AssistToolOutcome.isPlan` — an in-process field. Over MCP it is a second `_meta` key, `plantoir.app/isPlan`, sent only when true. Without it the gate reads every plan as a refusal and no write ever runs, which is why `ToolAnswerTests.APlanMarksItselfAsOne` exists. (b) `AssistAgent.PlanTwins` deliberately omits `re_date_classes`, which IS in the contract's twin list: it is not a tool the local model is shown, and `plan_re_date_classes` does not mark its answer, so gating it would hold a write behind a proposal the loop would then read as a refusal. `ContractTests.PlanTwins_CoverEveryLocalWriteThatHasOne` pins the scope so the omission cannot quietly grow. |
| 348 | 2026-08-18 | "What would students see in this section right now?" on Windows answered "83 visible pages are linked from nowhere" and listed the course's lessons. A healthy course read as a broken one, and the mac says nothing of the kind for the same content. | ✅ Two separate defects in `check_section`, both found from one report (`LinkGraph.cs`, `PlantoirTools.cs`). **(a) Class pages were counted as orphans.** They are the ROOTS of a section — everything else must be reachable FROM a class, and a class is reached through the site's own navigation of All Classes — so "nothing links to this" is their ordinary, correct state. ICD2O Section 1 has 85 class pages and 83 of them were reported "linked from nowhere"; reproduced against the real course, then clean afterwards. The mac has excluded them since it hit the same thing on an 86-period credit (84 reported). Windows now excludes them using the COURSE's own answer — `per_section_folders` from `course_config.json` — rather than the mac's folder-name heuristic. **(b) The preview state was read from the wrong process.** `PreviewLeases` is an in-memory static belonging to the app, and `check_section` runs inside `plantoir-mcp`, where it is permanently empty — so "Nothing is being previewed at the moment" was said every time, whatever was on screen. The trail shows this teacher pressing Preview 32 seconds before asking. It now reads the cross-process lease FILES the app already writes for exactly this purpose, and reports the mac's three states: building, showing (silence), or nothing. 510 tests pass. | **The mac needs no change; this is Windows catching up on both counts.** The trap worth carrying, though, is (b) rather than (a): on the mac the assistant and the preview are the same process, so `sectionWindow(...)?.previewState()` is a method call. Every fact the Windows assistant wants about what the APP is doing has to cross a process boundary, and an in-memory static reads as "nothing is happening" rather than as an error — silently, and identically to a real answer. `WorkLease` is the channel and it was already there. Two limits worth stating: the leases are per-COURSE, not per-section, so previewing Section 2 while asking about Section 1 reports the wrong thing; and the build lease is dropped the moment the server answers, which is what makes "building" and "showing" two states rather than one. |

(1) `AssistCardCommand.ToJsonObject` populated required parameter `includeLinked = false` for publish/unpublish tools. (2) `PlantoirTools.cs` methods added default parameter values `includeLinked = false`, `progress = null!`, `cancellation = default` to prevent ModelContextProtocol.NET binding failures. (3) Added `PublishPlan.UnitNamed` and `AssistWorkspace.PlanWholeUnit` / `ApplyWholeUnit` with full macOS parity: steps through unit classes in order (highest day down for unpublish, Day 1 up for publish), wraps changes into a single undo batch, and rebuilds the preview once. 522 tests pass. | Windows assistant now fully supports whole unit planning, publishing, unpublishing, and undo with macOS parity. |

## Planned

- **Windows equivalent of the GUI** — a native Windows counterpart to the
  macOS app, wrapping the same toolchain via the PowerShell launchers /
  WSL2 Docker Engine. First take built (see Windows status above);
  every behaviour in the table above applies unless marked macOS-only.

| 282 | 2026-08-19 | Deploying immediately after stopping a preview failed instantly, showing nothing past the launcher's opening lines. Stopping a preview fires the launcher's `--stop` sweep fire-and-forget; it kills the section's container-side processes BY WORKING DIRECTORY seconds later, and the deploy's own quiet build works in that same directory — so the natural stop-then-deploy order killed the fresh build before its first output flushed. | ⏳ Not yet on macOS — its `PreviewStopper` is the same fire-and-forget design this side copied (row 105), so the same race exists there and has only been dodged by timing. See MAC-HANDOFF.md. | ✅ Fixed on Windows first: `PreviewStopper` tracks in-flight sweeps (15 s cap each) and `Deploy_Click`, `PreviewOrStop_Click`, and `StartAutomatedPreview` all `await PreviewStopper.WhenSweepsFinish()` before launching a build. (origin: Windows) |
| 283 | 2026-08-19 | Windows deploys kept failing ~5 s in, transcript ending at "Starting container if needed …". An adversarial review found the real cause: a "hardening" change (521bbc7c) had given `ScriptRunner.WaitUntilFinished()` a DEFAULT 5-second timeout that force-finished and disposed the ConPTY of whatever it awaited — every quiet build and deploy was being executed for exactly five seconds. Fixed by making `WaitUntilFinished()` unbounded again (mac parity) with a separate `WaitUntilFinishedOrKill(timeoutMs)` reserved for a process already told to stop. In the same change Windows adopted the mac's deploy-during-preview flow (mac commit `922a29b1`): the Deploy button stays enabled while a preview runs, stops it itself, awaits the stop — sweep included, section-scoped, live-polled, 20 s bound — then deploys; the assistant's "section busy" gate now means "a deploy is already running", per the contract. | ✅ Already the mac's design — Windows adopted it; nothing to port back. The 5-second-timeout defect was Windows-only. | ✅ Fixed: `ScriptRunner.cs` (wait primitives), `PreviewStopper.cs` (synchronous sweep registration, live-polled scoped wait, no per-sweep cap), `SectionDetailView.xaml.cs` (`Deploy_Click` stop-then-deploy with post-stop re-checks and exception-path claim release), `MainWindow.IsSectionBusy` → deploy-only. (origin: Windows) |

Two things the mac still owes itself, both deferred out of 1.1.0: make the first turn await the warm-up (worth ~1.4 s, an optimisation not a fix), and sample the engine's discarded output into the trail the way `NoteServerLine` does — right now a mac problem report can carry nothing llama-server said. |

| 297 | 2026-08-20 | The marketing screenshots keep coming out with ugly black pixels on the rounded window corners, and the class-site shots carry the black address bar of a Safari private window. Use only the capture method macOS itself uses, and never a private window. | ✅ Both fixed at the source. **One capture method.** `MarketingScreenshotTests.save` tried `screencapture -o -l <window number>` and, on any stumble, fell through SILENTLY to XCUITest's `window.screenshot()` — a rectangle capture that bakes the corner curves against whatever was behind them and returns opaque black specks. A run could file a mix of good and bad shots with nothing to say which was which. The fallback is gone; a failure now stops the run and names the shot. `mask_window_corners` went with it — it existed only to paper over that damage, and masking a correct capture is not harmless, since it multiplies a GUESSED 11-point radius over a real one. Measured: a `screencapture -l` capture's four corner pixels read `(0,0,0,0)` already. **Never a private window.** It was private for a real reason — a class site remembers `localStorage["theme"]`, and a choice saved during ordinary browsing once overrode a dark pass — so the reason is answered twice over instead: an ordinary window in a hand-made Safari profile named `⎚` when one exists — one glyph, because Safari puts the profile's name in the toolbar and "Screenshots" would caption every class-site shot with our own photography — (separate storage, ordinary chrome — Safari's equivalent of Windows' `--user-data-dir`), and `verify_appearance`, which stops the run when a page came out in the wrong appearance. That check is measured, not guessed: across the sixteen class-site shots on the site today, light pages median 248–249 and dark ones 17–21 against a threshold of 128. | **Windows already had the better half of both.** `--user-data-dir=EDGE_PROFILE` is why the Edge shots never needed InPrivate, and that is what the Safari profile copies. Worth checking the Windows harness for the other half: `photograph()` grabs a REGION (`DWMWA_EXTENDED_FRAME_BOUNDS`) and masks the corners by hand, because Windows has no `screencapture -l` equivalent — so the black-corner risk is real there and the masking is NOT removable. The rule that does travel: if a capture cannot be taken properly, fail loudly rather than fall back to a lesser method, because a wrong screenshot that looks right is the worst thing this harness can produce. |
| 298 | 2026-08-20 | Every site Plantoir builds wore **Quartz's logo** in the browser tab. Quartz ships `quartz/static/icon.png` and its `Head` links it as the favicon, so a teacher publishing four sections got four tabs carrying somebody else's mark. Replaced with Plantoir's own icon, drawn from the app icon: `support/favicon/` (generated by `scripts/brand_images.py` from `mac-app/Plantoir.icon`) holds `icon.svg`, `favicon.ico` (16/32/48, BMP entries), `apple-touch-icon.png` (180, square and opaque so iOS masks it once) and `icon.png`; `build_site.py`'s `install_favicon()` copies them into `quartz/static/` on every build and `favicon.ico` into the content root as well, and `patches/Head.tsx` links all three shapes with page-relative paths. The favicon is the **fill** weight of the same plant, not the outline: at 16 px the outline's leaf midribs collapse onto the outline and the mark turns to mush — rendered and looked at, not assumed. Verified in Safari on a real site, and `verify.sh` now checks the files, their bytes, and the `<link>` tags. | ✅ Implemented — shared toolchain only; no Swift changed. Reaches a working folder on the next app rebuild, via `.toolchain/` as usual. | **Shared Python, shared Dockerfile, shared assets — nothing to mirror in C#.** Two things to know anyway. (1) `favicon.ico` is installed TWICE on purpose: `quartz/static/` (Static emitter → `public/static/`) for the `<link>` tags, and the CONTENT ROOT (Assets emitter → `public/`) because that is the only route to `/favicon.ico`, which feed readers and older browsers fetch without reading the page. The content-root copy must happen after `content/` is rebuilt or it is silently deleted. (2) You cannot regenerate this set: `brand_images.py` reads `mac-app/Plantoir.icon`, which is mac-only — same direction as `--write-contracts`. Treat `support/favicon/*` as data you receive; `windows-app/Plantoir/Assets/make-icon.ps1` stays a separate regeneration for the app's own `.ico`. Full reasoning, including the two rejected designs, in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md. |

| 300 | 2026-08-21 | Deploying (or previewing) through the LOCAL in-app assistant with no section window open ran completely silently — the chat answered "deployed", but nothing on screen ever showed a console, a progress header, or moved at all, which read exactly like the assistant hadn't done anything. Reported directly: "Using that command in the local assistant should simply do the same thing as pressing the Deploy button." Traced first: this was a pre-existing, deliberately documented design decision (`documentation/10-local-ai-assistant.md`), grouping "local assistant, no window open" together with MCP (`Plantoir --mcp-stdio`, a genuinely separate headless process with no Scene graph at all) and a 6:30 a.m. scheduled deploy (app not even running) as the same silent fallback — which was right for the latter two and wrong for the local assistant, since it alone runs inside a real, on-screen app session. Fixed for the local assistant only: `AssistToolRunner.revealSectionOnScreen` reuses an already-open window on the same folder if one exists (the common case — the assistant is almost always opened FROM an already-open window's sidebar), or opens a fresh one (`openWindow(id: "main")` — the "Plantoir" WindowGroup gained an explicit id for this, previously id-less), sets its selection to the section asked about, brings it to the front, and waits for `SectionWindowControllers` to actually register it before proceeding through the exact same path the button uses. MCP and scheduled deploys are structurally unable to do this (no Scene graph exists in either process) and keep the old silent fallback, unchanged. **Driving the real app caught a real bug the design missed**: the first version opened a new window that landed on the folder picker instead of the section, because it trusted the new window's own Cmd+N-style folder-guessing heuristic (keyed off whichever window was last focused) instead of setting the folder directly from what the assistant already knew — fixed by identifying the new window by identity and calling `adoptRestoredPath` on it explicitly; retested the same way, confirmed the section itself now opens with a preview genuinely building. | ✅ Implemented and verified live — `AssistToolRunner.swift` (`revealSectionOnScreen`, `openWindowModel`), `AssistSession.swift`/`AssistWindowView.swift` (threading `@Environment(\.openWindow)` down as `openMainWindow`, present ONLY on the local-assistant path), `QuartzTeachersApp.swift` (`WindowGroup("Plantoir", id: "main", …)`). 5 new tests in `AssistRevealsSectionOnScreenTests.swift` covering everything unit-testable without a real SwiftUI render pass (a matching window is reused rather than reopened; `openMainWindow` is never called when a window is already open; the nil-capability MCP/scheduled path is untouched) — the full flow needed a real SwiftUI render pass no unit test can produce, so it was driven by hand: quit every window but the assistant chat, ask it to preview, and confirm the section's own window opens with a build genuinely running. That drive is what caught the folder-adoption bug above — the unit suite stayed green through both the broken and the fixed version. | **Shared architecture, not shared code — Windows needs the same DECISION, not this Swift.** If the Windows app's local assistant has the same "no section open → runs silently" gap, the fix is the same shape: something only the in-app assistant can do (open/focus a window) that MCP and a scheduled task genuinely cannot. Full reasoning in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md, including why the fix could not be "just call openWindow with no arguments" (no such SwiftUI overload exists for an id-less WindowGroup, contrary to an initial assumption), why giving the main WindowGroup an id was verified safe (restoration is handled entirely outside SwiftUI's own id-keyed mechanism already), and the folder-adoption trap named explicitly so WinUI's port doesn't repeat it: identify a freshly opened window by IDENTITY, not by the folder it's supposed to end up with, and set that folder directly rather than trusting a last-focused-window heuristic built for a different situation. |
| 301 | 2026-08-21 | Every class site published to Netlify was about to start wearing Netlify's own "Powered by Netlify" ad badge in front of students — free-plan projects created 2026-08-19 or later default it ON, per Netlify's own rollout table, and there is no API to turn it off (checked their full published OpenAPI spec directly: no field named `badge`, `powered_by`, or `premium` anywhere). `deploy.py` now writes `public/_headers` with a Content-Security-Policy whose `script-src` omits `'unsafe-inline'` — the badge only renders via an inline `<script>` Netlify injects at their edge, and that policy makes the browser refuse to run it, which is Netlify's own documented way to suppress it. The policy is built by scanning the ACTUAL built site (every unique inline `<script>` body, SHA-256-hashed, plus any cross-origin `<script src>` host) rather than a hardcoded list, so it self-adapts to a Quartz upgrade or a teacher's own embedded script instead of silently breaking either. Runs on the Netlify path only, automatically, on every deploy — no teacher action, no per-site dashboard visit. A teacher who clicks "Show details" during a deploy now sees a plain-language line explaining what this is and that it checked their own scripts first. | ✅ Implemented — shared toolchain only; `scripts/deploy.py` (`write_netlify_headers_file`, `_collect_inline_script_policy`), tested in `scripts/test_deploy_netlify_headers.py` (no Docker needed — wired into `verify.sh` as a fast pre-check). Verified against the real Example Course build: 5 unique inline-script hashes, 1 cross-origin host (`cdn.jsdelivr.net`, KaTeX). | **Shared Python — nothing to mirror.** Windows inherits this the same way it inherits every `deploy.py` change, on the next sync. Full reasoning, including the two rejected designs (a hardcoded hash allow-list, and patching Quartz's components to externalize every inline script instead), in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md. |
| 302 | 2026-08-21 | Row 301's badge-suppression CSP scans the built site and hash-allows every inline `<script>` it finds — but `deploy.py` imports the scanning logic itself from a sibling module, `scripts/netlify_badge.py`, and the Dockerfile's explicit per-file `COPY` list for `/opt/scripts` never picked up that new file when it was added. Every real deploy through the container — Netlify OR the marketing site — failed with `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'netlify_badge'`. The pure-Python unit test never caught it because it imports `deploy` directly from the working tree, where the sibling module sits right there on disk; only a deploy through the actual BUILT IMAGE hits the gap. Found while testing this branch combined with two others on a shared test branch, before any of the three reached `dev`. | ✅ Implemented — `Dockerfile` (added the missing `COPY scripts/netlify_badge.py`), `verify.sh` (added the matching `check_baked` line, which had the identical gap: it checks every other baked script against the working tree but never checked this one). Full `verify.sh` green. | **Shared toolchain — nothing to mirror in C#.** Reaches a working folder on the next app rebuild, via `.toolchain/` as usual, same as row 301 itself. |
| 303 | 2026-08-21 | Row 301's badge-suppression CSP (script-src, hash-only sources, no `unsafe-inline`) silently broke Quartz's own Explorer SIDEBAR on every page of every site deployed to Netlify — a much bigger break than the badge fix was meant to risk. `patches/explorer.inline.ts` builds its sort/filter/map functions from `data-data-fns` JSON via `new Function("return " + source)()` — a capability `'unsafe-eval'` governs, not a script identity, so hash-allowing every actual inline script (which row 301 already did correctly) never touched it. Found by A/B testing a real deploy: Netlify's sidebar rendered "Navigate this site" with an empty list — reproduced in a fresh PRIVATE window, ruling out leftover localStorage — while Cloudflare (no CSP at all) showed the full tree; confirmed with the browser's own console via Safari's `do JavaScript`: "Refused to evaluate a string as JavaScript because 'unsafe-eval' ... is not an allowed source of script." | ✅ Implemented — `scripts/netlify_badge.py` (added `'unsafe-eval'` to the policy — a keyword independent of `'unsafe-inline'`, which is the one Netlify's own docs say the badge actually needs, so this does not let it back in). New test pins both facts: `'unsafe-eval'` present, `'unsafe-inline'` still absent. Full mac suite and `verify.sh` green. | **Shared Python — nothing to mirror.** Windows inherits this the moment `netlify_badge.py` is next synced. The lesson that travels: a blanket `script-src` restriction is only as safe as testing it against a REAL deploy through the built image, not just the pure-Python scanning logic in isolation — this is the second bug in a row (after row 301) that the working-tree-only unit test could not see. |
| 304 | 2026-08-21 | A course could only ever publish to ONE destination. Motivated by the Netlify ad-badge fix (row 301 on `issue/netlify-badge-suppression`): if a host has a problem, redundancy needs a second live copy already there, not a scramble to reconfigure one. This is piece 1 of 2 — config schema and the wizard/settings UI to configure additional destinations. **The Deploy button's behaviour has NOT changed yet** — it still publishes to `deployTarget` (the primary) only; piece 2 will make it publish to every configured destination. `CourseConfiguration` gains `additionalDeployTargets: [AdditionalDeployTarget]` (`{type, path}`), omitted from `course_config.json` entirely — never written as `[]` — for the overwhelming majority of courses that never touch this, so an untouched course writes the exact same file it always has. `PublishingChoiceView` (shared by the wizard and Course Settings) gained an "Also publish to, for redundancy" section: one toggle per known type that isn't the current primary, each showing the same detail fields (Cloudflare Account ID, folder picker) the primary would show for that type, factored into shared private subviews so the primary and additional cases can never drift apart. A destination can never be both primary and additional at once — `CourseConfiguration.pruningAdditionalTargets` is the one place that invariant lives, called from `deployTarget`'s own setter (for Course Settings, bound straight to the model) and from the picker's `onChange` (for the wizard's plain `@State`, not yet backed by a model) — plus defensively once more where the wizard actually writes the file. One of each of the three known types, maximum — never two of the same type. | ✅ Implemented — `CourseConfiguration.swift`, `PublishingChoiceView.swift`, `NewCourseWizardView.swift`, `CourseSettingsView.swift`. New `contracts/file-formats.json` entry and `documentation/08-course-config-reference.md` row for `additional_deploy_targets`. 14 new tests in `AdditionalDeployTargetsTests.swift` plus the existing `CloudflareDeployTests`/`FileFormatsContractTests` suites, all green (778 tests, 0 failures). | **Shared config format — Windows needs the schema, not (yet) any behaviour.** `windows-app/Plantoir.Core/Models/CourseConfiguration.cs` needs the equivalent `AdditionalDeployTargets` property with the identical omit-when-empty rule, and `windows-app/Plantoir/Views/PublishingChoiceView.cs` needs the equivalent toggle section — Windows has not implemented piece 2 either, so there is no urgency, but the two apps' course-settings forms should not disagree about what a `course_config.json` a Windows teacher wrote means when opened on the mac, or vice versa. Full design reasoning in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md. **Row-numbering collision, flagged rather than silently hidden:** this branch (`issue/multi-target-deploy`) forked from `dev` before `issue/netlify-badge-suppression` merged, so both branches independently claim row 304 for unrelated entries. Whichever merges second must renumber its new row to the next free number at merge time — a merge conflict on this file is expected and correct, not a mistake. |
| 305 | 2026-08-21 | Piece 2 of 304: **Deploy now publishes to every configured destination**, not only the primary. Three sentences added for the multi-destination outcomes (all succeeded / some failed, naming which / none succeeded); a course with one destination never sees them — its wording is byte-for-byte what `deployed(course:section:)` always said. Full mac suite green: 792 tests, 0 failures. | **Shared config format from row 304 was the easy half — this is the behavioural half Windows now needs too.** `windows-app/Plantoir.Core/Models/DeployCommand.cs` needs the same destination-aware split; a `MultiDestinationDeployRunner` equivalent needs to sequence `ScriptRunner` (or its C# analogue) per destination the same way; and the scheduled-deploy mechanism (if Windows has scheduling) needs the same "one line per destination, none chained, a failed build skips all, a failed destination doesn't skip the rest" shape. Full design reasoning — including why each destination gets its OWN runner rather than sharing one (the shared-runner design silently loses everything but the LAST destination's "Live URL:" line, since `ScriptRunner`'s URL-parsing only reads its own transcript) — is in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md. |
| 306 | 2026-08-21 | Row 305 built EACH destination its own `ScriptRunner` so no leg's "Live URL:" line could overwrite another's, but the DISPLAY still only ever showed one: the progress panel is bound to `deployRunner.activeRunner`, which after a run finishes is whichever leg ran LAST. Reported directly after a real Netlify + Cloudflare deploy: "only Cloudflare, the second deploy target, is visible" — the checklist above correctly showed both destinations checked off, but the body below only ever named and linked the last one, exactly as if the first deploy had never happened. New `DeployDestinationLinks` view lists every SUCCEEDED leg's own link (or, for a folder destination, its own "Show in Finder" button) once the whole run has finished; `TaskProgressView` gained a `hidesSiteLink` flag (default `false`, so every single-destination call site — including the new-course wizard's preview — is untouched) to skip its own one-leg link section for a multi-destination deploy, so the last destination's link is never shown twice. The panel's TITLE had the same bug one layer up — it kept naming "— Cloudflare Pages" even once every destination had finished, reading as though only that one had published — so `deployProgressTitle` now names the current destination only WHILE a multi-destination deploy is still running, and reverts to the plain single-destination title once done, letting the checklist and the new links list carry the per-destination detail instead. | ✅ Implemented — `DeployDestinationLinks.swift` (new), `TaskProgressView.swift` (`hidesSiteLink`), `SectionDetailView.swift` (`deployProgressTitle` refactored into a testable static function, consoleArea wiring). 5 new tests: `testSucceededKeepsOnlyFinishedAndSucceededLegs` / `testSucceededListsEveryDestinationThatActuallyPublished` in `MultiDestinationDeployRunnerTests.swift`, and three `deployProgressTitle` cases in `ConsoleFocusTests.swift` pinning the exact bug (title drops the destination name once the run is no longer active). Full mac suite green. Found and fixed while testing this branch combined with two others on a shared test branch, before any of the three reached `dev`. | **Shared architecture, not shared code.** If the Windows app's multi-destination deploy panel is similarly bound to whichever `ScriptRunner` ran last, it has the same gap: check whether its finished-state view shows every succeeded destination's own link, or only the last one's. The fix is the same shape — list every succeeded leg's link once the whole run is done, and stop naming a single destination in the title after the fact — not this Swift. Windows has not implemented row 305's behavioural piece yet either, so there is no urgency, but the same mistake is worth avoiding when it is. |
| 307 | 2026-08-21 | Row 306 fixed the multi-destination deploy PANEL only ever showing the last destination's live link — but "Advanced → Custom domain" in Course Settings had the identical bug one layer up, and worse: it was applied SILENTLY at deploy time rather than just misdisplayed. One section-wide domain was substituted onto EVERY destination's link, so a domain meant only for Netlify was also swapped onto the Cloudflare Pages leg's own address — a Cloudflare project that likely does not answer to that DNS at all. Reported directly, requesting exactly the shape row 306 already established: "ask the user which deploy target this applies to. Whatever target exists that is not using a custom domain should still show the default assigned to that service." `custom_domains.sections.sectionN` is now a map keyed by destination TYPE (`{"netlify": "...", "cloudflare_pages": "..."}`) rather than a bare string; `CourseSettingsView`'s "Advanced" section shows one field per destination that can even HAVE a domain (never a local folder — a domain is something a browser visits, and a folder is not), labelled just "Custom domain" for the overwhelming majority (exactly one destination, unchanged from before) and "`<Service>` custom domain" once there is more than one to tell apart. The caption ALSO used to hardcode "the Netlify address" regardless of what the course's actual destination was — a Cloudflare-only course was told to "leave empty to use the Netlify address," which was simply wrong; it now always names the real service. An OLDER bare-string value is still read correctly: as the domain for whichever destination is currently PRIMARY (`deployTarget`) — the only destination that could have set it — and setting a second destination's domain migrates that old value into the new shape rather than discarding it. `build_site.py`'s `resolve_section_domain()` (which decides the baseUrl baked into sitemap/RSS/social-card links — a single value shared by every destination this build uploads to, so it can only ever follow ONE domain) got the matching read: the new dict shape resolves through the PRIMARY destination's own entry, same as the Swift side's link display. A genuine, incidental bonus: the assistant's headless deploy path (`AssistSiteWork.swift`) was passing `customDomainForLinks: nil` UNCONDITIONALLY — the assistant never wore a teacher's custom domain even for a single-destination course — and now resolves it the same way the button does, since the parameter was removed from `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.run()` entirely in favour of resolving it per-leg, per-destination, inside the loop. | ✅ Implemented — `CourseConfiguration.swift` (`customDomain`/`setCustomDomain` gain a `destinationType` parameter and read/migrate the old shape), `SectionSettingsView.swift` (per-destination fields, `customDomainFieldLabel`/`customDomainCaption` static functions), `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.swift` (resolves the domain per leg, parameter removed), `SectionDetailView.swift`/`AssistSiteWork.swift` (updated call sites), `scripts/build_site.py` (`resolve_section_domain` reads the primary destination's own entry from the new shape). `contracts/file-formats.json`'s `custom_domains` entry rewritten to document the new shape and the migration rule. 10 new Swift tests (`CustomDomainTests.swift`, `SectionSettingsViewTests.swift`) plus 6 new Python tests (`scripts/test_build_site_domain_resolution.py`, wired into `verify.sh` against the real built image — it needs `frontmatter`, which lives only in the container, so it cannot join the fast host-side pre-checks). Full mac suite and `verify.sh` green. | **Shared config format AND a real cross-platform risk, not just a schema Windows hasn't caught up to yet.** `windows-app/Plantoir.Core/Models/CourseConfiguration.cs`'s `CustomDomain`/`SetCustomDomain` still read/write the bare-string shape unconditionally — `NestedString` degrades gracefully (returns `""`, not a crash) when it meets the new dict shape, so a Windows teacher opening a course a mac teacher has multi-destination-enabled would see their custom domain silently vanish, not error. The sharper risk is the WRITE side: Windows's `SetNestedValue` always writes a plain string, so a Windows teacher who edits and saves custom-domain settings on such a course would CLOBBER the whole per-destination map back down to one string, silently discarding any other destination's domain the mac side had configured — genuine data loss, not just a stale display. Windows needs the same per-destination-type shape and the same migration rule (an old bare string belongs to `deploy_target`) before it is safe for a course to move between the two apps once multi-destination deploy and a second custom domain are both in play. Full design reasoning in WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md. |
| 308 | 2026-08-21 | The "Show details" console during a multi-destination deploy is bound to `deployRunner.activeRunner` — whichever leg is CURRENT — so the moment a second destination started, the first destination's own console output vanished from view entirely, replaced by the second's (mostly empty) transcript. A course with exactly one destination (the overwhelming majority) passes `allLegs: nil` and sees byte-for-byte what it always has. | ✅ Implemented — `TaskConsoleView.swift` (`allLegs`, `combinedTranscriptText` static function), `TaskProgressView.swift` (threads `allLegs` through), `SectionDetailView.swift` (passes `deployRunner.legs` once there is more than one). 5 new tests in `TaskConsoleViewTests.swift` covering the fallback (nil / single-leg), the "Starting…" placeholder, the actual bug (both legs' output present, in order, once the second has started), and a not-yet-reached leg being left out. Full mac suite green: 814 tests, 0 failures. | **Shared architecture, not shared code.** If the Windows app's own multi-destination console is similarly bound to whichever runner is current, it has the same gap: concatenate every destination's own output that exists so far, under its own heading, rather than showing only the current one. Each destination's own caption-above-link pair stays left-aligned to itself, but the block of destinations as a whole is centred within the available width, per the request. | ✅ Implemented — `TaskProgressView.swift` (renders `DeployDestinationLinks` in place of the single-leg link section when `hidesSiteLink` is set, using the already-threaded `allLegs`), `DeployDestinationLinks.swift` (centred as a group via `.frame(maxWidth: .infinity, alignment: .center)`, redundant edge padding removed since it now sits inside `TaskProgressView`'s own padded container), `SectionDetailView.swift` (the standalone call site removed). Russell looked at the result and asked for the leading edge instead: "I don't like the centre alignment of the checkmarks or the final links and their labels. The wrapping `VStack` in `SectionDetailView.consoleArea` gained an explicit `alignment: .leading` (previously unset, so it silently defaulted to `.center`), and `DeployDestinationLinks` dropped the `.frame(maxWidth: .infinity, alignment: .center)` row 309 had given it — both now line up on the exact same edge as the panel's title, its Done/Failed status, and the single-destination "Your website is live" link, since all of that content already lived inside `TaskProgressView`'s own `alignment: .leading` VStack and never needed a fix. | ✅ Implemented — `SectionDetailView.swift` (explicit `alignment: .leading` on the wrapping `VStack`), `DeployDestinationLinks.swift` (dropped the centring `.frame`, kept `alignment: .leading` on its own `VStack`). | 311 | 2026-08-21 | A real Cloudflare deploy failed with wrangler's own `fetch failed` ("Please check your network connection and try again") and a bare Python traceback, in a working folder that had deployed successfully many times before — while a brand-new working folder deployed without a problem seconds later. Traced to the one difference: a long-lived container's own network namespace had independently wedged — its DNS resolution was completely broken (it could not even resolve `google.com`), while EVERY other container on the same Colima VM, including a fresh throwaway one built from the identical image, resolved instantly. This is a known Docker failure mode for long-lived containers, structurally separate from Colima's own health, the image, and this project's code — but a teacher hitting it would see exactly what was seen here: a bare traceback with no indication their internet connection was never actually the problem. `deploy.sh` gained `probe_container_network()`, run alongside the existing `probe_container_write()` health check whenever an existing container is about to be reused (skipped for `local_folder`, which needs no network at all): a container that cannot resolve Cloudflare's or Netlify's own API host — whichever this deploy actually needs — is silently recreated, the same way one with a broken or wrong mount already was. A teacher redeploying after months, or after their Mac slept for a long weekend, now gets a working container instead of a stack trace. | ✅ Implemented — `deploy.sh` (`probe_container_network`, wired into both branches of the existing container-reuse decision). `verify.sh`'s generic "every helper called in a launcher is defined" cross-check covers the new function structurally; the actual recreate path mirrors `probe_container_write`'s own proven, long-running shape, which has no dedicated test of its own either — reproducing a genuinely wedged container network namespace on demand is not something a test harness can do reliably. Full `verify.sh` green. | **Native Windows has no container at all to wedge** (row 292 — "Windows: the container is gone", no WSL2, no Docker) — this entire class of failure is Colima-specific and does not exist there; nothing to port. Worth knowing anyway, as the kind of thing worth a quick sanity check if a native Windows deploy is ever reported failing with a bare network-looking error after a machine has been asleep or off a network for a long time — the equivalent question there is whether Windows' own native networking has anything comparable, not whether a container has gone stale. |

| 313 | 2026-08-22 | On Windows, the Download button in Settings for the local assistant did nothing at all — the click handler was an empty comment where the call should have been. Fixed properly rather than patched: a naive wire-up would have reintroduced the mac's own already-solved double-download bug (row 219), since Windows' Settings dialog and its assistant windows each ran their own independent download with no shared state. Ported the mac's shared-store architecture instead — one store per model rung, shared app-wide, so a download started in Settings is visible (and is the SAME download, not a competing one) from an open assistant window, and vice versa. Settings now shows a live progress bar and a Stop button while downloading, matching the mac's own housekeeping rows. Verified against a real Hugging Face download end to end (Download → live progress → Downloaded → Remove → back to Not Downloaded), and an adversarial audit caught a real regression before it shipped: closing an assistant window used to cancel a shared download unconditionally, even one Settings had started — the identical bug row 219 already named and fixed on the mac. Fixed the same way, with the mac's own `startedTheDownload` pattern. | ✅ Implemented — `Plantoir.Core/Assist/AssistModelStore.cs` (new, ports `AssistModelStore.swift`/`AssistModelStores.swift`), `Plantoir/Views/AssistantSettingsDialog.cs`, `Plantoir/Views/AssistWindow.xaml.cs`. 12 new tests (`AssistModelStoreTests.cs`) plus a real, pre-existing test-parallelization bug found and fixed along the way (`LocalModelTests.cs` gained a shared xUnit collection, mirroring `ModelTests.cs`'s existing `SharedActivityState` pattern). Full suite: 620 tests, 620 passed. | Mac-authored architecture, ported here — no further Windows work implied by this row. Full reasoning, the audit's findings, and what was assessed-and-deliberately-left-alone (two low-severity races, neither causing data loss) in `MAC-HANDOFF.md`'s "Done" ledger. |
| 314 | 2026-08-22 | A section window had no way of saying that its pages had moved on since they were last published. Requested directly, by analogy with Pages' own title bar: "if for a selected section of a course, any page the section uses (exclusively) or a page it shares (with other sections) has changed since the last deploy — then an \" — Edited\" marker might appear beside the course and section name in the window title bar," and explicitly "without impacting performance." Nothing anywhere recorded when a section had last been published, so that had to come first: `MultiDestinationDeployRunner` now writes `courses/<CODE>/.publish_state/section<N>.json` — a content fingerprint, a timestamp, and which destinations it went to — and ONLY when every configured destination succeeded, since a course publishing to two hosts one of which failed has not published and must keep its marker. The fingerprint is of the file INVENTORY (each file's relative path, size and modification date, sorted and hashed) rather than of file CONTENTS: no page is ever read, so the check is one directory walk asking for two resource values, and it can run every time a window comes to the front. Two alternatives were rejected and the reasons are worth keeping: hashing contents would read every byte of every page for a marker nobody asked to be exact, and a plain "is anything newer than the publish date" comparison — cheaper still — cannot see a DELETION, which is precisely the unpublished change a teacher would most want flagged. The set of files counted is derived from what is on DISK (everything non-hidden under the course folder except other sections' `section<M>/` folders, `node_modules`, the legacy `merged_output`, `.DS_Store`, and the `course_config.backup.json` / `.json.tmp` pair `build_site.py` writes at publish time) rather than from `course_config.json`'s `shared_folders` / `per_section_folders` lists, because the build DISCOVERS new folders at publish time and appends them to those lists afterwards — reading the lists would leave a folder made this morning invisible to the marker until the next publish, which is the very publish the marker exists to prompt. `course_config.json` itself counts: fonts, the sidebar and the coverage map are inputs to the built site. The fingerprint is taken when the FIRST upload begins rather than when the last one ends, so a page edited during a publish that takes minutes is not wrongly marked as having gone out. A section that has NEVER been published shows no marker at all — there is nothing to have been edited since, and a marker that is always on for every new course is one nobody reads. One accepted imprecision, documented in the code and in the contract: a shared, course-level page marks EVERY section edited, even though editing only `publishForSection3` in fact changes only section 3's site — being exact would mean parsing the frontmatter of every shared page on every check, and the marker is early rather than wrong. | ✅ Implemented — `SectionPublishState.swift` (new: the fingerprint, the stamp, the title), `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.swift` (records what went out, once every destination succeeded), `SectionDetailView.swift` (`titleText` composes the marker; refreshed only when the window arrives, the app comes to the front, this window becomes key, or a run finishes — a body that recomputed it would walk the course folder every time a console line arrived, and the walk itself runs off the main thread), `ActivityTrail.swift` (new `sectionContentMarkedPublished` event). `contracts/app-rules.json` gains an authored `publishedFreshness` block — the marker's text, the seven `whenShown` cases, sixteen `filesCounted` paths and the four `whenRecorded` rules — and `contracts/shared-rules.json` the trail event. 14 new tests (`SectionPublishStateTests.swift`), the contract-driven ones run through the REAL walk rather than through the filter function. **An adversarial review of the first cut found five defects and all five are fixed here, which is worth recording because four of them failed in the direction that lies to the teacher.** (1) A symlinked page, or anything inside a symlinked folder, contributed NOTHING — a directory enumerator neither follows a link nor reports it as a regular file — so a `Media` folder symlinked into an Obsidian vault, the arrangement `build_site.py`'s own `_ensure_media_symlink` exists for, read as permanently up to date; links are now resolved by hand, one hop. (2) A scheduled overnight deploy never goes through `MultiDestinationDeployRunner` — launchd runs a generated shell script — so the flagship "publish tomorrow's class" feature published perfectly and left the window saying " — Edited" forever; the script now tracks each destination's own result and writes a sentinel naming where it went, and the app (which already runs the script, for permissions reasons) fingerprints before and records after. (3) The fingerprint was taken after the shared BUILD while the contract said before it — the build is the longest part of a publish, so a page edited during it was stamped as published; the capture moved above the build, accepting that a publish whose preflight discovers a new folder now ends still marked edited, which is true and self-heals at the next publish. (4) A course whose "publish to a folder on this computer" destination points inside its own course folder fed its own marker permanently, since `deploy.py` writes the whole site there; that path is excluded. (5) `NSWindow.didBecomeKeyNotification` fires for every window in the app, so overlapping walks could land out of order and re-assert " — Edited" about a section that had just published; a generation counter drops stale answers. Also fixed: a stamp that FAILS to write now leaves a trail line, which is the case the trail event was added for. One accepted false negative is now written down rather than discovered later: restoring a page from a backup that preserves its modification date, without changing its length, reads as up to date — the price of never reading file contents, and publishing is never blocked by the marker. Full mac suite green: 828 tests, 0 failures. | **Portable in full — the rule is data, and the file format is shared.** `.publish_state/section<N>.json` is written by whichever app publishes, so a section published on the mac must read as up to date on Windows and vice versa; the fingerprint algorithm therefore has to match to the character (sorted `relativePath|size|microseconds-since-epoch` lines joined with `\n`, SHA-256, lowercase hex) or every cross-machine publish will look like an edit. Run `publishedFreshness.filesCounted` against your own path filter first — it is the half most likely to drift, and the `.publish_state` and `course_config.backup.json` exclusions are load-bearing rather than tidy: counting either makes every publish end by declaring the section edited. Two things are NOT inherited and must be built there: the title-bar composition (WinUI titles are set on the window, not by a `navigationTitle` modifier) and the refresh triggers — the mac uses `NSWindow.didBecomeKeyNotification` and `NSApplication.didBecomeActiveNotification`, and Windows wants its own `Activated` equivalents. Do NOT reach for a file-system watcher: none exists on either side today, the marker only matters at the moment somebody looks at the title bar, and an FSEvents/`FileSystemWatcher` stream over a course folder is a cost paid continuously for an answer wanted occasionally. |

| 317 | 2026-08-22 | Reported by Russell, from a screen recording: deploying a section that had just been previewed briefly showed "Stopped" in the console before the deploy progress panel took over. Repro: deploy a section (fine), preview the same section (fine), deploy it again — a flash of "Stopped" appears first. | ✅ Fixed. Deploying stops any running preview first (`SectionDetailView.stopPreviewAndWait()` → `previewRunner.stopByUser()`), which sets `wasStoppedByUser` and is exactly what makes `TaskProgressView` render "Stopped" — correct when a teacher presses Stop Preview, wrong here since it is an internal step of starting a deploy. The console picks which panel to show (`showsDeployProgress`) by comparing `previewRunner.startedAt` against `deployRunner.startedAt`, and `deployRunner.startedAt` was not set until `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.run()` actually began — which happens only after the preview is stopped and awaited, plus `CourseActivity.beginPublish` bookkeeping. In that window neither runner was "running", so the comparison fell back to timestamps, `previewRunner.startedAt` (just refreshed when preview started) beat the deploy's stale `startedAt` from the PREVIOUS deploy, and the console kept showing the preview panel — now saying "Stopped" — until the real run finally began. Fix: `deployAndWait()` now claims the console immediately, before touching the preview runner — setting `deployRunner.startedAt = Date()` and resetting `legs`/`wasCancelled`/`wasStoppedByUser` to the fresh state `run()` would set moments later — so `showsDeployProgress` flips to the deploy panel right away instead of drifting back to the just-stopped preview one. The reset legs matter as much as the timestamp: without it, the deploy panel would flash the PREVIOUS deploy's finished outcome (its own stale success or failure) for that same beat, which is no more honest than "Stopped" was. `stopPreviewAndWait()`/`stopByUser()`'s behaviour is untouched — the teacher-facing "Stop Preview" button still shows "Stopped" exactly as before. Full mac suite green: 829 tests, 0 failures. | **Shared model, nothing to mirror mechanically — but the SAME race is worth checking for on Windows.** Whatever view model decides which of two side-by-side progress panels (preview vs. deploy) to show, by comparing "most recently started", is vulnerable the moment starting one task involves silently stopping the other first: claim the panel for the NEW task before doing any of that stopping, not after. If Windows' deploy path also calls something equivalent to `stopByUser()` on a running preview as an implementation detail (rather than as a teacher action), make sure that internal stop doesn't set the same status the user-facing Stop button sets, or reset the deploy side's UI-bound "last outcome" state before it can be shown mid-transition. |
| 318 | 2026-08-22 | Reported by Russell, from a second screen recording, as part of the same issue as row 317: with the "Stopped" flash gone, two more glitches were visible on the same deploy-after-preview sequence. (1) Right after clicking Deploy, the console went completely blank — nothing but the bare "Show details" disclosure triangle — for about half a second before real progress appeared. (2) Partway through a deploy that needed a fresh build, the panel briefly said "Done" with a green checkmark, then reverted to "Connecting to Netlify… Step 6 of 8" for several more seconds before actually finishing. | ✅ Both fixed, two separate root causes. (1) Row 317's fix claimed the console for the deploy panel by resetting `deployRunner.legs` to fresh, empty `ScriptRunner`s the instant Deploy is pressed — which stopped the stale "Stopped" panel from showing, but a brand-new, never-run `ScriptRunner` (`isRunning == false`, `lastExitCode == nil`) is a state `TaskProgressView` has no rendering for at all: neither its "running" branch nor its "finished" branch fires, so everything except the unconditional "Show details" row went blank. Replaced with a `SectionDetailView.isPreparingDeploy` flag, true only for the span between pressing Deploy and `deployRunner.run()` actually starting (stopping any preview, `CourseActivity.beginPublish`, the refusal checks) — `consoleArea` shows a plain "Preparing to deploy…" placeholder for that span instead of binding `TaskProgressView` to a runner with nothing to say yet, and the Deploy button now also disables itself on this flag (it previously stayed clickable through this whole window, which was a second, real bug in its own right: a click there could start a second overlapping deploy stomping on the same `deployRunner.legs`/`startedAt`; `deployAndWait()` gained the same guard for the assistant's path, which has no button to disable). (2) `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.run()`, for a leg that needs a build, runs `preview.sh --build-only` and `deploy.sh` back to back on the SAME `ScriptRunner` — `await runner.waitUntilFinished()` polled `isRunning` every 300ms, so between the build process actually exiting (a real `isRunning = false`, `lastExitCode = 0` — indistinguishable from the WHOLE leg being done) and the poll noticing and immediately starting the deploy script, the console could show "Done" for up to that long. Fixed two ways: `waitUntilFinished()` is now event-driven (a `CheckedContinuation` resumed from `finishRun()` the instant the process exits) rather than polled, collapsing that gap by itself; and a new `ScriptRunner.isBetweenPhases` flag, set by the caller only for a runner about to be handed a second script the moment the first one succeeds, tells `TaskProgressView` to keep showing progress rather than reading a real-but-not-final exit code as finished — reset automatically by `run()` itself, so every other caller is unaffected. Full mac suite green: 829 tests, 0 failures. | **All three pieces are shared-shaped, worth checking on Windows even though nothing here is shared code.** (a) If Windows' own preview/deploy panel binds to whatever runner object is "current," check what it renders for a freshly-constructed, never-started one — .NET's default-initialized state may differ from Swift's, but the question ("what does my progress view draw when nothing has happened yet") is the same one that caused the blank panel here. (b) A Deploy button that stays enabled through async prep work before the "real" busy flag goes true is a general trap, not a SwiftUI one — check WinUI's own Deploy button disables itself for the FULL span from click to real work starting, not just from when the underlying process object reports running. (c) If Windows' own build-then-deploy sequence reuses one process wrapper for two scripts, check whether its own "wait for exit" mechanism is polled (same latency-window risk) or event-driven, and whether its progress view can tell "finished, but a second script is queued right behind it" apart from "actually done." |
| 319 | 2026-08-20 | The assistant's first turn does not wait for its warm-up. `AssistSession` announced `.ready` — all `canSend` checked — and only THEN awaited the ~3,400-token priming request, so a teacher who typed straight away queued behind it on the engine's single slot (`--parallel 1`). Deferred out of v1.1.0 on purpose (row 295); this is the deferred half. | ✅ Done, and **it is not the speed fix it looks like — say so before anybody quotes a number**. `hasFinishedWarmUp` is set in a `defer` inside `warmUp(...)` so it opens however the priming request ends, and `canSend` requires it; `readiness` still becomes `.ready` first, so the box takes the keyboard throughout and only SENDING waits. The arithmetic from row 295 settles what it buys: 1.7 s warm against 3.1 s racing, and **3.1 ≈ 1.4 + 1.7 is the signature of two requests strictly serialised on one slot** — so the 1.4 s is MOVED out of the answer into the wait, not saved. Measured from the window opening, the total is unchanged. What it actually buys: nothing depends on how the engine behaves with two requests in flight; and the trail's `assistant chose a tool` seconds become comparable on the first turn, which matters because routing has no automated gate anywhere and that figure is the only place a regression shows. **The trap, and it is most of the work**: gating `canSend` alone leaves a dead send button for ~2 s (small) or ~12 s (large), so a fast typist's first Return vanishes — the same failure the composer's arrow keys already have a rule about. So `waitUntilWarmedUp()` parks callers on a continuation (released from `finish()` too, or a window closed mid-warm-up strands them), `sendOrHold()` holds exactly one send and reads the box AFTER the wait so text typed meanwhile goes with it, and the send button shows a spinner while `isWarmingUp`. Pinned by `AssistWarmUpTests`, which drives a new `beginConversation(baseURL:)` — split out of `startEngine()` because that spawns a real engine and cannot be tested — against a stub engine on a real socket that holds its answer; removing the gate fails it. **Rejected**: cancelling the warm-up on send, which is the version that would genuinely save time, because the only saving left by then is the priming request's remaining generation (the prompt evaluation, which is the expensive part, is already spent). **Verified by driving the real app** (MCV4U section 1, small assistant): the send button shows the spinner while the engine reads its tool definitions and turns back into the arrow when it is done, and a Return pressed the instant the box came alive was NOT swallowed — it went through and was answered ("Found 244 pages in MCV4U Section 1."). | Windows already awaits its warm-up, so the gate itself is inherited — but **check `AssistWindow.xaml.cs` for the dead-button trap**: if the send affordance is disabled during priming with nothing held and nothing shown, a fast typist's first Return is swallowed once per window, which reproduces for nobody and gets reported as "it ignored me". Hold the send rather than refusing it, read the box after the wait, and show that something is happening. And do not carry the 1.4 s across as a speed claim — the arithmetic above says it is a queue, not a cost. |
| 320 | 2026-08-20 | A mac problem report can carry nothing the engine said. `AssistServerHost` sent `llama-server`'s stdout and stderr to `FileHandle.nullDevice`, so model-load errors, slot warnings and timing lines reached nobody — and a report from a teacher whose assistant is misbehaving is the one place they would matter. Windows added `NoteServerLine` for this. Deferred out of v1.1.0 (row 295); this is the deferred half. | ✅ Done, **and deliberately not with a pipe**. `nullDevice` was load-bearing, not laziness: a redirected pipe nobody drains fills up and the engine blocks on its next log write, mid-request, looking exactly like a hung model — the wedge Windows had to fix, and discarding the output is why the mac never had it. Both streams now go to ONE FILE in the temporary directory and a bounded tail is read **when somebody asks**, never on the engine's timetable. `AssistServerHost.lines(in:since:atMost:)` takes the mark by reference so each look reports only what is new, reads at most the recent 64 KB, drops the part-line skipping ahead lands on, and resets a mark left past the end of a shrunken file; `stop()` closes the handle but LEAVES the file, because the never-became-ready path calls it before anybody has looked and the reason is the last thing in there. **The filter is narrow and the narrowness was measured** against the bundled engine on this Mac (llama.cpp b10435, Qwen2.5-1.5B): lines carry a severity letter as their second field, so `E` is the signal, and **warnings are NOT recorded because a healthy start prints six of them** — five a CORS block that cannot matter on a loopback-only server, one a token-type quirk in the weights. A word test (`error`, `exception`, `failed`, `failure`) sits beside it as a fallback and is what catches `got exception: … parse error`; both provoked lines are caught, including `E srv send_error: … request (20030 tokens) exceeds the available context size (8192 tokens)`. Sampled at three moments: the engine never becoming ready (filter OFF — then every line is the diagnosis), every 15 s while the window is open (teardown-only would have missed the case this is FOR, a teacher reporting while it is still misbehaving), and on `finish()`. **Capped at twelve lines a conversation** and cut to 200 characters, because the trail's failure mode is the one line that mattered ending up on page forty. Redacted on the way in like everything else, which matters here — the engine prints the model's full path. Pinned by `AssistEngineLogTests`, including a source scan for `Pipe(`/`readabilityHandler`, since every other test in the file would pass with the wedge back in. **Verified end to end against the real app**, which is the only way to know the wiring holds: a healthy conversation left the trail untouched, and an over-long prompt produced `the assistant's engine said: 0.56.869.873 E srv send_error: task id = 114, error: request (11965 tokens) exceeds the available context size (8192 tokens), try increasing it` about six seconds later. The line above it is what a report used to say on its own — `could not answer — The assistant's engine answered with an error (400)` — which is exactly the uninformative sentence this exists to explain. The temporary log folder was empty after quitting, so `discardEngineLog()` cleans up. | **The contract gained `assistant engine said`, so `Plantoir.Tests` goes red until `Plantoir.Core`'s event list gains it** — a request, not damage; see `MAC-HANDOFF.md`. The hard half is already yours: `LocalModel.NoteServerLine` / `RecentServerLog` keep a 60-line ring buffer of exactly this output and nothing puts any of it on the trail. Add the event, sample at the three moments above, reuse the filter — **but re-measure the healthy-start noise on your own engine build first.** Vulkan and CPU builds print different startup lines from the Metal one, and the entire reason warnings are excluded here is a specific set of six lines that may not be your six. Do NOT switch the mac to a pipe to match: writing to a file is what makes the no-blocking-read property structural rather than a promise about always having a reader attached. |
| 321 | 2026-08-22 | On Windows, publishing a page right after a preview had been running could dump the whole build log into the assistant's chat reply — reported from a real cycle ("preview", "Unpublish Unit 4, Day 20", "Publish Unit 4, Day 20") where only the final Publish leaked, with a request to show "Working…" then "Published" instead. Root cause: `AssistAgent.RunTool` already sends `preview: false` for `publish_pages`/`unpublish_pages`/`publish_class_on`, with a doc comment saying the server would then build nothing and the app's own visible rebuild would follow — but `AssistWorkspace.Apply`, the single-page publish path, had no `preview` parameter at all, so it built a preview on the server regardless, racing the app's visible one for the same output folder. Repro: Course Settings, primary stays Netlify, flip the Cloudflare Pages redundancy toggle on — Save never re-enables. | ✅ Fixed on Windows. `PublishingChoiceView.Problem` (gating `SaveButton.IsEnabled`) correctly requires a valid Cloudflare Account ID for an additional Cloudflare target the same way it does for a primary one, but the ONLY place on screen to type that ID was inside the primary Cloudflare block — visible only when the primary destination IS Cloudflare — and the additional-target row showed nothing but a note reading "Uses the same Cloudflare Account ID as above, enter it there if you haven't already."
