| 323 | 2026-08-22 | Row 314 (mac) named a scheduled overnight deploy on Windows as still leaving the " — Edited" title-bar marker wrong: a scheduled deploy that succeeded perfectly still said "— Edited" until the teacher published again by hand, because `TaskScheduling`'s scheduled task runs `powershell.exe` directly — no app process is alive at the moment the deploy actually happens to fingerprint the section or write `.publish_state`. Flagged in `WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md` as genuinely outstanding, not attempted at the time "since it touches shared launcher mechanics rather than app code." | ✅ Fixed. Mirrors the mac's launchd fix (row 314's item 2) with a Windows-shaped middle step: `TaskScheduling.Schedule` now ALWAYS writes a wrapper `.ps1` (previously only for 2+ destinations), which fingerprints the section BEFORE running any destination's `deploy.ps1` — right before the deploy actually happens, matching the mac's ordering, not at the moment the teacher scheduled it — then, only if every destination succeeds, writes a JSON sentinel under `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Plantoir\scheduled\pending\`. New `ScheduledDeployCompletion.ConsumePending()` applies any pending sentinel (stamps `.publish_state`, notes the trail, reuses `SectionContentMarkedPublished`) and deletes it either way, so a run that failed to record cannot be mistaken for tomorrow's success; wired into `MainWindow`'s `Activated` handler, subscribed before any `SectionDetailView` exists so it runs before that view's own marker refresh on the same activation. **The hard part**: the wrapper script cannot call C# to fingerprint (no app process is alive), so a THIRD copy of the fingerprint algorithm was written — `scripts/section_fingerprint.py`, run via the app's own bundled Python — proven byte-for-byte equal to `SectionPublishState.Fingerprint` by `SectionFingerprintPythonParityTests` (ordinary files, the self-publishing exclusion, an empty course), which actually runs the comparison against the real bundled runtime in this checkout rather than trusting a hand-read of both. **Rejected**: fingerprinting at SCHEDULE time (in C#, trivially) instead of at RUN time (in Python) — cheaper, but wrong in the direction that lies to the teacher: an edit made between scheduling and the overnight run would still go out correctly, but the stamp would record the STALE pre-edit fingerprint, so the marker would say "— Edited" about content that, in fact, had just published. `TaskScheduling.Schedule`'s signature changed from a pre-built argument-list parameter to `(courseDirectory, destinations, cloudflareAccountID)` directly, so it can compute the exclusion list, the arguments, and the destination names/types itself — both call sites (`SidebarPane.xaml.cs`, `PlantoirTools.cs`) updated. `ScheduledDeployCompletionTests` (5 tests: stamps + deletes the sentinel, notes the trail, survives a malformed sentinel, skips a vanished course but still deletes its sentinel, no-op with nothing pending). Full Windows suite: 649 tests, 648 passed, 1 pre-existing unrelated failure (`SharedRules_ActivityTrailEvents_Exist`, confirmed failing identically before this change — see row 322). Built for x64 (`PT - Dev`). | **Mac has no equivalent gap to close** — its launchd agent launches the app binary itself, so it always could fingerprint in-process; this row is Windows catching up to what row 314 already described as the mac's OWN fix for the identical bug. Nothing to port. Worth knowing for awareness: `scripts/section_fingerprint.py` is now a third, Windows-only-invoked copy of the fingerprint algorithm living in the SHARED `scripts/` folder — if the algorithm's rules ever change (which files count, the symlink one-hop resolution, the sort order), all three copies (Swift, C#, this Python file) need the same edit, and only the Windows one has a test proving it stayed in sync automatically. See `MAC-HANDOFF.md` for the full write-up. |
| 324 | 2026-08-23 | Reported by Russell with a screenshot: "can't schedule a deploy when (as you can see) I just ran a deploy for that section." Repro: deploy a section that has never gone out before (Netlify), watch it succeed with a live URL, then try Schedule a deploy — the dialog still refuses with "has never been deployed, so deploying it asks what to call the website," even though it plainly just went out and has a URL on screen. | ✅ Fixed — a real, pre-existing bug in SHARED `scripts/deploy.py`, not in anything from row 323. `main()` derived the course directory (for reading and writing the Netlify/Cloudflare site marker under `.netlify_sites/`/`.cloudflare_sites/`) by climbing two levels from the built section's own folder — `section_dir.parent.parent`, assuming the shape `.../<COURSE>/.merged_output/section#`. That shape holds on the mac (and in the old container), but Windows' native `PLANTOIR_BUILD_ROOT` (row 290 — builds moved OUT of the working folder so OneDrive never chokes on them) makes `toolchain_paths.merged_output_root()` skip the `.merged_output` nesting entirely, so the built section sits only ONE level below the build root, not two — climbing two landed on the build root's own PARENT, not the course. The site marker was silently written there instead: confirmed on Russell's own machine, a `.netlify_sites/section1.json` sitting under `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Plantoir\builds\<id>\` rather than under `courses\ICD2O\`, holding a real, live Netlify site record. Two consequences, both silent: `HasDeployedBefore` always read false for a section that plainly had deployed (blocking Schedule, exactly as reported), and every deploy — not just the first — created a BRAND NEW Netlify site rather than reusing the one from last time, since the marker was never found at read time either (visible in the random `-pspsps` site-name suffixes accumulating across repeated deploys of what should be the same section). Fixed by deriving `course_dir = COURSES_ROOT / args.course` directly, unambiguous regardless of where the build output lives — `COURSES_ROOT` already existed as a module-level alias. Two new pure-stdlib tests (`scripts/test_deploy_course_dir_resolution.py`, wired into `verify.sh`) pin the structural mismatch itself (`merged_output_root()` under a simulated `PLANTOIR_BUILD_ROOT` does NOT let `section_dir.parent.parent` land back on the course) and the fix (`COURSES_ROOT / code` does, unconditionally). Russell's own affected working folder was repaired directly: the misplaced `.netlify_sites/section1.json` moved from the build-root folder into `courses/ICD2O/`, and the working folder's mirrored `.toolchain/scripts/deploy.py` copy updated in place so the fix takes effect on the next deploy without waiting for the app's next `.toolchain` refresh. Windows app rebuilt for x64 (`PT - Dev`); no C# changed, so nothing there needed a rebuild strictly, but the app's own bundled `scripts/` copy (`Content Include` in `Plantoir.csproj`) picks up the fix on the next build regardless. | **Shared code, not a Windows-only fix — the mac's own copy of `deploy.py` needed the identical change**, even though the mac was never actually AFFECTED (its `merged_output_root()` still nests `.merged_output` since `PLANTOIR_BUILD_ROOT` is a Windows-only environment variable, so `section_dir.parent.parent` has always landed correctly there). Nothing for the mac app itself to do — the shared script already carries the fix — but see `MAC-HANDOFF.md` for why the structural assumption was fragile in the first place and worth knowing about if `merged_output_root()`'s shape ever changes again on either side. |
| 325 | 2026-08-23 | Reported directly, after a real overnight scheduled deploy for ICD2O never fired: "1. We should add a clock icon on the trailing edge of the section marker in the sidebar when a scheduled deploy is active... 2. It should be possible to right-click and modify or cancel a scheduled deploy using an appropriate dialog box. 3. The scheduled deploy I had set up did not fire." | ✅ **Item 1 was already there** — `SidebarRow.BadgeGlyph`/`BadgeVisibility`/`BadgeTooltip` and the `SidebarPane.xaml` `FontIcon` binding shown right after the section title already do exactly this; nothing to add. **Item 2, half fixed**: right-click already offered Cancel; now also offers **Change Deploy Time (h:mm tt)…**, reusing the same dialog `AskWhenToDeploy` now takes an `existing: DateTime?` — pre-filling the day/time pickers with the CURRENT schedule rather than tomorrow 6:30, retitled "Change the scheduled deploy" / "Save" instead of "Schedule a deploy" / "Schedule" when modifying. No new backend needed: `TaskScheduling.Schedule` already replaces by task name (one per section, by construction), so "modify" is just this same dialog, pre-filled, calling Schedule again. **Item 3 was the real bug, and a serious one**: `TaskScheduling`'s stored `/TR` command built the `-File` argument with `\\\"` (a literal backslash followed by a quote — TWO characters) instead of a real embedded quote character, so `schtasks /Query ... /XML` showed `<Arguments>` holding `\"C:\...\script.ps1\"` verbatim (backslash and quote both literal, plus a stray newline) — a path PowerShell's `-File` could never resolve. **This was not new** — it predates row 323 entirely and would have broken EVERY scheduled deploy on Windows, ever, for any course, since the very first version of this feature; row 323's refactor to always use a wrapper script just carried the same broken quoting forward unnoticed, because nobody had actually let a real one fire and checked the result before. Confirmed on Russell's own machine: the real ICD2O task's `Last Run Time` was populated (it DID run, at the scheduled moment) but `Last Result` was `0xFFFD0000` — Task Scheduler ran `powershell.exe`, handed it a garbage path, and it failed silently, hours later, with nobody watching. Fixed by using a real `\"` in C# source (which the compiler turns into one actual quote character) rather than `\\\"` (backslash-then-quote as two literal characters) — verified empirically by hand-registering a real scheduled task with both the broken and fixed command strings and reading back the stored XML (`<Arguments>` clean either way, but only the fixed version resolves to the actual file when PowerShell parses it), then scheduling a real one-minute-out task with the fix and confirming it fired with `Last Result: 0`. Extracted into `TaskScheduling.TaskRunCommand(scriptPath)` (`internal`, `InternalsVisibleTo` already wired to `Plantoir.Tests`) so the quoting can be pinned directly without spinning up a real scheduled task — two new tests, `TaskSchedulingTests.cs`. Full suite: 647 total, 646 passed, the one pre-existing unrelated failure (row 322/324). Windows app rebuilt for x64 (`PT - Dev`). | **Windows-only mechanics — nothing to port.** The mac's equivalent (`launchd`) builds its command as an ARGUMENTS ARRAY in a plist, not a shell command string assembled with manual quote-escaping, so this exact class of bug (a doubled backslash turning an intended quote character into two literal characters) has no equivalent surface there. Worth knowing as a general lesson for any future shell-command-string construction on either side: when a value is already going to be quoted once by the process-launching layer (here, `ProcessStartInfo.ArgumentList`, which quotes and escapes a value containing spaces automatically), do not ALSO hand-escape quotes inside that value — the two escaping passes compound instead of cancelling out, and the result looks plausible in a debugger (a C# string literal that "reads like" an escaped quote) right up until something tries to actually use it. See `MAC-HANDOFF.md` for the full write-up. |
| 326 | 2026-08-23 | Reported directly, right after row 325 shipped: "There is no clock next to the section name once a deploy is scheduled. I just scheduled one. There is no way to know a deploy is scheduled as a result! There is no way to modify or cancel an existing deploy." — from the taskbar-pinned build, in an already-open window. | ✅ Fixed — row 325 fixed the SCHEDULING bug but missed that the badge and the right-click menu it added never actually reflect a schedule made while the window is already open, for two compounding reasons. **(1) The value itself was stale.** `ReconcileSections` only read `TaskScheduling.NextRun(...)` into `ScheduledDeploy` inside the "row does not exist yet" branch — on every subsequent `Refresh()` pass, an EXISTING section row (the normal case, since the window was already open when the teacher scheduled) kept whatever `ScheduledDeploy` was true at the moment the row was first created, almost always `null`. Moved the read outside the create-only branch so it happens on every pass. **(2) Even with a fresh value, the UI would not have shown it.** `SidebarRow` is a plain class with no change notification, and `x:Bind` — unlike classic `Binding` — defaults to `Mode=OneTime`: it evaluates once when a row's container is first created and never again, which is deliberate elsewhere in this same file (rows are RECONCILED, never recreated, specifically so WinUI's `TreeView` never tears down a container and loses expansion state — the exact mechanism the comment above `Refresh()` already documents for a different bug, row 172's "Courses & Clubs kept folding up"). So `Visibility="{x:Bind BadgeVisibility}"` and `ContextFlyout="{x:Bind Menu}"` were captured once, at row creation, and never looked again — meaning even a same-session Cancel or a freshly-fixed schedule from row 325 would have shown the badge, or the up-to-date menu, only by accident (a row created fresh AFTER the schedule already existed). Fixed properly: `SidebarRow` now implements `INotifyPropertyChanged`; its `Menu` and `ScheduledDeploy` are real properties that raise change notification (`ScheduledDeploy`'s setter also raises it for the two DERIVED properties the badge actually binds to — `BadgeVisibility`, `BadgeTooltip` — since `x:Bind` subscribes to the exact property path named in the binding, not to whatever it's computed from); and the three affected bindings (`ContextFlyout`, `Visibility`, `ToolTipService.ToolTip` on the badge) gained `Mode=OneWay`. Also added: `MainWindow`'s `Activated` handler now calls `Sidebar.Refresh()` (guarded on `Workspace.State == WorkspaceState.Ready`), matching the established "— Edited" marker pattern, so a deploy scheduled through the assistant or from a different window is picked up the next time this window comes to the front rather than staying invisible until something else happens to trigger a reload. Verified by reading the fix against both root causes directly (both are textbook, unambiguous WinUI defaults — no speculation); attempted a live on-screen verification via synthetic mouse input but the automation harness available in this environment could not reliably land a right-click on the tree row, so that pass is inconclusive rather than confirming — recommend trying it live. Full suite: 647 total, 646 passed, the one known pre-existing unrelated failure. Windows app rebuilt for x64 (`PT - Dev`). | **Windows-only mechanics (WinUI's `x:Bind` default), nothing to port** — SwiftUI's own `@Observable`/`@State` machinery does not have an equivalent "silently stale until the container is recreated" failure mode; a SwiftUI view re-renders whenever an `@Observable` property it reads changes, full stop. Worth knowing as a general WinUI lesson for anywhere else in this app a row/item object is RECONCILED (mutated in place across a `Refresh()`-style pass) rather than recreated: any `x:Bind` on a property that can change after the container exists needs BOTH `Mode=OneWay` in the XAML AND `INotifyPropertyChanged` raised for that exact property name (and for any property DERIVED from it that XAML binds to directly) — `x:Bind`'s default is silence, not staleness that eventually resolves itself. See `MAC-HANDOFF.md` for the general note. |
| 327 | 2026-08-23 | Port the mac's "assistant engine said" activity-trail event to Windows — `WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md` item 2 of the still-outstanding list: sample the local engine's own stderr/stdout onto the trail so a problem report from a teacher whose assistant is misbehaving carries something the engine itself said, rather than nothing (both streams previously went nowhere a report could reach). | ✅ Implemented, ported from mac's `AssistSession` (`recordWhatTheEngineSaid`/`engineLinesWorthRecording`/`readsLikeATrouble`/`watchWhatTheEngineSays`). New `Plantoir.Core.Assist.AssistEngineLog` holds the pure filter as static functions — `ReadsLikeATrouble` (an `E`-severity second field, or the words error/exception/failed/failure, case-insensitive; deliberately NOT a bare warning, since a healthy start prints several that mean nothing here), `LinesWorthRecording` (caps at 12 lines per conversation, keeping the LAST few once the cap is hit since a giving-up engine's reason is at the bottom of what it wrote), `Shortened` (200 chars + ellipsis) — checked in `AssistEngineLogTests.cs` against the same real llama.cpp (b10435) fixture lines the mac test uses, so both platforms are checked against the same evidence. `LocalModel` gained `LinesSinceLastLook(ref long mark)` on top of its existing 60-line ring buffer (Windows already avoided the mac's original pipe-wedge bug by draining via `Process` output/error EVENTS rather than a blocking pipe read, so no file-backed log was needed here — architecture difference, not a gap); pinned by `LocalModelEngineLogTests.cs`, including the case where the engine writes more between two looks than the buffer holds. `AssistWindow.xaml.cs` wires it at the same three points mac's `AssistSession` does: the two engine-start-failure paths (`keepingEverything: true` — every line is the diagnosis when the engine never got ready), a 15-second background poll starting once the assistant is ready (`keepingEverything: false`), and one last look in `Shutdown()` before the model stops. New `ActivityTrail.Event.AssistantEngineSaid` / `"assistant engine said"`, already pinned generically against `contracts/shared-rules.json` → `activityTrail.mustRecord` by the existing `ContractTests.cs` set-equality check — no new contract test needed. An adversarial review caught a genuine data race the mac original does not have: mac's version runs on one actor, so its non-atomic properties are safe by construction, but Windows deliberately moved the watch loop to a background thread while `Shutdown()`'s own last look runs on the UI thread without waiting for an in-flight background iteration — fixed with a lock (`_engineLogGate`) around the shared mark/count state. Full suite: 655/655 passed. Windows app rebuilt for x64. | **Shared reasoning, nothing further to port** — this row IS the Windows port; the mac's own version is unchanged. The one thing worth carrying back the OTHER way if it ever comes up: Windows' event-driven pipe drain (`Process.OutputDataReceived`/`ErrorDataReceived`, no blocking read) sidesteps the exact wedge class that made the mac move to a file-backed log in the first place — worth knowing as prior art if a future engine integration on either side needs to redirect a subprocess's output. |

Nesting one broke rendering outright: every crumb showed the literal text "Plantoir.Views.PathBarCrumb" (the bound object's default `ToString()`), reported directly after the icon/click work was believed done and built. Icon vertical alignment nudged with `Margin="0,2,0,0"` on the `Image` to sit on the text's visual baseline rather than its bounding-box center, matching how it reads on macOS. `FolderCrumb.ForPath`/`AncestorPaths` (root-first ancestor list) untouched. Full suite: 655/655. Windows app rebuilt for x64 (`PT - Dev`). | **Nothing to port — the mac already has this path bar**; this row is Windows catching up to it. Worth knowing for awareness on either side: a `DataTemplate` that wraps the exact container type an items-control (`ListView`, `BreadcrumbBar`, etc.) already generates per item is a trap that passes a normal code read and even a successful *build* — it only fails at runtime, and fails by silently falling back to `ToString()` rather than throwing, which reads as "the binding is wrong" rather than "the template structure is wrong." Worth checking for the same shape anywhere else in `windows-app/` an `ItemTemplate` wraps a control-specific item-container type by name. See `MAC-HANDOFF.md` for the ledger entry. |

| 336 | 2026-08-23 | Russell put our simulated combo box side by side with a real `NSComboBox` in a test app and listed five ways the geometry was off: text placement inside the field, the chevron button's size, its padding from the field's edge, the field's corner radius, and the fact that ours tinted the button when the real one does not. | ✅ Every number is now MEASURED off a real `NSComboBox` rather than matched by eye. The radius only *read* wrong because the same 6pt on a 30pt-tall box looks squarer — the height was the whole bug. Button **24×19pt**, 4pt in from the trailing edge (was 22×24 at 3pt). Text inset **6pt**, which is the cell's `titleRect` x=4 PLUS ~2pt of line-fragment padding the text system adds inside it — measuring glyphs settles what the API alone does not: "B" of "Banana" lands 7.0pt from a real combo box's outer edge, "C" of "Chemistry" 6.5pt in a rounded `NSTextField`, and at a bare 4 our "S" landed at 4.5. Chevron glyph sized by rendering `chevron.down` across sizes 8–13 at four weights and measuring tight bounds: 8.5pt semibold is the one that hits AppKit's 7.5×4.5pt (we had 11pt bold). Button fill is untinted and needs TWO semantic colours to match both appearances, because AppKit draws it with control artwork rather than a named fill: `tertiarySystemFill` composites to exactly (41,41,41) in dark and `secondarySystemFill` to exactly (235,235,235) in light — either one alone is visibly off in the other appearance (dark's secondary lands at 48, light's tertiary at 243). Glyph is plain label colour; the old accent-luminance calculation existed only to keep a white-or-black glyph legible on a tinted pill and had nothing left to compensate for. Verified by driving the real app and re-measuring our own field from the screenshot: height 24pt, button 24×19pt, trailing inset 4pt, fill (41,41,41), glyph 7.5×4.0pt, first typed glyph 6.5pt from the field edge — every one matching native. Full mac unit suite green (841 tests, 0 failures). **Known remaining difference, deliberately not chased:** the field interior renders (30,30,30) from `textBackgroundColor` where a native combo box renders (23,23,23). No semantic colour is (23,23,23) in dark — AppKit shades its own bezel — and hard-coding it would break light mode and the next macOS. | **The method is what travels, not the constants** — WinUI's `AutoSuggestBox`/`ComboBox` metrics are its own, and copying 24/19/6/4 into C# would be worse than useless. What to copy is: render the platform's REAL control offscreen, read it back pixel by pixel, and fit shapes to it, rather than tuning against screenshots of your own app. Three traps this caught that a Windows session will meet in its own dialect: (1) a corner radius that is already correct can *read* wrong when a different dimension is off — check the box before changing the radius; (2) the API's text rect is not where glyphs land (here 4 vs ~5.7pt), so measure glyphs when matching text position; (3) the accent-coloured affordance is the tell that a control is imitated rather than native — platform chrome for a dropdown arrow is nearly colourless in both themes. Also worth knowing: a semantic fill that matches in one theme can miss in the other, because the platform may not be using a named colour at all — check both rather than assuming one token does the job. |

| 337 | 2026-08-23 | Russell, on row 336's field: the text in Course code sits slightly lower within its box than Course name's does — Course name reads vertically centred, Course code does not. | ✅ Real, and measurable without any font metrics because both fields' contents happened to start with a capital "I": in one screenshot at 2x, Course name put 17 device px above the cap and 16 below the baseline, while Course code put 18 above and 11 below — 3.5px, or 1.75pt, too low. The cause is that a `.plain` `TextField`'s glyphs ride low inside its OWN intrinsic box, so `.frame(height:)` centring the box still leaves the text low in the field. Fixed with 3pt of BOTTOM padding inside the fixed-height frame — padding P moves centred content up by P/2 — applied as padding rather than `.offset` so it stays part of layout and the caret and hit-testing follow the text instead of being left behind by a visual-only shift. Re-measured after the change: 15 above / 14 below, against Course name's 17/16, which is the same half-pixel-low convention. **Flagged, not changed:** the two boxes are still different heights — ours is 24pt (the native `NSComboBox`/`NSTextField` figure from row 336) and Course name is 26pt, because SwiftUI's `.roundedBorder` renders 2pt taller than the AppKit control it stands in for. Matching the native control and matching our own sibling field are no longer the same target, and which one wins is Russell's call. | Three things travel. First, the **method for judging vertical centring**: pick a glyph both fields share (a capital with no descender), measure space above the cap and below the baseline in device pixels, and compare the two numbers — it needs no font metrics and no guessing about line height, and it turns "looks a bit low" into 3.5px. Second, the **trap**: a text control's glyphs are frequently not centred within its own layout box, so centring the box is not centring the text — check the glyphs, not the frame. Applies to any hand-built field on any platform. The 3pt itself does NOT travel; re-measure in WinUI. Third, a **UI-test-harness fact worth knowing before you copy it**: on macOS the XCUITest runner is sandboxed and cannot spawn a child process, so `Process`-based cleanup in `setUp`/`tearDown` compiles, runs, and does nothing — verified here by making the helper write a marker file that never appeared, with `try?` swallowing the failure silently. If your harness needs to reap something a test leaked, check that it CAN before trusting it. Also note SwiftUI's own approximation of an AppKit control was 2pt off the real thing here, which is a reminder that a framework's "native-style" control is not automatically the native control's metrics. |

| 338 | 2026-08-23 | Russell, three asks on the wizard's course-code field: (1) if native fields are 24pt then Course name and Timetable section numbers should be 24 too, not 26; (2) pressing the chevron a second time should HIDE the popup, the way a real `NSComboBox`'s arrow does; (3) up/down arrows should walk the flyout and Return should take a selection, again like a real combo box. | ✅ All three. **(1)** `.frame(height: 24)` does NOT do it — worth knowing before anyone tries it again: SwiftUI draws the `.roundedBorder` bezel at its own intrinsic 26pt regardless of the frame it is given, so the field measured 26 anyway and merely overflowed its box. The only route to 24 is to stop asking SwiftUI for a bezel and draw one, which the course-code field already had to do for its chevron. Down with the popup closed reopens it and takes the first row, so Escape does not strand a keyboard user. `ScrollViewReader` keeps the highlight on screen. Check what your `TextBox` actually measures against the WinUI control it imitates before assuming they agree. **(2) and (3) are plain behaviour to mirror**: a dropdown affordance toggles, and up/down/Return must work in the flyout. Two implementation notes worth having: keep ONE piece of state for "is the flyout showing" and have the toggle, the drawing and the animation all read it; and key handlers should decline rather than swallow, or you break the arrow keys and the default button for anyone who is just typing. | 352 | 2026-08-23 | Nothing in the repository had ever removed a Docker image. `docker system df` on this Mac: 139 images, 50.09 GB, ~115 `teaching-quartz:src-*` tags — a grep for `docker rmi`, `image prune` and `dangling` across every `.sh`, `.ps1`, `.py`, `.swift` and `.cs` returned zero hits. The tag is a hash of the build recipe, so every recipe change mints a new tag and orphans the previous one permanently. | ✅ Fixed — `prune_superseded_images()` in all three launchers (`setup.sh`, `preview.sh`, `deploy.sh`), called only on the success branch of an actual build, so a run that reuses an existing image touches nothing. It removes `teaching-quartz:src-*` tags other than the one just built, skipping any tag a container still references (`docker ps -aq --filter ancestor=`), and every step is non-fatal so a failed `rmi` can never turn a teacher's preview into an error. **Three guards were missing from the first version and found by an adversarial review, all of which read as over-caution until the case is named:** (a) do nothing at all unless the tag just built is itself a `teaching-quartz:src-*` tag — `--image` can point it anywhere, and "remove everything except the tag I was given" would then mean removing every other working folder's current image; (b) never touch an image younger than about a day, because the container check is a point-in-time read and a folder that is mid-recreate references nothing for a second or two — long enough for a build finishing elsewhere to delete the image it is about to run, which surfaces as a registry-pull failure for an image that exists on no registry; (c) take the age from `docker images {{.CreatedSince}}` and never compute it, because `docker image inspect {{.Created}}` returns LOCAL time with an offset rather than the UTC `Z` it resembles — comparing it against a UTC cutoff is silently wrong by the machine's offset, and `--filter since=` takes an image NAME, not a duration. **Keep exactly one tag, not two:** the "keep the previous one so a downgrade is cheap" argument does not survive contact with the mechanism — an older Plantoir carries its own bundled recipe and rebuilds its own tag either way, so N=2 doubles the retained delta for nothing. **The build cache is deliberately untouched** (14.25 GB here, all reclaimable): `docker builder prune` is global with no per-project filter, so it would throw away other projects' cache too — it stays a by-hand developer job, recorded in `TODO.md`. Gated by `verify.sh` §3c, which `cmp`s the three hand-copied bodies against each other (the existing cross-check proves only that each NAME is defined, so a fix applied to one file would otherwise leave a green suite over a real difference), extracts the real function from `preview.sh` rather than retyping it, and drives it three times against throwaway fixtures: too-new → spared; foreign keep-tag → nothing touched; superseded and old → removed, while the current tag, an in-use tag and another project's image all survive. The test stubs the daemon's image LISTING to its own fixtures, because sourcing the real function and calling it for effect would otherwise delete the maintainer's genuine images as a side effect of running `verify.sh` — a multi-minute, network-dependent rebuild in every working folder, from a command whose banner promises only to check things. | **Nothing to mirror, and the reason matters more than the fact.** Windows dropped Docker entirely on 2026-08-19 for the native runtime, so there is no image, no tag and no container to leak — see "Windows no longer runs any of this in a container". `TODO-TODAY.md` asserted "their launchers have the same gap"; that was wrong, and is corrected here so nobody ports a cleanup for images that do not exist. The one thing worth checking on that side is the analogous question rather than the same code: whether a superseded `Vendor/runtime/` (or a stale model download) is ever left behind by an update. Not investigated from here — the mac cannot see it. |

Confirmed empirically by running real `preview.ps1 --build-only` and `deploy.ps1 --to-folder` transcripts on this machine: neither string appears. Practical effect: the first two-to-three stages of most progress bars (“Getting this PC ready…”, “Building your website builder…”, “Starting up…”) could never be reached by marker match -- the bar sat at 0% until a later, still-real marker jumped it forward several steps at once, exactly the silent failure `WINDOWS-HANDOFF.md` warned about. New `TaskMilestoneLauncherMarkerTests` reads the actual `.ps1` files rather than a hand-typed transcript, so a future launcher rewrite that drops one of these lines fails the suite instead of silently stalling a teacher's bar again; a second test guards against ever matching the four mac-only strings again. Caught by adversarial review: `MarketingShotCapturer.cs`'s `PreviewTranscript`/`DeployTranscript` mock transcripts, whose doc comments explicitly claim to be coupled to `TaskMilestones` (“change one there and this stops advancing”), still contained the four dead strings -- fixed to match, verified by hand-tracing `ScriptRunner.AdvanceMilestones` to confirm each still lands the screenshot on its intended stage. The lesson that DOES travel: a marker list is a claim about what a SPECIFIC script prints, and it silently rots the moment that script is rewritten out from under it with no test reading the script's own text -- the fix here (a test that greps the real launcher file, not a synthetic transcript) is the general pattern, worth having on the mac side too if `.sh` output is ever restructured. |

New `ScriptRunner.IsBetweenPhases`, set true synchronously right after the build launches (well before it can finish) and cleared only by the next `Run()`'s own reset or an early exit — `TaskProgressView.Render()` and its tick timer now check `IsRunning \|\| IsBetweenPhases` everywhere they checked `IsRunning` alone, mirroring the mac's identical flag. **One thing the adversarial pass caught worth naming:** an explicit `RefreshChrome()` call originally sat between clearing `_isPreparingDeploy` and awaiting `RunAsync` — almost certainly never visible (everything up to `RunAsync`'s own `Notify` runs synchronously, before any frame paints) but it re-derived the exact stale-`Legs` race the fix exists to close, one future `await` inserted in that gap away from becoming real. Removed — `RunAsync`'s own synchronous prefix already triggers `RefreshChrome` through the existing `PropertyChanged` subscription. Full suite: 657/658 before and after (the one failure is the pre-existing, unrelated item-9 course-code-dashes case). Students can reach it now." unconditionally after a `deploy_section` tool call, success or not, because `MainWindow.DeployForAsync`'s completion source resolved the instant the click was dispatched to the UI thread rather than when the deploy actually finished. | ✅ Fixed on Windows. `SectionDetailView.Deploy_Click`'s body is now `DeployAsync()`, an `async Task<string?>` that RETURNS the true outcome sentence on every exit — success/partial/all-failed computed by the existing `MultiDestinationDeployRunner.Result(...)`, and `AssistWording.DeployDidNotFinish` on every early return (refused, already deploying, the assistant is building elsewhere) and the catch block. `Deploy_Click` itself is now a thin fire-and-forget wrapper; a new `StartDeployForAutomationAsync()` returns `DeployAsync()`'s own `Task` directly. `MainWindow.DeployForAsync` awaits that (instead of a TCS resolved at dispatch time) and `AssistWindow`'s `StartDeployInAppAsync` threads the string through to `AssistAgent.RunTool`, which now answers with the real sentence, falling back to `DeployDidNotFinish` — never to success — if no outcome is available. Rewritten to return the outcome directly from `DeployAsync()` per call instead — no shared mutable state between concurrent invocations, so the race is closed structurally rather than guarded against. New `AssistScenarioTests` (`DeploySection_SucceedsWhenAsyncSeamReportsSuccess`/`..._ReportsFailureWhenAsyncSeamReportsFailure`/`..._FallsBackToDidNotFinishWhenAsyncSeamReturnsNull`) exercise the real `StartDeployInAppAsync` production seam directly, which the existing scenario fixture (`AssistScenarioTests.cs:80`) never wired at all. | 384 | 2026-09-01 | The open item left behind by the special-folders branch: **a section with no `index.md` cannot be published at all, and nothing said so.** Fix the circle, and strengthen the health check's wording, which understated it. | ✅ Two defects, and the second was not the one on the list. **(1) The circle.** `_sync_public_to_host` (`scripts/build_site.py`) mirrors the built site to the host only when `public/index.html` exists — right, since Quartz emits none without an `index.md` and half a build must never be published — but the build printed "✅ Static build complete" either way, and `deploy` then said "Built site not found — build first" to a teacher who had just built. The sync now RETURNS whether it mirrored a site; a build that mirrored nothing says "Nothing to publish … it has no front page, so no website was produced" and **exits non-zero**, so a publish stops at the build with the reason in front of it instead of at the step that cannot know why. The app already shows the folder-problem dialog on a failed build (`SectionDetailView`), so the teacher lands on **Put them back** → **Preview Again** rather than on a dead end. **(2) The one nobody had named, and the worse of the two: a publish could ship LAST week's pages and report success.** The guard left the previous build's `public/` sitting on the host, and `deploy.py` uploads whatever it finds there — so delete a front page, build, publish, and the teacher is told it worked while students get the older site. `_clear_stale_host_site` now removes that mirror whenever the merged tree has no `index.md`, in BOTH preview and build modes, turning a silent wrong answer into an honest refusal. Nothing of the teacher's is lost: `.merged_output` is derived, and every successful build rewrites it with `rsync --delete`. **(3) The wording.** `sectionIndexMissing`'s detail said the site "will open on whatever page happens to come first" — true of a preview, and it understated publishing by a mile. It now names both outcomes. **What a teacher is told about the failure** is a new pair of `failureExplanations` cases, the second of which pins ORDER: a publish runs the build and the deploy on one transcript, so both lines appear, and the specific reason has to beat "hasn't been built yet". No new trail event — `folderProblemFound` already records this finding on every build where it fires, and what changed is a message and an exit code, not what happens. Tests: `scripts/test_publishable_site.py` (4, wired into `verify.sh`) plus the two contract cases; 40 mac tests green across `AppRulesContractTests`, `FailureExplainerTests` and `SiteHealthFindingTests`. | **Shared Python — you inherit (1), (2) and (3) with no work.** `build_site.py`, `deploy.py` and the health wording are the same files. What you DO owe is the C# half of the explanation: `FailureExplainer.cs` gains `MissingFrontPageExplanation`, and it must be asked **before** `MissingBuildExplanation` — that ordering is the second contract case and it will fail your suite if you chain it the other way round. Both are written here already, so this is a review rather than a port. `deploy.ps1` gained the same two-line hint as `deploy.sh`. One thing to check on your side rather than copy: `_clear_stale_host_site` uses `shutil.rmtree` on `PLANTOIR_BUILD_ROOT`'s copy of `public/`, which on Windows is outside the working folder and outside OneDrive — if a virus scanner or an open handle makes the removal fail, the build says so and carries on rather than dying, but the stale-publish risk returns for that run. Worth one real test on a machine with OneDrive running. |
| 385 | 2026-09-01 | `TODO.md`, deferred 2026-08-23 while planning the special-folders work: **let a teacher rename a special folder from inside Plantoir.** Russell's call on the scope, 2026-09-01: the full thing, disk included. | ✅ Folder rows in Course Settings gain a **pencil**, which opens a sheet that renames the folder **on disk** — in every section that has one — rewrites the qualified links that name it, and carries across every `course_config.json` key that mentioned it (`shared_folders`/`per_section_folders`, `graded_folders`, `curriculum_folder`, `excluded_items`). **The two foot-guns behind the item are closed in the same breath:** adding a name now CREATES the folder (it used to write an entry pointing at nothing), and removing one says “‘X’ and everything in it stays on your Mac — this only takes it off your website”, which teachers previously had no way to know. **Renaming is offered on rows whose REMOVAL is blocked**, deliberately: “All Classes” can never be removed and can perfectly well be called something else, and conflating the two would make the one folder every course has the only one a teacher cannot rename. **Three decisions worth the reasoning.** (1) It commits to disk immediately rather than at Save, and the sheet says so — Settings otherwise holds edits in memory with Cancel reverting them, and a folder that has really moved cannot be un-moved by a Cancel. `CourseConfiguration.recordOnDisk` writes the rename to a FRESH read of the file, so the teacher's other unsaved edits stay unsaved. (2) **The class folder must keep the word “class” in its name**, and is refused with a sentence naming the reason. `ClassFolder.name(inPerSectionFolders:)` finds it by that substring, so renaming it to “Lessons” would hand the curriculum map a different folder with nothing said — the exact silent wrong answer this family exists to end. The proper fix is a `class_folder` key in the config; that is a file-format change both apps write, and Piece 2 of the special-folders branch is the standing evidence such a change lands on its own or not at all. (3) **Nothing moves until every destination is checked**, so a per-section rename cannot get half way through four sections and stop. **The `TODO` entry's central worry turned out to be wrong, and that is why this was buildable without an undo:** it feared a rename would strand every link pointing into the folder. Obsidian resolves `[[Quiz 1]]` by searching the vault, so a bare page link survives the folder moving untouched; only QUALIFIED links (`[[Tasks/Quiz 1]]`, a full vault path, or a Markdown-style `](Tasks/Quiz%201.md)`) break, and `FolderPathRewriter` rewrites exactly those. Tests: 38 new (14 rewriter, 24 renamer), plus contract assertions for eleven new sentences and one that fails if a key is added to the contract's `carriesAcross` list and not to the code; 57 green with `SharedRulesContractTests`. | **Not yet built on Windows — this is a port, and the shared contract carries most of it.** Every sentence is in `shared-rules.json` → `specialNames.renameFolder` / `addCreatesTheFolder` / `removeLeavesTheFolderOnDisk`, and `carriesAcross` lists the keys a rename must rewrite, so your suite can assert the same things ours does. The two trail events (`folder renamed`, `folder created`) are in `activityTrail.mustRecord` and will fail your trail test until you add them. What does NOT port: the sheet is SwiftUI, and `CourseConfiguration.recordOnDisk` is a mac type — but the RULE it implements is the one to copy, because your Settings holds edits in memory the same way ours does, and a rename that waited for Save would let Cancel appear to undo a folder that has really moved. One platform trap that is yours alone: a per-section rename is several `Directory.Move` calls, and Windows will refuse one whose folder has a handle open — OneDrive and Obsidian both hold them. Check every destination first the way the Swift does, and report which section failed rather than leaving the rename half done. |
| 386 | 2026-09-01 | `TODO.md`, deferred 2026-08-23: **let a teacher rename the `Unit` keyword.** Russell's call on the scope, 2026-09-01: new courses plus configurable parsing — not renaming a course already in use. | ✅ New `unit_word` in `course_config.json` (**ABSENT means “Unit”**, so every existing course is untouched). The New Course wizard asks “What do you call a unit?” of **every** course, ready-made ones included, and the payload is poured in that word: `install_payload_file` rewrites the text and `renamed_for_unit_word` renames the file, so a Module course's ~3,000 pages are *written* as “Module 1, Day 1” rather than renamed afterwards. **The parsing follows the config on both sides**, which is the half that makes it real rather than cosmetic: `scripts/class_pages.py` holds the rule (`build_site.py` holds this build's answer), and `ClassPageTerm` + `UnitDay(term:)` hold the Swift's. **Why the parsing mattered more than the naming:** `_is_class_page` deciding “no” does not fail — `_pages_the_course_teaches` returns nothing and the curriculum map falls back to counting EVERY published page, so a Module course would have got a map that looked healthy and was wrong. That is the same silent-success failure the whole special-names family exists to end, and it is why the “new courses only, parsing left hardcoded” option was rejected outright. Four decisions: (1) **“Day” stays fixed** — a teacher who says Thread almost certainly still says Day 3, and a second word would double the migration for something nobody asked for; (2) **the word is escaped before it becomes a regex**, because it comes from a teacher's own configuration and one containing “(” would otherwise fail to compile mid-build; (3) **a number or comma in the word is refused**, in the wizard and in the CLI, because the pages would be built and then recognised by nothing; (4) **the rewrite matches “Unit” only when a number follows**, which is what separates a unit reference from the English word — and it is run ONLY over content Plantoir ships, never over a teacher's own writing, where “Unit 3 of the textbook” would be a false positive nobody could undo. It also catches the ~574 payload files that say “by the end of Unit 3” in prose, which would otherwise leave a Module course talking about Units. **The assistant speaks the course's word too** (“Module 4 was published.”). Tests: 14 Python (`scripts/test_class_pages.py`, in `verify.sh`) and 11 Swift, plus three new contract cases in `class-planning.json` → `pageNaming` that BOTH suites run. | **Two of the three halves are shared Python and arrive free** — `class_pages.py` and the payload rewrite in `setup_course.py` are the same files you run. What you owe is the C# mirror of `ClassPageTerm`/`UnitDay(term:)`, a `unit_word` field in your wizard, and the wizard writing the key. **Your suite will go red first, and that is the mechanism working:** three cases were added to `class-planning.json` → `pageNaming` carrying a `term` field, and a case WITHOUT that field means the default word — so read `term` with a default of “Unit” rather than treating its absence as a new shape. The case that matters is the one where a Module course must NOT read “Unit 2, Day 3” as a class page; getting that backwards puts two numbering schemes in one section and nothing fails. One thing to copy rather than reinvent: the word travels ON the parsed value and on the page summary, not looked up per call — a page read out of a Module course is then written back as a Module page without every planner needing the course as well. |

Throwaway folders deleted. **Three defects the design created, all found before shipping:** (1) `--stop` finds a preview's processes by WORKING DIRECTORY and `/proc/<pid>/cwd` is the RESOLVED path, so the sweep would have found nothing and reported success — it resolves now, checked against a real serving preview; (2) archiving, restoring or replacing a course removes the link but not the build outside, and a restored course's pages can be OLDER than that build, so it would read as up to date and publish last month's site — **a build with no link pointing at it is now cleared, never adopted**, with archiving a course or section discarding its build outright and a RENAME carrying it across, since a rename never used to cost a website; (3) renaming lost the site anyway on the first attempt, because the re-point went through `ensureLink`, whose answer to a link pointing elsewhere is to start from nothing. **And one that was PRE-EXISTING and is fixed here anyway:** restoring ONE section — from a backup, or the assistant undoing what it just did — left that section's built website standing, and a restored section carries the timestamps it had when it was backed up, which can be older than the site built from them; so the freshness check read "already up to date" and publishing would have shipped the pages the teacher had just undone. Equally true when `.merged_output` sat inside the folder (a section restore replaces `section<N>` and never touched the built tree beside it), so this is not damage the move did — but it is the same family of silent wrong answer, and the test was checked by putting the fault back. **On Russell's question about existing users:** every step in the launcher block may now fail WITHOUT stopping the run — `setup.sh` and `deploy.sh` run under `set -euo pipefail`, so an unguarded `ln`/`mv`/`mkdir` would have turned "the built website could not be moved" into "publishing is broken" on a read-only folder or a full disk — and the fallback is the OLD behaviour; if the move works and the link then fails, the move is put BACK. `build_site.py`'s dangling-link repair was turned round for the same reason: creating the target answers a folder synced from a second Mac and silently ruins a container made before the mount, so it replaces the link with a real folder instead. So the rule is a disjunction of three evidences plus a walk down the process tree, and it stops strictly MORE than any of the three did alone — pinned as a deliberate behaviour change (a differential test runs the old sweep beside the new rule on a realistic snapshot and asserts exactly what is gained: the driver and its children, nothing else). Writing it down found **two live bugs, both in `preview.ps1`, both the same shape**: `...\section1` is a prefix of `...\section10`, and `--section=1` is a prefix of `--section=10`, so stopping section 1 also stopped section 10 by two routes at once — needs ten sections in one course, which nothing refuses. The rule now lives once in `scripts/stop_preview.py`, pinned by `contracts/shared-rules.json` → `stopPreview` (23 cases, each a whole process SNAPSHOT rather than one process, because `Win32_Process` exposes no working directory and the descendant walk is not a property of any single process). `preview.sh` PIPES the recipe's copy in over stdin rather than running one baked into the image: stop mode must never build anything, so it runs against whatever container is already there — after an upgrade, one built from the previous image — and naming a baked path would fail with a message nobody sees, since both callers discard the launcher's output and neither checks its exit code. **`verify.sh` never drove `--stop` at all before this**, so the mac's stop path had no automated gate; section 6d now drives it against real processes in the real container, including one known only by its working directory, one belonging to another section that must survive, and a container with the module deleted from it. The test also stopped re-implementing the rule it tests — the old `test_stop_preview.py` carried its own `would_stop()` copy, and a test named "section 1 does not match section 10", while the platform that had that bug went on having it. Each fault was put back to confirm the case that names it fails, and only it. **A trail event WAS added, after the first answer was wrong** (rule 5). The first pass reasoned that reclaiming processes is machinery a teacher would not recognise, and that "task started"/"task finished" already bracket the run — the adversarial review pointed out that the BEHAVIOUR changed: a stop now ends a mid-flight build and its driver, and Cancel on a publish now ends the publish's own build. Rule 5 is explicit that a CHANGED behaviour changes its line too. Worse, the launcher prints the one number that settles it — "Stopped N process(es)" — and `PreviewStopper` sent stdout to the null device, so nothing anywhere could distinguish "there was nothing left to stop" from "a build was still running and was ended", which are the two competing explanations when a teacher reports that their publish stopped halfway. `section processes reclaimed` now carries the course, the section and the count, in a teacher's words rather than the script's; a sweep that never RAN (no container) deliberately leaves no line, because a line claiming zero would not be true. Deliberately left to you: `--stop` must never start anything, and whether Python is reliably resolvable on that path is measurable only on your machine. **Your descendant walk is now part of the shared rule** — this is the mac catching up to you, not the reverse. One harness lesson if you write the equivalent gate: a process that scans other processes for a marker string will find ITSELF, because the marker is on its own command line. It bit twice here — first inflating a count so every check in the section failed while the code under test was right, then, in the cleanup, making the script SIGKILL itself part-way through, print nothing, and silently leave the processes it existed to collect. Exclude your own process id, and treat "scanning for a string I am myself carrying" as the shape to watch for.
